The **twenty-third reading is eight and seven-eighths**, a quarter of an inch wider than the twenty-second in one step, and a crack does not step. **Wenna Cray arrives with a slate and asks, in front of nineteen men, whether he is going to enter it as a fact or as a fault, because a fault can be given to somebody and a fact can only be carried** — and the answer is a fact, and Adrian says the true thing about the twenty-fourth day and a description that is true of two places, and says that about nine people in that gallery had already decided the number was a boy's, and asks the first of them to say so out loud, and nobody does. Ovin Sarr asks for the rule twice, is refused the first time, is given it the second, and asks for the first request to be left in the book. **Jonah Pike is on the wall with his right hand on a hauling line and has no standing in that building at all**, and says one thing on his way past — *the man who cannot be replaced is not a holder, he is a bottleneck with a name on it, and you have been one for forty days and the only reason nobody has broken it yet is that nobody has wanted to* — and then holds the line for two hours and says nothing else. **Ovin Sarr writes *works* in the last column of the tide table at about the eighth hour with a stick of chalk, small, the *k* pressed too hard**, and asks *is that the right word*, and is told it is the only one anybody has ever written in that column, and says *it is not the only word*. Adrian was not asked to and did not offer. **The bottom field is given to Ovin as a day's work at about the ninth hour and he refuses it in nine seconds in front of about forty people**: *a field is not a day's work; a field is a thing a man is asked for again next season, and a day's work is a thing a man hands back.* And the debt is entered for the **sixth** time in a month and the notary reads her own sentence about a notation again and then says the half she has never said: **a column re-entered six times in a month is a column this city can afford, and the reason is that about four hundred people have never met the man the column is in.**

### Chapter 0044 — A More Attractive Form
Day 44, the Wednesday, the nineteenth day of the new month, into the night. In the loft, Adrian finds out that the ninety hands on Monday were a **quarterly payment** and not a Crown, because a docker of about fifty-eight said *because I am tired*, and Priya Shah has it entered so that in four months nobody can read that this city chose a single-author Crown containment and have it be even nearly true. He tells the nine people in that room the thing he has been wrong about since Monday: **a Crown's clerk is a man who can be sent, and that is the whole of what a room wanted.** At about the sixth hour he is taken up two hundred steps to a house with a burnt lintel, because **Sabra Vint has let a man in a good coat come up them for the first time in nine years.** For about two hours, with no document, Marrow offers the third option **with the Crown taken out of it** so that it will cost this city nothing: the containment held by Adrian, no workway, no mark, no stipend, no advocate, no seal, no office, no Crown, entered in this city's own book, with his own undertaking that the Crown has no part in it, and **his answer not answerable by any other person in Saltwake.** **He does not ask for obedience and he does not ask for a mark and he asks for a seat** — and a seat is a place to be angry at and has to be legible to a person who does not like you, and that is the one argument in this city Adrian could not get round and did not say so for two hours. Adrian refuses in a yard at about the fourth hour of the evening, in about two minutes, with two witnesses, and says out loud the sentence he has been carrying since the thirty-eighth: **a man in a good coat asked me for the true reason and I wrote mine, and a man on a table told me the difference between an argument and a record, and it took him four minutes and it took me nineteen days, and you have been in this city for eight weeks and you did not have to wait for a man to die to work it out.** Marrow's face does not do anything, which Roke enters as the first time in nineteen years. He answers in four seconds: **"I did not have a man to die. I have nineteen years of other people's records, and a great many of them are about men who did not die."** And Adrian writes that a man who has kept the record for nineteen years and wants to be the only hand that answers is not asking for a seat, and that he does not have a name for it and is not going to invent one in a room. **Sabra Vint is asked what she thinks because she is the only person in that yard who can be asked for nothing, and says the true thing about her own paper: she writes the shapes and has never once written what she thinks the shapes mean, in nine years, and somebody has to be the one who writes that down, and she is sixty-one and would like to stop.** Peth Lomas thanks him in public for the other one and says he is sorry about it. And at about the ninth hour, two hundred steps above him, **a woman of sixty-one says the number nineteen to a man on a road for the first time in nine years, and he says goodnight, and she writes in the margin: said it. Nobody answered. Go home.**

### Chapter 0045 — What a Route Is For
Day 45, the Thursday, the twentieth day of the new month, into the Friday. Tamsin Quill gives the notary her reason for keeping the word *standing* out of the book for two days: she said it in a room of two hundred and forty people before she had read it in a book, and a word said out loud in that language is in this city's mouth from that moment, and it costs about nine months, and she has watched about four people find out what that costs and is not going to be the fifth. **She asked for the non-entry herself and entered that she asked and not that she was asked.** On the walk-round, at the cap of the old landing, Adrian looks at the socket for four minutes and can read nothing in it — *there is no join-line in a closed seam* — and Renna Ord enters that she had been waiting nine days for somebody to try to use him for it. **Tamsin proposes that the ordinary work of the Lock of Salt is the working that closes the join, and that the hand in it is the crew and not a man, which has no instance in this world.** Vask gives two sentences: **a floor that has come open once is not a pattern, it is a hole**, and **the forty words under the near gate are the toll and they are the pattern, and a city cannot spend a thing and be its own foundation at the same time** — and the cost of them is not a season but the year of certainty gone out of a hundred and forty acres, and the four villages whose gates cannot be promised are four miles away and are not in the group. Renna Ord names the price: forty men twice a day indefinitely, **four of the forty hurt before March, one at a time, on an ordinary morning, and no instrument can stop it**, four people in the wrong weather with a gauge before every tide, **and the whole price in one sentence: the day the bottom field is not paid in March, this stops, and the join stops being worked, and the join will be where it is** — and **you do not get a door that is not somebody's field.** And then the condition that makes it plural: **nobody can open it; forty men turn up or it does not happen; nine named persons can stop it and one of the nine named herself; a door with no handle in it.** And Adrian says **I cannot promise you that it will close** before anybody asks him, because Renna Ord asked him to. On the Friday, in a yard of about two hundred and forty people, **the two documents are put side by side and Adrian reads the notary's entry of the fortieth aloud first and says she was right**, and names the three instruments of that shape in five weeks and that only two of them are the Crown's. **About ninety people refuse the third instrument in public in nine minutes and the refusal cannot be entered as a refusal of the city**, because ninety of the ninety are owed money and a refusal a person cannot afford is not a refusal; it goes in as ninety statements of error, and Adrian reads out the fifth clause of the Registry's notice — *a refusal which costs other people money is a decision about other people* — and says he did not write it. **Jonah Pike argues for the Crown's form in nine sentences and is right in eight of them**, and the ninth is not about Aurel, and Adrian says the tenth: a Crown advocate is a man who is expensive and he is not on this side of the water; and says to Jonah that he is not wrong to want a structure and this city does not have to want it. **And then a flood-creel woman of about forty asks, in four words, whether the crew knows, and nobody in that yard had asked, and Nell Bray, Renna Ord and Hesk each say the same eleven words: we had not been asked and we were not going to say.**

### Chapter 0046 — Ask the Crew
Day 46, the Friday, the twenty-first day of the new month, into the small hours of the Saturday. **Hesk, the foreman of the day crew, says the four words under the near gate for the first time in his life at the sixth hour of the morning, before anybody has asked him for anything**, and Renna Ord notices and says nothing and enters why: a woman's noticing a man's first time saying a thing is the oldest trick there is. The ask is made at the eleventh hour of the morning in the Salt Weavers' chapel in front of about two hundred and forty people, **and the crew of forty is not in the room**, and about four of the people putting the ask together knew it. **At the sixth hour of the evening, under the near gate, with forty-one in the room, the crew says no in about nine minutes**, and the reason is Renna Ord's rule in eleven words: **a thing nobody has asked for cannot be owed, and a thing that cannot be owed cannot be paid, and a thing that cannot be paid is a tax.** Hesk comes down off the sill and says that nobody in this city has ever asked him anything, and asks to be asked. Renna Ord then says what she has been waiting five weeks to say: **the owing is not the fairness, the owing is the mechanism**; a shift is a thing the city owes, on a roll with the forty names and **no name at the top of it**; a man who has not been paid does not come and there is no entry and no fault and nobody goes to anybody's door; **and the city's credit in this fortnight is a field four miles away.** **At the eighth hour they say yes — thirty-seven for, three against, one abstention — and the three against are entered by name: Hesk, who says he said the words this morning and voted no fourteen hours later anyway, Ovin Sarr, and Ferris, whose reason is the shortest in the book.** The six conditions go in that night and are read back at the first light: no single person may open or suspend it; four inspectors and a gauge before every tide; a flood-season review with a date on the page before the day; a right to be asked before a day's work is added; a roll with forty names and no name at the top, owed by the city; **and nobody whose standing comes from the holder of the pull may open, inspect, sign or suspend it, and the holder of the pull is not on the roll, at his own request, in his own hand, with the date on it.** That condition takes **two hours and ten minutes** to be accepted, because nine people think he is building a martyr; he cannot say the true reason and has entered that he has been the fair reason for five weeks; Tamsin Quill declines twice, saying a sentence in a room is not a wall; and **Priya Shah gets it accepted in about forty seconds with a document sentence: a bar on a man is a thing this city can only obey while the man is standing in the room, and meaning it is not a term, and she is asking for the paper and she does not care what he meant** — and asks that it be entered that he did not think of it in two hours and that he does not think of things that are about his own death, and that this is a fault and not a virtue. **About a minute after the sixth condition is entered, a pale field appears: a person struck out of a roster may be entered again by a person who remains on it, and the person who enters him is answerable for him** — the Crown's *a position may be filled by a person who can hold it* reformulated, and the first of the sixteen to name a process rather than a person. Adrian refuses it in under a second: *answerable is not the opposite of possible.* Renner Sallow says *it is now the eighth.* At about the second hour of the morning of the forty-seventh the loft wall is being read wrongly by about nine people — that the group is finishing, that the dead man's work has gone to the lock crews, that somebody is going to be asked to be in charge of it — and **nobody in that room pointed at the fourth column or said the word column**, and Adrian stands in front of it for four minutes and writes that the wall does not care what he meant.

### Chapter 0047 — The Sentence He Could Not Say
Day 47, the Saturday, the twenty-second day of the new month. About two hundred and forty people put in the converted salt store, about two hundred in it, both numbers entered. The six conditions are read back at the first light and **Adrian declines to read them himself and says why: they are not his and he will not make them sound like his.** The instrument is then put to the city. **About forty people ask him to promise** — a man with a barrow, a woman of sixty, a Hearthguard, a woman with a slate who says *say it properly* twice — and he opens his mouth about eleven times in nineteen minutes and does not say the word. **Priya Shah takes the word off him in English in front of about two hundred people: do not promise, promises have no date and no price and nobody can check them in four months, sign the paper** — and the reason is that a promise binds one person to another and there is no page, and the only man she has watched make a promise in this city is under a board with a lease he never met. **Wenna Cray enters the third statement of error in the history of the book, against a person, naming him, four sentences, all of them correct**: a seat is not a mechanism and it is not a liability; a liability can be got rid of in a room and a seat can only be got rid of by the people who are angry at it; on the sixteenth day nine argued for keeping the post and nine for dropping it and nobody has asked her why; and she is not withdrawing her vote and she is withdrawing her silence. **He answers by standing by it and saying he can say no more** — and says aloud that on the fortieth night he refused a pale field and called it a version lock with better manners and was right about the field, and that he is using the clause today, and that both are true and he does not know which matters more. **Marrow is in the room for nine hours, is the most useful man in it, and says nothing wrong**: a bench for a woman of seventy, the fourth condition read twice for a man who cannot hear, forty cups of water carried by a docker who keeps four, and one entirely true sentence to Adrian that he had nothing to be useful with and he does not think that was luck. Then Adrian says the sentence. He says **I do not know** first, unprompted, and **the crew is the wall and a wall cannot be a price for very long** second, and then **yes, and here is what it will cost, and I am the one who will have to say so every time** — and stops for four seconds, which Vask entered because it is the only four seconds in the history of this book she has wanted in a book, and says aloud that he believes it will fail, and begins a sentence about a man in a good coat and **stops and does not finish it**. He mispronounces *charter* in a yard and **Tamsin Quill corrects him in front of about two hundred and forty people: a charter is a Crown word, we do not have a Crown here, call it a paper with terms on it and let the notary name it** — the fourth time, and she says it is not going to stop being fair and it is going to keep costing him a room. **Ottoline Vask names it: it is the same shape as the five words in that yard, it is a widening and not a rota, and if a name goes at the top of it I will read it out in a yard.** He signs nine pages having read all nine in about nineteen minutes, reading the part about the forty men twice. The **ninth page of the instrument is entered and sealed**, with the work **on the board for the forty-ninth day and not done**, the main breach open, the ground under the chapel entered as a black mark in a place and not repaired, and a man in the ordinary ground under a name he chose himself; and **the Registry's demand is answered a second time**, in nine lines, not in the form required, with the notary asking a clerk in Aurel to notice which of the two answers has a price at the end of it. At about the tenth hour of the night he stands on the chapel stone and **it says nothing** — and he thinks, and does not say, that the twelve words it said in English on the twenty-fifth day describe the Lock of Salt, and has told a notary once and no one else. At the bottom of the two hundred steps he stops at a stair wall where a form with nine clauses and a refusal clause has been hanging for nineteen days, four feet from a wall whose fourth column has been empty for nine days, and there is not one instrument in this world that connects them. In the lower gallery the number is **eight and seven-eighths**, unmoved; the box against the forty-ninth day is empty; and a man who has said *yes* in a room does not go up the two hundred steps.

## Volume 01, Batch 0006 — Chapters 48–50: The Trial Licence (the volume's closing three chapters)

### Chapter 0048 — The Day Before
Day 48, the Sunday, the twenty-third day of the new month. Four named people go down the walk-round in the wrong weather at a falling tide to look at **a gauge with one mark cut on it on the twenty-seventh day of this flood**, which is the only number in this city that can stop a tide, and a docker of fifty-eight who cannot read argues for the ninth day running that **a river does not keep one number** and is entered as not answered, because a mark is not a rule, not a term and not a promise. The six conditions are read for the last time and the seventh item is read with them — **the cost is entered before the tide turns and not after**, a cost entered before being a price and a cost entered after being a bill — and the cost column is read aloud at its present size. **Nine names are filled in over most of the day** by a room that discovers that the first four people anybody thought of were all crew or all the man in the seat, that the crew cannot stop the crew, and that **the nine have to be people the working costs**: Renna Ord (named by herself), Hesk, Nell Bray, Elsa Skell, Ilda Marsh, Hessa Ruun, Hesper Saye, Peth Lomas and Nyla Ferrow. Adrian is refused a place on it by Wenna Cray, not by himself. **A form for watching the scar is written by Nyla Ferrow with a slate and a girl of seventeen** — a line of salt, four chalk depths, four people twice a day, a slate on a nail — and the keeper enters what it **cannot** do: it cannot close anything, and it cannot say where tomorrow's residue will go, and it is a form and not a promise. **The fourth page of the instrument is written and it is the one nobody wanted**: what happens on the day the crew does not turn up, that the join is where it is, that the instrument is not suspended by anybody, that the record says the crew did not turn up and does not say a fault and does not name a man, and **a cost that stops is a cost that has stopped and is never to be entered as paid**. Vail says she has never entered a page whose purpose is to be read by somebody who is angry, and a man from another world says four words: **then that is the point.** Renna Ord gets *refused* changed to **not owing** and says the change is worse than hers and says good. **A name is found at the top of the second page of the roll** — the boy of nineteen who keeps it, for the true reason that a page with no date on it can be moved to a different month — and Vask reads it out in a yard and it comes off and the date stays. **The twenty-fourth reading is prepared and not taken**, in a gallery with nineteen men, because a reading on the ebb of a working tide is a number and a number on the wrong water is a lie, and Nell Bray says it is the first true thing anybody in that gallery has said about a number in forty days. **Vask's two sentences are put in front of the room a second time and are not answered**, and Renna Ord answers in nine words — *the toll is the crew, the date is March* — the room does not find it satisfactory and the crew does not care. **No System field.** It ends at the eleventh hour with an empty box in the tide table and a boy of nineteen at the bottom of two hundred steps asking for a rule for Monday, and there is none, and the difference between an answer and a rule is that the page is what is left when the person is asleep or hurt.

### Chapter 0049 — The Working
Day 49, the Monday, the twenty-fourth day of the new month, the next tide. **The four go down at about the fourth hour with a gauge at a falling tide in the wrong weather**, the water is four inches below the twenty-eighth mark and the tide is worked, and the four say four different things about the same piece of oak and **do not agree, and the notary enters that they did not agree**. **The post ends at noon in daylight because a term is a term and a date is a date**, the seat is vacant from noon, a vacant seat is not a fault, and its terms go with it; Wenna Cray says *then somebody had better put him in the form*, and the form on the nail gains a fifth item — **that he is told and asked, and that it is a question and not a duty, and the day it becomes a duty somebody is to take it off the wall in daylight and say so.** The crew goes under the near gate at the sixth hour and the eighth hour, at the ordinary volume, out of the line and not together, and **Adrian stands four feet back on the sill of the third bay and a docker makes him say out loud, in the local language and to twenty men, that he is not in it and is barred from opening, inspecting, signing and stopping it.** **The join steps at the seventh hour** and the step is entered in the unnamed mason's own hand before the number is read, the second time in six days, and Wenna Cray asks the fact-or-fault question and gets *a crack does not step*. **It is closed in about eleven minutes of decided work** — a bar, a line, a woman calling a number every eleven minutes, forty men on a Tuesday's job — and Vask gives the mason's sentence: **a closed join does not get narrower, it gets dressed**, and the docker's *nine* and the boy's number on the wall of the second bay stay where they are for the rest of this world's life. **Nobody died, nobody was torn, and one man did not come back for the second time under the near gate** — Ferris, who had voted no on the twenty-first and asked to be asked again, and who has not been paid — and there is no entry and no fault and **nobody went to anybody's door**, and Renna Ord is seen at the top of two hundred steps not going down. Renna Ord's prediction of four men hurt before March is **entered as a prediction with her date and the right date beside it and neither above the other**, and she says she will be proved wrong and hopes to be. **The twenty-fourth reading is eight and seven-eighths**, because the join is closed, and there is one ruled line left in the column, and Vask says a decision can be looked up and a number can only be taken. **The cost is entered for the seventh time before the tide turns and not after**, and Vail says the sentence about a column this city can afford and then **says the other half**: a column re-entered at its new size seven times in one month is a column being kept honest, and it is the first thing in five weeks she has been able to enter that she is not ashamed of. The residue goes under the chapel floor: a man with a barrow who was nearest felt a finger's breadth and warm boards, nothing came up and nothing came out. **It was luck and not skill** and Renna Ord says it in seventeen words: *that is not a thing we did, that is a thing that did not happen to us.* The main breach is closed and **said nothing**. Tamsin Quill says she did not feel a working. **No System field.** The row for the forty-ninth day stays empty, because a boy of nineteen who wrote that word once **puts the chalk down and says he is not going to be the man who writes it every time**, and a woman writes in the fourth volume until the lamp goes out.

### Chapter 0050 — The Trial Licence
Day 50, the Tuesday, the twenty-fifth day of the new month. **The four who signed the inspection of the fourth day of this flood — Tamsin Quill, Nell Bray, Ovin Sarr and Hettis Roke — re-inspect the Narrow Mark with Ottoline Vask's hands on the stone**, and Vask says *it has not moved, not one sixteenth, and I have been wrong about that, and being wrong about that is the best thing anybody has said to me in a month.* **The count required per opening goes from four to two; the quorum does not change and cannot.** Nell Bray gives the argument: *four was a quorum, two is an opening, and if you make an opening cost a quorum nobody will ever open it, and if you make the quorum cheap you have a man who opens it alone* — **a working made cheaper without being made private** — and the two may not both be of the crew. The standing order is entered whole: one named living person per opening, two witnesses not of the crew, a fresh inspection before every opening in whatever weather there is, a separately established and separately costed return mark belonging to the person who goes, a flood-season review with a date on the page before the day, suspension by any one of nine named persons or the receiving community, Nine Elms' water still not received, and the instrument of the twenty-second day of the new month unamended. **The fourth column of the loft wall is filled in for the first time in fifty days**, after the keeper asks *what is the work that is in that cell* and the room discovers nobody knows — because nobody had ever written down what the work was. **The work is the nineteen stones**, because Sabra Vint asked on the nineteenth day of the new month for somebody to write down what she thinks they mean, and the cell is filled in two hands — a woman's from another world and a Hearthguard's — with the nineteen, the eight that are different, the meaning nobody has written, and the single word **Eight**, and it is not a post and anybody may pick it up. Roke says, in nine seconds and in front of about two hundred and forty people, that he has not been asked about his own in nine years and is not being asked now, and **a person who has never been asked is not a person who has refused**, and does not look at Sabra Vint when she comes down the steps for the third time in nine years. **Owen Park's burial term is settled in four pages** that stand and are not shortened and are not summarised in any instrument, return, quarterly description or letter, entered as what a person said, containing the two languages and the upside-down second line, the shoulder, his own reason for his own name, the year of certainty in a mason's words, and Mina Okoye's sentence in her own words, with **nobody permitted to write *he died because*** — and **Aldis Kerrow tries to write one economical line beginning with *because* and Priya Shah takes it out of his hand and gives him four lines that fit his column instead.** **The seventeen are named**, six on one page and eleven on another, and the longer list is what they do not have: no land rights, no class, no standing, **no workway of any kind**, entered as a term and not a favour, and Adrian's page says **Unbound** in his own words with two dates on it and that he is party to the licence and is given nothing by it. About ninety people want a stipend and Renna Ord refuses it in the language of the room: **a stipend is a wage, a wage is owed to a person, a person is described on a roll, and the roll goes to Aurel four times a year in a wooden case.** The licence has to say who answers for the next test, the room reaches for Adrian, he is offered a small seat a third time, and **the instrument says nobody**. Marrow does not put his hand up and enters that he does not know why. **The seventeenth System field arrives on the page that lists what the seventeen do not have**: the roll may be closed, the person named may be returned on the next tide once, and the person named does not owe the standing charge — three true things and a price, and the price is a field and nine households who have refused twice. **He refuses it in under a second and gives the reason: closing the roll means the woman in Camden is never told anything, ever, by anybody.** Renner Sallow says it is now the ninth. **Adrian says *class* in a room of two hundred and forty people and is stopped by a procedure and not by a woman** — the bar from the twenty-sixth day reads the document back as an error — and says *people* instead. The licence is signed at the sixth hour, sealed, dated on every page, with a right of review, a right of refusal, a right to be asked, a page that says what it costs, and the page about the day the crew does not turn up attached. **He reads it in twenty-nine minutes and signs last.** In the evening **Ferris writes *works* in the box against the fiftieth day**, in the fiftieth's own row, five weeks after he did not come back for the second shift under the near gate because he has not been paid, and **the box against the forty-ninth day is still empty** because a boy of nineteen put the chalk down on a ledge rather than write the word every time; and fifty days after she wrote the word against the thirty-third **Renna Ord is asked what she decided** and gives the smallest answer in the book: *I did not want to be the only person in the room who had not.* **The lock opens for one named living person** — two witnesses, Peth Lomas and Hessa Ruun, a fresh inspection signed that morning, the four who authorised it in the yard and not at the opening — and **Eli Mercer, who refused eleven times, strikes his own name and then unstrucks it**: *I have said no eleven times to people who wanted something. I am going to say yes once to a page.* He goes and comes back in about eleven minutes and says **four words: *the same room, taller***, and nobody asks him a second question. **The final image:** Tamsin Quill writes the next inspection date in the public ledger on a slate on a nail while the entry of the fourth day of this flood — the one naming Adrian Vale as the route's first witness — lies on a sill two hundred steps below under a piece of ordinary river stone; **Sabra Vint says the number nineteen to a boy of nine who does not know what it is**; and **Tam Crane asks whether the next ones will be players**, and Adrian Vale looks at the dark water and has not yet found an answer that does not make the child smaller, and says instead that nobody who comes through that wall is a special kind of person, and that he was not the first one, he was the first one anybody could see. **Nothing gained. He is Unbound to the last line.**

## Volume 02 — Salt and Silver

### Chapter 0051 — The Room and the Road
Day 51, the Wednesday, the twenty-sixth day of the new month. **The door is not a road home, and the reason is on page two of the licence and is read out in a room with nineteen signers in it, and a notary of fifty enters that she did not ask one of the nineteen what that paragraph said and that a notary and not a schoolmistress is the correct division of work and is the whole of what happened here.** Adrian gives the true answer nine times in nine rooms to nine people entitled to ask it, and about nine people hear the difference and about two hundred and thirty do not. **The eleven of the second list are asked one at a time with the door shut: eight want the road that goes where they were born, and three do not — Kirsty Lund, sixteen, who wants to know what the shapes on the nineteen stones say; Eli Mercer, nineteen, *there is nobody there, I asked*; and Bern Zolt, forty-four, who does not want to go back and has asked for a page saying he asked and was not argued with.** Odile Ferrand, thirty-eight: **a review with no questions on it is a queue.** A licensed carrier of Crown's second-table mail says that a letter is a thing that travels along a road and that there is not one, and asks, in a room, for the Crown channel offered on the fortieth day to be asked about, and it is entered that Adrian has known about it for eleven days and has not mentioned it because the sentence he has not wanted to say is *it is not a road and it is a queue*. **The first request for an opening is entered as a request**, with a name, a date, a person who asked, an answer that is a reason and not a refusal of the person, and a fifth line that is not part of the form. And at the seventh hour of the evening the review date is read off the slate on the nail in about two hundred and forty people's hearing — **the fiftieth day of the new month, with a date on it and no questions on it** — and somebody asks how many times the door has opened, and the answer is **twice in fifty-one days and one of the two was a breach of its own terms**, and nobody in the yard laughs.

### Chapter 0052 — Salt
Day 52, the Thursday, the twenty-seventh day of the new month. **The price on the nail is a double handful of salt twice a day and salt has gone up a third since the season turned**, and Vask's sentence is that a price is not a fact about a thing but a fact about how many people want the thing on the same morning. Nyla Ferrow, seventy, has eleven weeks of her own ration left and sold none of it. **Ottavia Reek offers thirty tons on the Measure's account at the retail price, plus timber, iron, lime, rope, a boat and the rail in nine days, against the day and flood crews' labour drawn from the Measure's list — refused in a yard in about eleven seconds by Renna Ord's rule, which is in the book since the fortieth day of the flood: a wage off another list is not a wage, it is a favour with a handle on it.** The salt is bought with labour out of the crew's shift, **and in the doing of it the room discovers that the four who change the salt and take the four chalk depths are the four who sign the fresh inspection before every opening, and that the nine who may stop the working may stop neither.** Nyla Ferrow, asked whether she would rather not, says that she would rather be asked. **The rail on the two hundred steps is half-rotten for about four feet at the fortieth step and goes on ice at the third hour of the afternoon**, and a docker's apprentice of twenty, Tam Ockley, goes down on it and cannot rise for about nine minutes, and the four have to choose between the man and the seam, **and the choice is entered in nine lines by the girl of seventeen as what a person chose and not as what was right**, and she wants it entered that nobody did a wrong thing and that is not the same as nothing having gone wrong. The rail is condemned, the mending goes on the cost column for the eighth time, and Ovin Sarr says that he has put his hand on that rail thirty times and has never once asked who cut it. And in the market the word on the board is on three boards, and a man who wrote it in the second week and has not said so in forty-five days finds the line in his own notebook that says *they think it is a game* and reads it four times.

### Chapter 0053 — A Hand That Will Not Close
Day 53, the Friday, the twenty-eighth day of the new month. **The eighteenth field comes up in a room above the salt weavers' chapel with nineteen people in it and stands about nineteen seconds, and it is the cruellest shape yet because it puts the whole of the cost on a named person and calls that fair, and the sentence is true: a workway may be given to one person, the hand will hold, the person will not be able to stop, nothing is asked of this city, nothing is asked of the licence, the person is the whole of the cost, the cost is entered.** Adrian refuses in under a second and is the wrong man to refuse it and enters that he has said no first and understood later for fifty-three days and that it is the worst habit he owns. **The holder is Teodor Blaz, thirty-one, of Leeds, of the second list, whose right hand has not made a fist since the twenty-fifth day of this flood, in the same yard where the eleven came out, and the page that exists about it is four lines long and is about somebody else.** He refuses in four seconds and says the true reason, which is not the good one. **Mina Okoye asks the standing question — what is the work — and the room discovers in about eleven minutes that there is no office in Saltwake for a hand that holds things**, because every other work in this city has a place where somebody expects the holder, and a workway is a claim on a job that does not exist, and the cost is not the work: **the cost is that a man with a standing is a man who has been given a reason to be angry, and a seat is only ever got rid of by the people who are angry at it.** Vask: *a workway is a post with a person in it, and the post has not been through a room.* Afterwards, in a yard, in private, **Teodor asks the question nobody has asked him — if I had said yes, who would have been angry — and Adrian writes the answer down and gives it to him and it is nine names and not one person, including Ivenn Marrow, who would not be angry and whose ninth sentence would be a compliment.** Teodor reads it twice and says *then it is not about me* and then *it is about the room.* Renner Sallow's count is ten. And Adrian writes in a private book the sentence he has been avoiding for forty days: the thing he refused three times is the only thing he was ever going to be offered, he refused it because it worked, and a man whose hand does not close has not got that luxury.

### Chapter 0054 — The Word He Wrote Down
Day 54, the Saturday, the twenty-ninth day of the new month. **Gareth Mbeki, twenty-seven, of the second list, proposes in about four minutes that the seventeen be split among eleven households for the winter, two to a house, and it is the first thing one of the seventeen has proposed in fifty-four days and it is a good idea, and about four people in that room are frightened by how good it is** — because it is what a person says when they have stopped believing a door home exists, and Adrian writes that in a notebook and does not say it in the room, because it is a sentence about a person's belief and he has no standing to have it. **The household that offers first is the household that owes an answer: Delya Crane and Sesh Brack offer two beds and the reason is a man who is under a board behind an ordinary wall, and a bone-setter of fifty-eight makes them both say *I do not know what I am proposing* and then say it anyway, in that order, and neither of them had practised it.** The two beds are accepted and the eleven-household plan is not, and the difference is nine lines: **eleven houses with two strangers in each is a roll, and a roll goes to Aurel four times a year in a wooden case.** And at about the fourth hour of the afternoon, in a yard under the chapel, in about two hundred and forty people, **Adrian Vale says out loud that he wrote the word *player* on a market board on the ninth day of this flood in his own hand in a language almost nobody there can read, and told six men it was their error, and that about forty people have copied it since.** Renna Ord says *then take it down*; he says he cannot; and she says four more words: *then say over it.* **Wenna Cray enters the fourth statement of error in the history of the book**, four sentences, all correct, withdrawing a belief and not a vote, and asking to be entered as having said it to him in a yard and not to nine people in a room. **And Priya Shah gets up on a trestle in the market and writes fifty-nine words over it in chalk, in two lines of ten and forty-nine, in front of about two hundred and forty people, and leaves the shorter word underneath.** Adrian says it nine times to about ninety people, and Sabra Vint makes him say both sentences in the same mouth in one evening because in nine years she will be asked which came first. And that night he finds the line in his own notebook and writes under it, in thirty-one words, the only correction he has ever entered in that book: *it is the only sentence in this book that I quoted back at myself, and it is four words long, and it is mine.*

### Chapter 0055 — The Man Who Did Not Say
Day 55, the Sunday, the thirtieth day of the new month. **The keeper's board is washed at the seventh hour in front of about a hundred and forty people and the four words and the fifth go back on and nothing else is on the keeper's side, and Vask's four items are on the other side of the door and are not washed.** The batch's centre: **the fifth item of the form on the nail obliges somebody to be told and asked, and it is a question and not a duty, and at about the sixth hour and forty minutes of the morning a docker of fifty-eight reads out the list of the four who read the join-line in about two hundred and forty people's hearing and puts a Hearthguard of forty-four on it in daylight, for the first time in nine years, because a person who has never been asked is not a person who has refused.** He says no to the form, asks to be asked again on a different day, and predicts he will say no for about four years — and then says the thing he has not said in nine years, and it is forty seconds long: **the names were written down once, in his grandfather's hand, in the year the ground was made, because a Hearthguard who held a gate was required to give a roll of the dead of that gate to the Crown twice a year; eight got a stone and four got a name cut on the stone of a person they were buried with, because there was not enough money for eight, and he has known that for nine years and never said it in a yard; the book went to Aurel in a wooden case, then four times a year, for nineteen years, and a clerk on a quay wrote the count in a book and the count was eight every year; and nobody came.** And in one afternoon about two hundred and forty people work out, in chalk on a yard wall, that **a description is not a record of where a person is and is a list of where a person is with a column for it, and the last column is the widest one and it is always blank because the office is nine hundred miles away and has never once sent anybody to fill it in** — and the roll of strangers goes in the same kind of case, four times a year, on a river boat. Priya Shah asks four questions about the column, and a notary of fifty answers in eighteen words: the column is for the office, the office is nine hundred miles away, it has never sent anybody. **And Adrian asks, out loud, whether anybody in this city has ever seen a name come back out of one of those cases, and about two hundred and forty people understand that nobody has.** Marrow is in the room all afternoon, gets forty-one cups of water out, asks a docker to carry them and to keep four, and she keeps four. And at the ninth hour a woman of sixty-one who cannot read is told what a man said about eight graves, and says the number nineteen out loud twice, and will not say why, and asks to be asked properly, in a room, on a different day.

### Chapter 0056 — What the Description Is For
Day 56, the Monday, the thirty-first day of the new month. **The fourth Crown instrument arrives and it is a table, and Sera Vail says out loud, before anybody has finished reading it, that it is a fourth document and not a version of the other three, and that it is the first Crown instrument in this city that does not want a person in it.** A **schedule of crossings**, quarterly, in a form required, complete or not received, a summary is not a schedule, a figure may not be rounded, **at a fee of eleven ounces of silver**, and the fourth line says *a fee is charged in respect of the office's work, and an office that is not paid does not work.* **Renner Sallow, who has been at a table in this city since the fourth day of the flood and has never been asked to stop, gives the volume its politics in fourteen words: a fee is a thing that tells you what an office thinks you are** — and that the office not working is a condition and not a punishment — and then adds, in a second sentence, that an office that is not paid does not collect the ounce either, and that he did not mean to. And in a yard, with a bar in her hand, **Ottoline Vask invents the honest useless form in nine sentences: fill every column in truthfully and then make every line useless, no rounding, no summary, nothing an office can act on, because a summary is what an office acts on and a summary is where a decision hides.** It is sixty-one lines, sealed, and **it is dangerous in the exact shape Adrian has warned about for sixty days, because it puts eleven names in a column headed *name of the person who went* and a true sentence in the last column saying there is no receiving community, and that sentence now goes to a building nine hundred miles away four times a year in a wooden case.** The schedule's last column is headed ***person to be asked***, *where the local record is not maintained in the district* — **the only seat in nine lines of Crown table, and Renner Sallow says so and says it is not a demand for an interpreter, it is a demand for a person who answers.** Adrian puts his own name in it, and says out loud that he has wanted a page with his name at the top of it for five weeks and that this is the cheapest possible version of one and it is a Crown document, and **Priya Shah tells him in twenty-five words what she has done and makes him write them on a slate: you have made yourself the only person in this city an office can write to, now be honest about how much of that you wanted.** The fee is entered as owing with no date, and Renner Sallow enters that the office does not charge a fee it cannot collect and that he does not know what the fee is about and has never written that in any book before.

### Chapter 0057 — The Nineteenth Field
Day 57, the Tuesday, the thirty-second day of the new month. **The nineteenth field comes up in a room over a chandlery in front of about nine people and one of the nine is asleep, and Perry Lask, forty-one, of the second list, says yes in about four seconds after saying one sentence first: that she has been waiting since the ninth day of this flood for the wall to say something she recognises, and that she is not going to pretend to a room that she checked it first.** She says the rest of it out loud, and it is her sentence and it is the volume's: **a rule that only covers the thing you can see is a rule about a thing and not about the world**, and she has read a licence page in four languages in her life and has broken every one of them. **The city then discovers in about eleven minutes that it has no procedure for a workway and cannot have one**, because a workway is a job and a job means somebody is entitled to expect a thing of the holder, and there is no office, no guild, no bench and no Crown instrument in Saltwake that can say what a holder is expected to do. Vask: *a workway is a post with a person in it and the post has not been through a room.* Priya Shah, in five words: *then put it through a room.* **They write a procedure in about a day: nine clauses and nine weaknesses, both entered on the face of it, with a review date of the fourth day of the month after this one, and the instrument cannot compel and cannot remove, and the work itself is not written down anywhere and the document says so in twenty-eight words.** And Adrian Vale says the sentence this volume is built on, in thirty-one words, in front of nine people: **his bar is not broken — a pale field in a room is not the instrument, nobody used anything, there was no hand on any lever — and the page is perfect and does not reach, and I wrote a rule about a thing and I did not write a rule about the world, and I have spent fifty-seven days telling a city that the difference is pedantry.** He says the page they have just written is a mat, four boards and a nail, and that if it is the best this city can do about a wall that says something in a stranger's language, then the honest thing is to say that in a yard with a date on it. Teodor Blaz then says the sentence nobody has answered: **nobody was angry about my hand, what happened is that there was nothing for me to be angry about, and not one word in nine clauses is about a person who has nothing to be angry about.** The girl of seventeen enters, in four lines in a four-line column, that the holder asked what she is expected to do and nobody could answer her, and that there is no office.

### Chapter 0058 — The Day the Crew Did Not Turn Up
Day 58, the Wednesday, the thirty-third day of the new month. **At about the fourth hour of the morning, in ice, Ovin Sarr, nineteen, of the day crew, who has had eight watches in three weeks and who wrote *works* in the last column of the tide table once and then put the chalk down, is hurt under the near gate at the ordinary volume in no working on a wet shoring beam, and nobody did a wrong thing, and the entry says the beam was wet and the boy was fast.** **The fourth page of the instrument is used for the first time and does all four things it was written to do**, in about two hundred and forty people's hearing: the join is where it is, nobody is suspended, the record says the crew were not owed and did not come, **there is no fault and no name and no door and no entry of anybody going to anybody's door**, the cost column stands as last entered and the difference between that figure and the truth is a gap and not an error, the next tide is not worked until four people have been down the walk-round in the wrong weather with a gauge, and a cost that stops is a cost that has stopped. **Sera Vail: it works, it is horrible and it is correct, and both of those are the same fact, and I would like the room to be quiet for about nine seconds.** One man short, the day's work is not done and is entered as not done — not deferred, not short, not carried forward, because a thing that is not done is not done — and the four went down the condemned rail with a gauge and **did not agree about the dressed edge, and that is entered, and no person may say the four agreed because the four did not.** And at the eighth hour of the evening Adrian says the sentence he has been avoiding since the first week: **he told this city before March that four of the forty would be hurt, one at a time, on an ordinary morning, in no working, and no instrument can stop it, and the other half is that the instrument is the reason it is being counted, and writing a price down is not the same as agreeing to it.** Renna Ord says four words: *it is the same price.* And a docker of fifty-eight asks, in about two hundred and forty people's hearing, whether the four is a number or a prediction, and it is entered as **a number entered by a person and a prediction entered by a person, on the page together, neither above the other, both standing, neither removable**, and the entry says she was thanked by nobody. And in the evening the sixth word goes into the last column of the tide table in a hand nobody recognises, and it is not the word that is in the other five, and a boy of nineteen with a shoulder that is out asks for the chalk and writes nothing and gives it back.

### Chapter 0059 — Eight of Nine
Day 59, the Thursday, the thirty-fourth day of the new month. **Jonah Pike comes back with the only winter money in Saltwake: the Brass Measure's credit, which he has drafted himself, as a materials account with forty men in it and the fourth term in a note in another hand — salt, timber, iron, rope, lime, chalk, a boat for a fortnight, the rail in nine days, against the day and flood crews' labour drawn from the Measure's own list.** Renna Ord refuses it in a yard in about eleven seconds with the rule that has been in the book since the fortieth day of the flood, and about nine people in that yard want it and the want is entered as a show of wanting and not as a count. **He then says nine sentences in about two hundred and forty people's hearing and is right in eight of them, and the ninth is not about Aurel and it is the best thing anybody has said about Adrian in sixty days: you have spent fifty-nine days making sure that none of them has a job, you wrote the page yourself, in March one of them is going to be a person with no work in a city with no money, and you have made that a term, and a term is the most durable kind of promise there is.** His fifth and sixth sentences are the front door of the White Ledger: **a city in which nothing that goes wrong has a person in it is a city in which nothing that goes wrong can be fixed, and a guild is not a person and can be refused and does not go home and get old and want a different winter.** Adrian has a tenth sentence ready and says out loud, in the yard, that it is about a man who is expensive and is not on this side of the water and that it is the wrong sentence, and that the ninth is about March and the tenth is about a gateway. And at the ninth hour of the evening, on the two hundred steps, with the rail condemned and the dressed join eight and seven-eighths below, **Tamsin Quill takes the notebook out of his hand and does not give it back**, and says that the ninth sentence is not about the seventeen but about him — that in about six pages he has written down what a person may not do and not once what a person may do, and that he has been frightened of a Crown for six weeks and being frightened of a Crown is the only thing he knows how to do — and then gives him **twenty-four words, once:** *go and ask the city what a person with no work is for, and take the answer, and do not offer anybody a seat.* And she enters that she said it and not that she found it, and that she has declined twice to find his sentence and that this was the same work and not the same favour. And the day book for the day has a list of nine names in it that two people got onto by being in a room, and neither of them was asked.

### Chapter 0060 — Four Knocks
Day 60, the Friday, the thirty-fifth day of the new month. **A docker of fifty-eight picks up the work in the fourth column of a stranger's wall because a work anybody may pick up is a work, and has been copying the shapes of nineteen stones into a slate for three days, and cannot read what she is copying, and has entered that she got tired of waiting and that this is not a noble sentence.** Hettis Roke goes up the upper road for the first time in nine years with Sabra Vint and the docker, and on the way he says in about forty people's hearing that **he did not stay away because of the eight but because of the walking, and that he has told this city grief for nine years and it was the walking.** **At the eleventh stone, about two hundred yards short of where the eleven end, there is a name cut in letters about a hundred and forty years old in a Hearthguard's hand, and it is one of the eight, and he puts his hand on it and says four words: that is my grandfather's.** The names were not lost; they were moved, nine miles, to a road, by a person with a knife and a reason, and the reason is not on any page in this city. And at about the second hour of the morning, **a man in the lower gallery reports three knocks in the dressed join on the twenty-ninth and four on the thirty-fifth, and Renna Ord enters both facts and deletes neither, on the standing rule: a man who cannot be right about a number hears a number, and a join that is dressed does not knock, and neither fact is a reason not to write the next one down** — and a number going up is not a pattern and a number going up is also not nothing, and **nobody had to open anything and nothing in this city was done at either hour.** Adrian goes down at about the eleventh hour, four feet back on the sill of the third bay with a rule he does not need; the dressed join is eight and seven-eighths and does not narrow; **the black line in his palm is fifty-nine days old and has not moved one sixteenth of an inch, and neither that nor the number on the wall is an answer and both are the only things he has.** And **Curtis Okonkwo, twenty, of the second list, who came out of the chapel floor on the twenty-fifth day of this flood and has asked for nothing in the thirty-five days since and is on no list and has been fed every day by people who did not ask him whether he wanted the food, asks: *are they coming?*** Adrian answers in four sentences — he does not know; there is a seam that opens for one named person and the far side is a room with no window and no door anybody has found; nobody has ever come through it into this city; a man in the gallery heard three knocks and then four and both are in a book and a form on a nail cannot say where a residue goes or who is in that room — **and the fourth sentence is a question, asked out loud on a stair to about nine people: what do we do about a knock.** Nobody answers it, and it is entered that nobody answered it, and it is entered that about nine people heard the question and that about two hundred and forty people in this city have not been asked it and will be, in a yard, with a date on the page before the day. Tam Crane is not there. **The answer to the boy of nine is still unwritten on a wall in a room above a market.**

### Chapter 0061 — What a Person With No Work Is For
Day 61, the Saturday, the thirty-sixth day of the new month. **Tamsin Quill's twenty-four words are acted on over one day and it takes four kitchens, because he cannot ask the question the way he means it.** A woman offers him a chair and takes it back when she hears him call it news. A boy of fifteen offers him a day's carrying. A docker offers him a place in a queue and says the truest thing anybody has said to him in sixty days: *it is not work and it is not nothing and it is the only thing in this city a man with no work can get.* And then Marek Osley asks whether he means work or a wage, and teaches him in four sentences that the two are not the same, and that a wage in this city is a thing only a person with a name can hold, and that a name in this city is a thing that can be found. **The room's word is not *work*. It is *turning up.*** Ottoline Vask then writes nineteen lines in about eleven minutes, and they are a widening and not a rota, and they say what a person in this city may be asked to turn up for, at a night's notice, at the ordinary rate, with no name at the top of anything, and with a line saying a person may refuse and the refusal does not stop the line being offered again on a different day, and a nineteenth line that is the only question among the nineteen. **Vask says the second sentence in the book that says a working can be made cheaper without being made private, and says it is about a person instead of a door.** And the two beds in Silt Row are the test case and they fail on the first morning they are used, because the clause that protects a household from being asked again is the same clause that makes a person who did not turn up unfindable, and the girl of seventeen writes the most uncomfortable page written in sixty days. The first line is read out in about two hundred and forty people and about nine understand it and about two hundred and thirty think it is a job.

### Chapter 0062 — The List That Did Not Go
Day 62, the Sunday, the thirty-seventh day of the new month. **The reason the second list has not gone into a wooden case since the third week of the new month is a box and not a conspiracy.** The roll of strangers goes to Aurel four times a year, carried by a licensed carrier of the second-table mail, and the roll is written in the notary's own hand, and she did not fill in a column, and eleven days later the carrier came to a counter and waited about an hour and went away. **A case with nobody in it is a case nobody can charge to carry, and a carrier is paid by the person whose description is in it.** Sera Vail has entered, on her own motion and not at anybody's asking, that she did not send it, with two reasons, one true and one convenient, and they are not the same one: a description is the only protection anybody here has ever been offered by that office, and eleven people who have asked to go home are eleven people an office may act on. **Nadia Brandt, twenty-nine, works the whole of it out in about an hour on a salt bin and tells a woman of twenty-four and not the man, and gives her reason in nine words: *I do not yet know whether I am complaining.*** Peth Lammay then asks the question nobody can answer — does anybody in this city want that case to go — twice, in a counter room and in a yard, and nobody answers her, and she says thirteen words she has been carrying for eleven years. Two people are named who are on no roll at all, a girl of sixteen and a woman of thirty-eight, and neither has been asked, and the asking is a form with a date on it that about nine people signed and about nine did not, and one of the four reasons given for not signing is that a person who has said no to a form is now on a page. The roll goes into the book with a hole in it, the girl of seventeen writes *a hole in a page is not a smaller number, it is a place where a number used to be* and asks for the line herself, and Vask enters the finding in twenty-five words with no number in it at all.

### Chapter 0063 — Eleven Ounces
Day 63, the Monday, the thirty-eighth day of the new month. **There is no silver in this city that a city can use, and the arithmetic is not a conspiracy.** About forty ounces are in eleven private places and about eleven people know the total and the reason none of them has ever said it out loud is that saying the number is making a list of houses with money in them, and that is the shape this city has spent sixty-two days learning to be afraid of. Adrian asks four people how to raise eleven ounces and gets the same sentence out of three of them and it is not *no*, it is *ask me why*: a quartermaster tells him he does not own anything and that she would rather write that down than have him tell a clerk in Aurel that he had. The Brass Measure offers eleven ounces as a loan against the crew's shift list and is refused in about eleven seconds by Renna Ord's rule for the fourth time, which she says in English on purpose, **and this time the coat is a receipt, and a receipt is not an instrument and cannot be refused in a room, and it is on a table in a yard and nobody has torn it up.** Orla Specht, sixty-one, of Silt Row, has eleven ounces under a floorboard and has not spent a coin in nine years, and says why in seventy-one words, and it is not about money: her mother was carried out of a yard in the fourth month of a flood year by four men who were owed money and came anyway, and one of them carried her and one of them carried the box. And nine people turn out to have each assumed somebody else was going to raise the fee, and Adrian's notebook sentence is thirty-three words long, and under it, in a private book nobody has seen, is the batch's real finding: the credit of this city is not a chest of silver, it is a list of about eleven people who each believe they are the reason something happened.

### Chapter 0064 — The First Wayhouse
Day 64, the Tuesday, the thirty-ninth day of the new month. **The first Wayhouse is defined and written down, and local law in this book means somebody in the room had to say no to something, so four of its six things are refusals.** A bed and a bowl for anybody travelling, paid by a levy on the road's trade at a rate the traders agreed among themselves, kept by a named household with a date, open to a list of nobody, closable by its own household on a date and not asked why twice, not a Crown instrument and not a page anybody may carry to Aurel, and a sixth thing that came from a man from another world who did not mean to give it: **nobody may be turned away for asking what it is.** It is nearly lost to a misunderstanding about a list, because a housekeeper of fifty-one wrote the four households that have agreed to the levy on a board in chalk in a yard, the way this city does everything, and **a board anybody can read is a thing anybody can copy.** The board comes down in daylight in front of about two hundred and forty people, read out before it is washed, and the four names go into a book with a date, and nobody in that yard says the word *legal* and about nine people have it in their heads. The fourth refusal is the levy itself, which is collected whether anybody uses the house or not, and a carter says the reason out loud in fifteen words. Hettis Roke is asked about his aunt being a keeper and says he would rather be asked than be safe. Two of the seventeen are refused a bed, and the reason is that eleven people in a rope store with a list of what is owed on the wall is the only thing keeping that room dry, and a Wayhouse that cannot say no is not a Wayhouse. And Adrian's notebook says that in sixty-three days he has built a protection for eleven people out of a room, a list and a key, and the key is the strongest thing he owns and the only thing in this world he has ever been able to give away.

### Chapter 0065 — One of the Seventeen Is Paid
Day 65, the Wednesday, the fortieth day of the new month. **The first mixed trade in Saltwake happens on a floor above a chandlery, badly, in the wrong weather, and it is four transactions, three of them a mistake and one not.** A man of thirty-one with a hand that does not close is paid for nine weeks of counting at a rate he set himself and was too low, and enters the reason himself in thirteen words. A chandler of forty-four buys nine weeks of chalk-marks from a girl of sixteen and does not pay her, and when she asks for it in his own yard he says the worst sentence anybody has said about work in this city, and it is nine words, and it is not malice: *I am not going to pay for a habit.* A man who keeps a stall by the wharf buys a fortnight of translating from a woman of forty-one of the second list and pays her **on a second page**, and the man who keeps the stall says the other half of the rule in fourteen words — *a wage off a second page is a wage she can be removed from* — about a woman standing in the yard who says yes, **and she is paid and she is described, both.** The fourth transaction is a household buying nine weeks of slate-copying from a docker of fifty-eight who cannot read, paid in salt, and she says nine words and the ninth of them is *silver*. Then **the twentieth thing the wall has said comes up and it is a licence to be written about**: a holder's work may be described in any document in her own words without anybody asking her first, and nobody may be asked to describe it, and the holder does not owe the standing charge, and the cost is entered. **The holder says yes in about four seconds and does not say a sentence first, and the not saying is the finding.** Adrian refuses it in under a second; the room refuses it in nine seconds because a page exists and nine people can read one out loud; the acceptance is entered in her own words with a date and **nobody has yet asked her whether she meant it.** And the room decides, in nine minutes, the four things a holder of a workway is asked before the fourth day of the month after this one — three of which are refusals — and does not ask her anything, and she says six words: *then ask me on the day.*

### Chapter 0066 — The Man With a Shoulder
Day 66, the Thursday, the forty-first day of the new month. **Dante Kuo has his scene, thirty-one days late and thirty-one chapters overdue, and it is small.** He is thirty-five; his right shoulder went on the thirty-first day of this flood while steadying a door frame, was reduced twice, cannot be set, and will not be noticed for a year, and it **does not go in this chapter and no scene makes it whole.** He has slept in a room over a market with eleven other people since the twenty-eighth day of the flood and has not complained once, and the reason is his and it is forty-seven words: a man complained about a shoulder in the fifth week and a page got written about him and the page was right and he would rather be the second kind of man in this city than the first. He was put on paper work for five days by a man in a good coat without being told, found out on the fortieth day, and did not go to anybody. The rescue argument is had in about forty minutes and is over in about nine minutes, and both men know it and neither says so, and then they say the real thing for nine minutes and do not resolve it: Adrian asks whether Dante has been kept safe by being useful, and Dante asks why the only reason anybody wants him here is that he can carry a stretcher, and says that if that man goes the man who cannot is not a man but a mouth. **A woman of twenty-nine who has been in the room the whole time says both men are wrong in the same way, and it is hers: being useful is not safe and is not unsafe, it is a description.** Peth Lammay is made to answer for nine days of silence about a column with a man's name in it, and is right about four of five things and wrong about the fifth, and gets *she would do it again* onto the same line. And Adrian offers the one thing he has never offered anybody, which is to be asked, and is refused in four seconds, correctly, in twelve words and then forty-three: **if you ask me it stops being a thing I do and it becomes a thing I am a part of, and I would like to have done one thing that nobody was keeping.** The two of them stand on the walk-round at the ninth hour, neither opens anything, the dressed join is eight and seven-eighths, one ruled line is left and is not filled, and Dante says *that is a thing a man can stand next to.*

### Chapter 0067 — A Price on Good Faith
Day 67, the Friday, the forty-second day of the new month. **The batch's turn, and the batch's best idea and its worst news.** A woman of seventy said four words under an arch on the thirty-eighth day of the new month and nobody asked the office anything for four days, and then a licensed carrier sent the question out in a book with no rider and no seal, and the Registry at Aurel answers on the same evening, in the form required, in four lines: **the fee of eleven ounces of silver is discharged in full by the delivery of salt, at the office's own assay, taken out of the district's own production, at a standing rate the office will not review this quarter.** Sera Vail says out loud, in nine seconds, that it is the same instrument and not a fifth — the first time in five weeks she has said that something from that office is the same document and not a new one — and the rate is generous, and the generosity is the finding. **A Crown office that will take salt for silver is a Crown office that wants salt, and Saltwake is the only place in four hundred miles with salt to sell in this month, and a district that can pay a fee in the only thing it has is a district whose good faith has a price on it and a buyer.** Ottavia Reek says the half of the truth that is bad for her and asks somebody to notice; Renna Ord says the rule she has used four times does not fit this and that a thing with no rule on it is the thing a person is proud of and also the thing that gets used; and Nyla Ferrow says the four words that the price on that board is now a price in two currencies. **The twenty-first field arrives on a page that is otherwise about a fee and is the best of the twenty-one: the district may be described as a district that pays in kind and will not be charged a fee again. It puts nothing at all where a cost goes. It is an offer to describe a place.** It is refused in under a second in three parts, the third of which is that a thing an office knows it can make a district do is a mechanism and not a favour and not a courtesy. The salt is not sent and the finding stands anyway, because the office now knows what this district has and this district now knows what the office will take, and neither is on any page the office has sent. **Renner Sallow says the sentence the volume's politics are built on, and it is thirty words long, and Adrian Vale asks for it to be written in a clerk's hand and not in his own.**

### Chapter 0068 — The Questions
Day 68, the Saturday, the forty-third day of the new month. **The questions for the review get written, and the four drafts that came first are on the page and struck out with the striker's name against them.** The first asks whether the door is working and is struck out by the man who wrote it. The second asks whether the door should be closed and has an answer in it before anybody opens their mouth. The third asks who is responsible and is stopped in four words because there is no person in the question. **The fourth asks whether the route has opened twice in sixty-eight days, and about nine people go quiet, because it is the correct question and it is a count.** The room then works out, in about forty minutes, that a review's questions are not a list of subjects but a decision about what the answer is allowed to be, and that the person who writes them has done something to the door that no amount of daylight undoes. Sera Vail gives the finding in nine words — *I would not sign a page I cannot read* — and then the count: **four of the drafted questions are questions a city asks when it wants to keep a door and six are questions a city asks when it wants to be able to close one, and the difference is not in the tense and is not in the mood and is in who is able to answer.** Hettis Roke supplies the question nobody else thought of, *who in this city is not in this room*, and the room then spends forty minutes failing to answer it. **Eleven questions go on a page with a date, and a second page headed *what we are not asking and why* goes on with nineteen reasons in nineteen hands and is entered as part of the instrument and not as an annexe, and both go to Aurel, and Vail chose the longer answer for them and against herself in nine seconds.** Adrian's is the eleventh and it is *What is a person with no work for?* and it is eight words and it is the only question on either page about a person rather than a door. Nyla Ferrow reads the whole thing out twice in about two hundred and forty people, in the wrong order, on purpose, so that the second page is what people remember.

### Chapter 0069 — The Third Refusal
Day 69, the Sunday, the forty-fourth day of the new month. **The ninth household of Nine Elms refuses for the third time and this time the refusal costs them nothing, because a Crown factor of the reed levy has made it affordable first, quietly, in a form with a column four lines high, by using the Crown's own fifth clause *correctly*.** The form advances the coming quarter's levy against the district's own reeds, without interest, without security other than the goods, and the reason given is that a refusal which costs other people money is a decision about other people and the office is required to take that into account before it treats a refusal as a refusal. He did not bribe anybody, he did not threaten anybody, he did not break a rule, and he entered that it took him four days to find out whether he was allowed to. **Ilda Marsh, seventy, stands up in her own house and says the twenty-five words this chapter exists for: a favour you cannot refuse is not a favour, and we have taken one, and we are going to be asked about it in March.** Hessa Ruun reads the page in a reed channel and gives the warning she gave on the twenty-third day of the new month, and it has come true in twenty-two days, and she says the half nobody says: when a thing is affordable the people who cannot afford it look like the people who do not care, and that is how a quarter of a Reach goes, not by a door and not by a flood, by a page. **And then a woman of twenty-four says nine sentences, and the eighth of them is that a form written by a city that has decided to be unhelpful and a form written by a city that has nothing look the same on the page, and the honest useless form has put a Crown officer in a village in four days, and she is proud of it and afraid of it in that order and has now said both twice.** The credit is refused, the quarter is gone, Ilda Marsh's nine sentences go on the page in the plain words of a woman who has lived in it, a Crown officer enters that he was the one who made it affordable, and at the fifth hour of the evening Marrow asks Adrian Vale, in nine words, for the first time in two volumes, what he wants him to be useful for — and Adrian does not get a clever answer, and Marrow says that he had been useful for a month and it got him nothing and he did not notice.

### Chapter 0070 — The Slate
Day 70, the Monday, the forty-fifth day of the new month. **The slate goes on the nail and the reading happens in daylight in about two hundred and forty people, five days before the review, and nothing is resolved and the batch does not stage the review.** A slate and not a page, because a page is in a book and a book goes in a case and a slate is a thing you can read standing up in the rain. Three of the eleven questions are read out twice, and the third one is admitted on the slate to be a trap, and the notary who drafted it says in nine words that a notary who writes a fair question and holds an opinion about the answer is the exact mechanism this volume is about and that she caught herself in four seconds. Hettis Roke asks *who in this city is not in this room* out loud a second time and the room has to answer it in daylight in nine minutes, and the answer is a list of about nine names, **two of which are a girl of sixteen and a woman of thirty-eight who are on no roll at all and who have not been asked anything this volume, and about two hundred and forty people discover that the review is going to be argued about by people who were not in the room, and nobody thought of that on the thirty-eighth day.** **The eleventh question is four words long, is on the back of the slate, and is written by a woman of fifty-eight who cannot read, with her own hand over a girl's fingers as a rest: *what are they called.*** It is about the people the door costs, and the moment a review answers it the licence is lost, because a description is a removal with a column for where the person is. She reads it back, does not like it, and says it stays, because a question that is visible can be argued with. Adrian says the thing he should have said on the twenty-ninth and has written down that he has not fixed it because a thing fixed before it is asked is the thing the volume has been about for seventy days. Tamsin Quill, on the walk-round, says the thing nobody has said better: a city is not a room. And Dante Kuo is put on the nineteenth line, and Adrian watches and writes down that this is what he does instead of asking.
