Day 119, the Monday, the twenty-ninth day of the month after this one. **THE VOLUME'S REVERSAL, AND IT IS NOT A VICTORY.** A woman of seventy of Nine Elms carries a boundary page of her village into a room over a market in a fold of oilcloth and gives it to a girl of seventeen and not to anybody else, and says she refused a fourth time seven days ago and has had a thing in the bottom of a chest since she was a girl. **A man of forty-one in a very good coat cannot read it and says so: it is a district form and not a book and he is not going to guess at it in a room.** Nobody can read it but a man of thirty-two, because what nobody can do is the form — it is not a sentence, it is a place where things go, and he is the only person in *that room* who has had to learn to tell what a person said from what a page said about them, two lines below a man who has read every book that office keeps. **He reads it in about nine minutes and nobody improves on it and it is entered as what a person said and not as a finding.** The form has one place for the count and one place for the assent and they are the same place, and the word in it is the word for a count, so the village's agreement is a number of men having been taken; there are eight names at the foot of it and **he does not read the eight**; the ninth line is the hand of a man whose name is on the fifth thing that office put in a case in the fifty-second day of the new month. **A translation is a description; a description is a removal with a column for where the person is; the column is him** — and he says in four minutes that he is not going to be able to stop, and that reading is the whole of what he can do here, and that it is not a neutral thing to be able to do. A notary of fifty then says the part nobody can carry, and **the three shapes stay apart on the page as Chapter 0116 kept them apart: the third instrument of the seventeenth day of the new month, a single-author containment, refused in a yard; the second answer of the twenty-second, which is that office's answer to it, in a form that is not the form required; and the instrument of the twenty-second, which was answered ninety times with statements of error.** Forty-one of the ninety were entered by people who could not afford the refusal, which is the fifth clause of the notice of the twenty-eighth day of this flood, so it stands answered forty-nine times and unanswered by ninety.

### Chapter 0120 — A Slate on a Bench
Day 120, the Tuesday, the thirtieth day of the month after this one, the hundred and twentieth day of the flood. **THE CLOSE OF THE BATCH.** A hearing is in thirty-four days and there is one hearing and one clock, and **about nine people are going to be asked to be in a room in a building nine hundred miles away and there is no road in this world that reaches it.** **The season of ninety-one acres out of a hundred and forty is settled in for the second time in thirty-five days, and the chapter is explicit about the first.** The first was on the first of March, which was the eighty-fifth day of the flood, at a gateway four miles down the Reach road at about the eighth hour of the morning, in daylight, in the local language, **by the woman the field is on**, and a number was said once and then not said again. This time it is read off the bottom of a board by a man with a stall and settled in **by the rule drawn under the eleventh line of a cost column, by nobody**, and the entry says who settled it in and what it cost and that nobody was asked, and the person the field is on was standing in the room the whole time and was asked nothing — five words, a figure and a decision, and she says nothing else for the rest of the day and does not turn round. **A man of thirty-two enters the difference and it is the finding: the first was a person and could be asked whether she would have it, this one is a rule and cannot be asked anything, and the rule under the eleventh line was ruled and empty on the first of March as well.** A woman of twenty-four says it is the second time in a hundred and twenty days that this city has settled something about a person standing in the room, and that she is not counting the first of March, because on that day she settled it in herself, and that a rule cannot be refused, and that she does not know whether that is because the rules are right or because the rules are not people. A notary of fifty's entry that she would like to be asked before the box is opened has been standing in a book since the twenty-sixth day of the month after this one and she has been asked about it four times in four days and has answered none of the four, because she does not know what her answer is. Then everything is gone through out loud, and **the last image is a slate face down on a bench with nothing on the other side of it and the six words on the back of another of his three, the six words are not the twenty-eight, nobody turned either of them over, the case is upstairs with its strap still untied, and the stone in the arch has said nothing in a hundred and twenty days and has never said a word.**

### Chapter 0121 — Standing Near a Thing
Day 121, the Monday, the thirty-first day of the month after this one, the hundred and twenty-first day of the flood. **THE BOX HAS BEEN UPSTAIRS FOR FIVE DAYS AND A HEARING IS IN THIRTY-THREE DAYS.** The room with a door is on the second floor of a building over a market and the stair to it goes past a hall where nine people eat, and about four people have worked out that this is why there have been nine people on that stair and not three. **Somebody put a chair on the landing outside that door on the second day and nobody asked for it and nobody has taken it away, and it has no price and no date and no right of refusal and no page under it.** A man of thirty-two asked for a date on it and a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf refused in about four seconds: a date on the chair is a page, and a page can be taken down on a date, and then the chair is a chair nobody can ask anything about, and a chair is the only thing in this city that is allowed to be nothing. **The finding is that a thing nobody is waiting for is a thing everybody is standing near, that standing near a thing is a practice, and that a practice is a toll — because every person who stands near the box adds a line to the only account of it that will exist, and that account is a nineteen-line page of which three lines are about the box and sixteen are about nine people.** A docker of fifty-eight who cannot read said she is not going up that stair again, because a thing she cannot read about herself is a thing about somebody else. A man of thirty-two came up, stood near it for nine minutes, and did not read the writing round the edge of the wax for the third time in six days. **A notary of fifty's fifth asking was written out on a slate and not delivered, and the reason is that a person who has entered that she does not know has a thing to protect, and nobody has asked her a sixth time.** The last image is a chair with no date on it and a strap still untied four feet inside a door.

### Chapter 0122 — Two Things Nobody Did
Day 122, the Tuesday, the thirty-second day of the month after this one. **A FOREMAN HAS NOT BEEN TOLD ANYTHING AND THE FINDING IS THAT THE NOT-TELLING IS A PAGE.** Thirty-nine of forty men turn up under the near gate, unpaid since the first of March, which was the eighty-fifth day of this flood, and that is thirty-seven days, and a foreman who has said four words under that gate every working day since the seventy-first day of this flood has not come for eight days. A man of about forty-four who keeps a stall proposes that somebody carry him a word and is refused in about nineteen seconds: **a word is a thing that travels along a road, and what it says when it gets there is that we know he has not come, and that is a sentence about a man, and a sentence about a man is a description, and a description is a removal with a column for where the person is.** The decision not to send it goes on a slate with a date on it and no name on it, and it is the first page anybody in this city has written about itself; a docker of fifty-eight who cannot read had it read out to her and said eleven words, and nobody checked the count. Then nine miles up the road, a page in a stone is found to be wrong: **the second of its three pages is nineteen lines and the line beside the name of a woman of forty-four of Corrow says she is owed a figure and a decision about ninety-one acres, and a rule under the eleventh line of a cost column gave her both on the thirtieth day of the month after this one without asking her, and a page cannot be told the difference between a person paying a debt and a rule settling it in, and the only correction available in four hundred miles is to ask the keeping household to take the page down.** Nobody has ever asked that household anything. **A woman of about thirty-four said out loud that nine inches of stone is not a safe place for anything, and had it written down, and a woman of about sixty-one of Four Elms said that asking a household what it thinks is how you find out it can say no, and that this city would rather have a page it can never check.**

### Chapter 0123 — A Name Goes On Something
Day 123, the Wednesday, the thirty-third day of the month after this one. **A NAME GOES ON A LOCAL PAGE FOR THE FIRST TIME IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-THREE DAYS, AND THE PERSON WHO PUTS IT THERE IS THE PERSON IT BELONGS TO.** A girl of seventeen, who is on no roll, writes her own name at the top of a nineteen-line page headed *the box as received* in about four seconds, in a room over a market, in daylight, with about nine people in it. Nobody asked her to. **She named the cost herself: a page with nobody at the top of it cannot be wrong by being looked at and that is the safest page this city has built, and she has just given that up, and a page a person can be angry at is also a page that can be used against the person.** A woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf is glad about it and says so and admits she has been asked four times in a hundred and twenty-three days to put a name on something and has said no three times. **A man of thirty-two is asked to read the page out loud in the local language and says no in about four seconds, because a page with a girl's name at the top of it does not get his voice on it, and on the twenty-ninth day of the month after this one a page turned out to be a description of a district and the column was him.** **She then reads her own page out herself, badly, in about four minutes, and nobody corrects her and nobody reads it after her,** and when a woman of about thirty-four asks *what is this about* she answers it in about four seconds, about herself and not about the box, and nobody counts it. A notary of fifty, not in the room, entered three days later that she had noticed.

### Chapter 0124 — Two Empty Places
Day 124, the Thursday, the thirty-fourth day of the month after this one. **THE TWENTY-FOURTH DAY WAS THE SIXTH DISAGREEMENT AND A MAN WHO DID NOT COME, AND THIS DAY LOOKS AT BOTH.** The four go down two hundred steps of a river wall on a condemned rail in the wrong weather; the twenty-fifth reading is not taken for the thirty-ninth day; all six disagreements stand and Peth Lomas is still under five times out of six. **A man of thirty-one named Teodor Blaz, of the second list, whose right hand has not made a fist since the twenty-fifth day of this flood, comes down with a gauge in the crook of his arm because he cannot hold it in a hand, and he had not been up there before, and he said why: a man of fifty said in a yard nine days ago that he would not read the twenty-fifth alone, and a man of fifty who says that is a man expecting to be asked for help by somebody he has never asked.** He said that a man who cannot hold a thing is still the man holding it, and that the reason nobody has ever asked him about that is that he is not a page. **A man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board then asks out loud, in a room, on a day he did not choose, whether the four can make a rule about a man, and the man of thirty-one answers I do not know without being asked, and a girl of seventeen does not count the four words because nobody counts them any more.** **A Hearthguard of forty-four of the outer ward stood on the landing for about forty minutes in the wrong weather and was not asked anything by anybody, and it is the fourteenth day in a row, and nobody in that place said so to him.** The finding: an empty ruled line and a missing man are the same shape, this city has spent thirty-nine days protecting one of them and has no rule at all for the other, and the difference is that one of them has a person standing next to it.

### Chapter 0125 — An Asking Is a Kitchen
Day 125, the Friday, the thirty-fifth day of the month after this one. **A HEARING IS IN TWENTY-NINE DAYS AND NOBODY IN THIS CITY HAS AN INSTRUMENT FOR ASKING NINE PEOPLE WHETHER THEY WILL GO.** About nine people spend four hours in a room over a market and find, inside about nine minutes, that **nine answers is a list, and a list of who will stand in a room is a removal with a column for where the person is, and a column of nine people who will not come is the same column with the other word in it.** **The two people in this city who could carry an answer anywhere are the two people who may not ask, and both of them work that out about themselves and neither worked it out first.** A man of thirty-two says he has not asked one person one question in a hundred and twenty-nine days and asks for that and the reason to be entered: a person on no roster who asks nine households a question is a person who is asking, and asking is what a chain is made of, and he is the only person here who can read two systems of writing, so any answer arrives in two at once and he is standing at the junction. **A girl of seventeen refuses in under a second to be the one who goes — a woman with nine answers and no roll is a carrier, and a case with a name in it can be carried — and then goes to her mother's kitchen anyway.** A decision that nobody will be asked to ask goes on a slate with a date on it and no name and no list, and a keeper of about seventy who has kept a floor for fifty-one years is not asked anything and the reason for that is entered in the correct words. **The finding: a hearing is a room and an asking is a kitchen, and the only instrument this city has is a room, and a room is the wrong shape for an asking because nine people hearing an answer is eight other people hearing an answer.**

### Chapter 0126 — Nine Rooms
Day 126, the Saturday, the thirty-sixth day of the month after this one. **A BOY OF NINE'S ANSWER IS EIGHT DAYS OLD AND THE QUESTION IS SEVENTY-SIX DAYS OLD, AND THE SIXTY-EIGHT IS THE NUMBER EVERYBODY KNOWS AND THE EIGHT IS THE NUMBER NOBODY IS KEEPING.** Under a wall above a market in the wrong weather, a woman of twenty-four and a docker of fifty-eight who cannot read find out in about nineteen seconds that they had both been saying the same two words to other people in this city. **The finding is that a name is a thing two people have said in one room, and the two words are in about nine rooms and none of those nine rooms has met any of the other eight, and a sentence about arriving is not a Crown name and is in no book that office keeps, and the man who could have found it checked in about four seconds on the day the boy said it and has not volunteered since.** A man of thirty-two says he is not going to say his answer to a boy's six words again, because a thing said twice in a room has an account, and **the finding is that the answer is on a slate because there is nowhere else and not because it is safe: a hearing is a room and a room is gone in a day, a slate goes in a book that travels, a stone nine miles up a road cannot be read, and four ears is not a record and is not nothing either.** The last beat: a woman of about thirty-four asks the boy five words — *has anybody read it back* — and he says one word and then nothing else, and nobody in that place said anything to him about having been asked.

### Chapter 0127 — The Sixth Room
Day 127, the Sunday, the thirty-seventh day of the month after this one. **THE SIXTH ROOM, HELD, AND FAILED, AND THE THIRTY-FIRST THING THE WALL HAS SAID, AND THE BATCH'S REFUSAL.** About nine people sit for four hours in a room at the back of the Open Hand that has no key and no owner, which is the reason they were allowed four hours and is also the reason they got nothing. Six attempts; the first five are the ones from the hundred and second day of the flood, and **the sixth is *who wanted it to work*, and the answer is that nobody in this city ever has, and a man of thirty-two says in about nine minutes that a description of a thing is made by people who want the thing to be different from what it is, and that not one of the nine people in that room ever wanted the page to have not worked, and that this is why five rooms and then a sixth have got nowhere, and that it is not because the page is hard — they are the wrong shape for the job.** He is asked who the right shape is and says **I do not know**, and enters that this is the first time in a hundred and twenty-nine days he has said that about himself, and that he is not going to look for one. The sentence that came closest was not written down. **At about the fourth hour and nine minutes of the afternoon the thirty-first thing the wall has said comes up, offering to make one person the account of the page for good, and a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf says no in about four seconds, and names the cost: she has said out loud in front of nine people that she knows what a page that ends a document is, and a person who knows that is a column, and saying a thing out loud in a room cannot be put back in a drawer.** The count of refusals in a clerk of the Registry's second book goes from twenty-three to twenty-four, the count at Aurel is thirteen, and the difference is eleven. A seventh room was wanted and not held. The count of rooms on the page is six.

### Chapter 0128 — What a Person's Fourth Refusal Is
Day 128, the Monday, the thirty-eighth day of the month after this one. **A VILLAGE'S FOURTH REFUSAL IS SIXTEEN DAYS OLD AND UNEXAMINED, AND IT IS ASKED PROPERLY, IN A KITCHEN, IN DAYLIGHT, ON A DAY THE PERSON DID NOT CHOOSE.** A woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf walks four miles up the Reach road to a village of nine households and asks a woman of seventy, and says before anybody has got their coats off that she has been the person who says the hard thing for a hundred and twenty-eight days and has never been asked whether she wanted to be that. **The answer: the first three were not hers; three times the village said no and the no belonged to nine households, and she has been thinking about the other half of that for sixteen days and nobody has asked her, and she has never been asked what the first three were and has never asked any of the eight other houses. The first three were refusals of a document — a notice, a date, a channel, a term sheet — and the no went into the same thing as the notice. The fourth is a refusal of a person, and there is no document, and there is a mark in chalk on nine doors that nobody in this city can wash off and nobody can explain. It is the strongest one and it is held up by luck, and a refusal with a document under it can be un-refused by a person who reads the document, and a refusal with nothing under it can be un-refused by anybody who never heard it, and in about two months there is going to be a piece of paper in a building nine hundred miles away that says the village agreed to something and nothing anywhere that says she said no. That is not a protection. That is a woman of seventy and a mark on a door.** She asks to be put on something that is not a document. A man of thirty-two reads the date off the second door out loud, four feet back, and says nothing about it. Nobody went to a fourth house and nobody stood outside one, and the chapter names the five people at that village who are not one.

### Chapter 0129 — A Woman Who Keeps a Register
Day 129, the Tuesday, the thirty-ninth day of the month after this one. **TAMSIN QUILL STANDS IN A ROOM FOR THE FIRST TIME IN SEVENTY-NINE DAYS, AND THE TENTH SHAPE IS NAMED AND DATED.** In a converted salt store, in front of about forty people, in daylight, on a day nobody chose, nobody sent for her. She brings the eleven-line table she has carried in a coat for seven years and **does not read it out**, and gives the reason, and says the top of line one once: line one is the act in the heading of the boundary page a woman of seventy carried into a room eight days before, and its cost column says the bottom field failed in that year and four hundred people were rehoused, and five of the eleven lines are places where a number went in, and that is why it is in a coat. **And she says the thing she has been not saying for eight days, which is not about a man: she is on a roll, her name is on the front of the public register in the second line, she was not offered the page and was not in the room, and both of those were correct, and the finding is not that anybody was rude to her — it is that she has been holding a document about that act out of a book for seven years on purpose, and that is the single most consequential thing she has done in seven years, and it is why a woman of seventy had to carry a page down a road in a cart herself.** She refuses to be forgiven a thing nobody accused her of and says that a speech about somebody is a thing she does not do. A man of forty-one in a very good coat says the one thing nobody else in the room can say: his office has no name for a document that is in a room and not in a case, and he has spent a hundred and twenty-nine days trying to find a way to act on one and has not found it, and that is not modesty. Nobody asked him to explain either name, and a number was written in a book in his own hand and dated and not read out. **A woman of about thirty-four begins to put the fifth clause of the notice of the twenty-eighth day of this flood next to a cost column in that table, and a notary of fifty puts her hand flat on the table and stops it, and nobody in that room answers it.** At the seventh hour Tamsin Quill writes one line on the public roll with no name on it and reads it out.

### Chapter 0130 — Twenty-Four Days
Day 130, the Wednesday, the fortieth day of the month after this one, the hundred and thirtieth day of the flood. **THE CLOSE OF THE BATCH, AND A HEARING IS IN TWENTY-FOUR DAYS.** About nine people go through what there is out loud, and a woman of twenty-four reads out the last ten days as a list with no names on it — a page in a stone that is wrong, two decisions not to do something on slates, a sentence about nine inches of stone, a name at the top of a page, six rooms, a fourth refusal with nothing under it, a tenth shape — and then reads out the other half of the list: **nine things this city has that are not anywhere, and every one of them is a person or a person's sentence, and there is no instrument in four hundred miles that any of them can be put into, and that is not because the instrument has not been built yet, it is because the instrument would be a document.** A boy of nine has been to nine kitchens and nothing has been written down. A Hearthguard of forty-four has gone twenty days without anybody asking him about his own eight and that is the correct number of times. **The last image is a slate on a bench in a room over a market with a child's handwriting on the back of it and nothing at all on the front, and the twenty-eight words are face down on a different one of his three, and the third is in the pocket of a docker of fifty-eight who cannot read, and nobody turned any of the three over, and the case is upstairs, and the wall has said nothing in a hundred and thirty days.**

### Chapter 0131 — A Compact Is a Document
Day 131, the Saturday, the forty-first day of the month after this one, the hundred and thirty-first day of the flood, in a room over a market. **The batch opens on the narrowing: the thing this city has been calling a compact for four days is a piece of paper, and a piece of paper has to go somewhere, and there is nowhere in four hundred miles it can go to in twenty-three days.** A case is eighteen days each way and the office is nine hundred miles away and no road in this world reaches the building. A woman of twenty-four says it; a man of about forty-four reads the arithmetic out loud and a girl of seventeen writes it in a column four lines high in his words. A man of forty-one in a very good coat says the room is writing it for the wrong end of it — you are not writing it for me, there is no version of this that is a page I can hold — and that the reader is not the problem, the *holding* is. A man of thirty-two says the volume's argument in the shape of a form: a compact is a document, a document is a description, a description is a removal with a column for where the person is, and there are two documents and only one of them is a person. A notary of fifty finds the thing the room has been standing in the middle of for four days: a document that lives in two places is not safer than one that lives in one, it has twice as many things for a person to be cross with. **The compact is named, dated and kept apart from all ten as the eleventh shape**, and the chapter's last line is the sentence that shapes the next nine days: a shape that travels stops being a description of this city and becomes a description of a district.

### Chapter 0132 — Four Days
Day 132, the Sunday, the forty-second day, on a quay in the wrong weather with the water against the third stone. **The arithmetic of a person who might be in the room on the sixty-fourth day, worked out from scratch and wrong in the batch prompt and right on the page: a case is eighteen days each way, so anybody who is going to be in that room got on the water between the forty-third and the forty-sixth of that month, and on the forty-second there are four days of window and nobody in this city can use any of them.** A woman of twenty-four says the whole of it: if a person is going to be in that room, this city did not send them and cannot unsend them and cannot ask them not to, and there is no instrument anywhere in four hundred miles for wanting a thing at a place. **This is the first thing in a hundred and thirty-two days that this city has wanted that it cannot get.** A docker of fifty-eight who cannot read says eleven words on the quay and then, after about four seconds, *that is a good thing to be* — and nobody writes either down. A man of sixty-eight who has carried nine cases in his life says he would carry a person and would not write the household. A carrier of the second table, twenty-four and eleven years in, refuses the Open Hand's carrying of a person in about four seconds and then says that the right of refusal cannot be bought out and that she is standing on it because the refusal is easy and the easy ones are the only ones worth having.

### Chapter 0133 — The Mistake a Compact Makes
Day 133, the Monday, the forty-third day, on the back of a shut stall by a wharf, out of the wind. **A compact with a person's name in every place in it is a description of a district, and it is just longer and it is signed.** A woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf and a girl of seventeen who put her own name at the top of a page ten days ago work this out between them in about nine minutes each, and the girl reaches the answer nobody had said: the count in one place and the assent in another, and they are not the same place, and nobody in four hundred miles has to be told that twice because there is one place in this world where a count and an assent are the same place and it is a page about a hundred and forty years old. **She says she has not read the eight and nobody is going to, and asks for that to be written down.** The woman then says what it has cost her to be the person who says the hard thing: a refusal with nothing under it can be un-refused by anybody who never heard it, and she has said no in about nine rooms at a time for a hundred and thirty-three days, and not one of those rooms has met any of the other hundred and thirty-two, and that is not a hundred and thirty-three protections, that is a hundred and thirty-three people telling somebody. And the chapter's last beat is that she asks for her own name to go on one line of a slate, and a girl of seventeen writes it, and it is the first name she has allowed in a hundred and thirty-three days, and the cost of it is hers and she names it in four seconds.

### Chapter 0134 — One Person, or a Number
Day 134, the Tuesday, the forty-fourth day, in a room over a market. **A man of sixty-one who has copied other men's sentences for nineteen years reads the four lines under the seal out loud for the first time in his life**, and he says that he has never once been asked, and that he has had a sentence of his own ready for two days in a road and never said it, and he asks for both to be entered and for it to be entered that they are the same shape. **A man of thirty-two was asked about the writing round the edge of the wax and said nothing at all, and the not-saying is the entry, and the chapter says so** — a form is not a face, said four times in seven days, and on the forty-fourth day nothing. A notary of fifty refuses an answer that four people had agreed she would give, in about four seconds, and the reason is that a supplied answer is a description of a person and she has been the description of about nine hundred people, and she asks for the relief to be entered as well, because relief is what a supplied answer feels like from the inside. **And the finding of the day is that that office asked for one person and this city has made a compact that asks for nine, and the two are not versions of one another and nobody in that room can put them in the same order.**

### Chapter 0135 — A Figure on the End of an Entry
Day 135, the Wednesday, the forty-fifth day, under the near gate in the wrong weather on a rail that has been condemned. Thirty-nine of forty men turn up, unpaid since the first of March, which is fifty days. **A man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall asks one man whether he will take a day, and says the shape of it out loud before the answer, and pays it out of a stall, and a figure goes on the end of a line with his hand and the date on a slate, and the man who is paid is not named.** A docker of fifty-eight who cannot read says nine words about it: you paid him and you signed it, and that is the two. He then says the reason, and it is the reason for the whole of the page: a person who is owed on a page is a person with somebody, and in about four months that somebody is going to be asked a question by somebody nine hundred miles away. The man who was paid refuses the thanking in about nine seconds and gives the reason: if he is thanked in front of thirty-eight men then in about a month every one of them knows, and in about two months one of them asks why, and there is no answer to that on a stair. **A woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf goes down nine steps she has not gone down in a hundred and thirty-five days and says the finding of the day: a person who carries a cost is a person who can be asked, about four people in this city have wanted to do that for a hundred and thirty-five days, and the reason nobody has is not that we worked it out, it is that we are frightened and we found a rule that says not to.**

### Chapter 0136 — A Refusal That Was Never Asked For
Day 136, the Thursday, the forty-sixth day, four miles up the Reach road in the wrong weather and then a room over a market. **A girl of seventeen walks back to the ninth of nine kitchens to say that she is not going to ask, and a woman at that door tells her that she has had a refusal ready since the fortieth day of this flood and that it is not one, because nobody ever asked her.** The finding is the day's: a refusal answers a question, a person who has not been asked anything has not refused, and a list of remarks is a list. **Nine kitchens in ten days produced nine remarks and no answers, and the number nine stands with nobody at the top of it, and a girl of seventeen says she would rather have a number nobody can check than a list anybody can be angry at, and says she has gone back and forth about it nine times and counts everything because counting is the only useful thing she has ever done and it has been in a hundred and thirty-six days of not being on a roll.** A man of thirty-two gives the third reason he may not ask, which is the new one: he would be asking, and then the answers would be his. And at about the two-mile mark, alone on the road, she said her eleven words out loud to nobody, to hear whether they were the right eleven words, and then said *a practice is a toll*, and there was nobody there, and that is the reason she said it.

### Chapter 0137 — A Room, on a Day He Did Not Choose
Day 137, the Friday, the forty-seventh day, in a room over a market in daylight. **THE VOLUME'S BEST UNPAID DEBT IS PAID, in full, in a room, with a date on it, on a day he did not choose.** A Hearthguard of forty-four of the outer ward had asked for this on the thirtieth day of the new month and had been waiting twenty-seven days. **The instrument for asking took four days to build and is the first instrument of its kind in this city: the question in his own words, given in writing to every person who would be in the room four days beforehand, and anybody who could not be given it was not going to be in the room; about nine chairs; a date on a slate; and nobody asked him whether the day suited him, because that would have been the first asking and it would have been on a day he chose.** He came off duty nineteen minutes late and nobody said anything about it. **He said no in about four seconds and it is the same no**, he will stand for about four years, he gave the same forty seconds in the same words, and he gave the reason for the difference himself: nine people in this room were each given the question four days ago and one of them can be asked whether the asking was proper, and nobody at the end of a yard can be. A man of thirty-two then says the thing the last fifty days have been about, with a person standing in it: **there are three eights in four hundred miles and a man who wants them to be one is a man who wants and not a man who knows**, and that the only thing he is prepared to say is that a number can be maintained for a hundred and forty years and names cannot. Nobody thanked him, he asked in advance not to be, and the count of twenty-seven days is not going to be counted again.

### Chapter 0138 — A Correct Number About a District
Day 138, the Saturday, the forty-eighth day, two things up the Reach road in one cart. **A receipt for the case of the eighth day, which is not a shape and is four lines: received at the fourth desk, carried, not closed, return by the quarter, no answer due before then, and *a case with an eighth column is not a case that can be closed.*** And a district flood return made by a waterboard in the ordinary course, **the twelfth shape, dated, and correct, and nobody asked for it.** A man of thirty-two reads it out in the local language in daylight because nobody else can, and says before he starts that he is going to be the column again and is going to do it anyway, and enters that he noticed the difference between being rescued and being asked and that it took him nine minutes to notice it. **The return is headed with the district, gives nine households, says the water is not received, says the fourth channel is shut, and has a line saying the district assented in the word they use when a number of men have been taken — so the count is nine and the assent is nine and they are the same place, and it is the same form as the ninth shape and it has been that form for a hundred and forty years, and somebody filled it in on the forty-fourth day of the month after this one without doing anything wrong.** A notary of fifty says the part nobody could carry: a correct number about a district is a district that is described correctly, and a page nobody can argue with is a page nobody can stop, **and the eight at the foot of a boundary page are not households and were never households, and there is no line in that return for a man who was taken, and there has not been one for a hundred and forty years, and that is not what has happened to them, that is where they are.**

### Chapter 0139 — The Thing That Is Asked, and It Is a Post
Day 139, the Sunday, the forty-ninth day, in a room over a market. **A printed appointment of an interpreter with authority without limit comes out of a cart that has been in a lane since Saturday morning, because a thing addressed to a person and not to a district does not get looked at.** It is the thirteenth shape, dated, Crown, kept apart from the other twelve, nine terms, of which the fifth is the only one anybody in the room wrote, and of which there are two eighths: the person appointed is not to be asked whether he accepts it, and a declining is not a refusal until it is read by the person appointed. **A man of forty-one in a very good coat says he is required to put it and is putting it, and asks for it to be entered that he put it after the man said no and not before.** A woman of about thirty-four refuses in about four seconds to say the name of the shape in that room and says about four people in this city know it and two of them are at that table; the man in the good coat confirms neither name and explains neither and says he has just declined to be useful in the one room in four hundred miles where he is the only person who could have been; **and a man of thirty-two says one of the two words out loud, once, in the local language, and does not explain it.** At about the fifth hour a pale field offers to make one person the reading of this district, and **a woman of twenty-nine who keeps a public register says no in about four seconds** and names the cost — her name is on the front of the public register in the second line and has been for about twenty years, and a thing said out loud in a room an office knows about is a thing that can go into a return. That is the third registered refusal and the count in the second book is twenty-five and the count at Aurel is thirteen and the difference is twelve. **And the finding of the day is that a post does not change the form, a post changes who fills in the places, and a man who is filling nine places is a column** — so the compact, which is a form, will exist whatever he does, and the only question is who will be in the places in it. He says he will answer it in a room, in daylight, in the local language, on a day with a date on it, and that everybody who has to live with it will hear him say it.

### Chapter 0140 — The Compact as a Shape
Day 140, the Monday, the fiftieth day, in a room over a market. **Nobody could draw the compact, and that is the first thing on the page and it took about nine minutes**, and a notary of fifty says she is not going to draw it and is going to sit at the end of the table and say nothing. **A woman of twenty-four draws it, and it is entered that she is the same person who has written down every decision this city has made since the second day of the flood and that nobody had noticed until the notary said it out loud.** Eleven lines, in her hand, with the date on it, not read out, not signed, and nothing in it travels before the hearing. **The second place is the count and the third place is the assent and they are not the same place, and a figure in the second is not an assent in the third** — the whole of the volume's argument as a shape, and a man of thirty-two says the difference between this form and the boundary page a woman of seventy carried in a cart is two lines, and that the cost of the two lines is that no office in nine hundred miles can check either of them. A notary of fifty says line five is the one that matters and it is a person and the person is the reason, and that in about four hundred years nobody has wanted one badly enough to draw it. A girl of seventeen is offered the reading and a man of thirty-two says no in about four seconds — **a page with a name at the top of it does not get his voice on it, and if he reads it out on the sixty-fourth then he is the account of it and nobody can say what nine people meant** — and then says there is no line on that page that lets him choose the reader, and a girl of seventeen says that somebody is going to have to stand up.

### Chapter 0141 — The Thing That Is Not Counted
Day 141, the Tuesday, the fifty-first day, in a room above a chandlery at about the eighth hour of the evening. **A seventh room, in a different place, and a seventh question, and a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf says in four seconds that it is the same room, because the key on the table is the same thing as no key and a shilling is the same thing as no owner.** The seventh thing they tried, which a girl of seventeen thought of and says is hers, is *what did the absence cost* — not how the page worked, what it was worth not having. It got about four minutes. The document that was ended was the undertaking of the Lantern Concord, which made about a hundred and forty people the permanent holder of the licence; **a notary of fifty put her hand flat on the table and said *there is no harm*, and a man of thirty-two corrected her in one line — there is harm, and the only evidence anybody in four hundred miles has of what that page did is what it cost, and about a hundred and forty people paid it, and putting them in a book to explain an object is a description of people.** **The count of rooms on the page is six and is not being made seven, because a count that moves is a score and this is not a score**, and about four people in that room wanted the page to have not worked and not one of them wanted it out loud. The one sentence that came closest was not written down, a girl of seventeen had a slate under her hand and did not put it on the table, and the rule she gives on the road afterwards is that a sentence about a page that works is a mechanism, and a diagram of the most dangerous object in this city is a worse thing to have in a hundred and forty-one days than nine minutes of nothing.

### Chapter 0142 — The Slate That Is Turned Over
Day 142, the Wednesday, the fifty-second day, nine miles up the upper road in the wrong weather with a barrow and a woman of fifty-eight who cannot read. **A boy of nine turns one of his own three slates over, without being asked and without any appearance of having decided to, and there is nothing on the other side, and he says *because the front is for other people*, and then asks for that to be written down and not written down, and both were entered.** The six words he asked on the sixth mile are on the back of that one in his own hand and the front has been blank since the day he wrote on it, and nobody had read the front because there is nothing to read. A woman of fifty-eight says nine words about it and they are the finding: **that is the only page in this city with an address on it.** They do not turn the third slate over, which is face down and has been since the twenty-eighth day of the month after this one, and a girl of seventeen gives the reason in a gateway nine minutes after they come down the road: two words in about nine rooms and none of those nine rooms has met any of the other eight, and that is the only reason they are not a name, and she read the answer out in one room on the twenty-eighth and she is not going to be the one who makes it two. And the boy says the last thing of the day: he has not looked at his nineteen shapes on the wall for about four days, and he has not looked because he does not want to see it there, and he wants that written down and not written down and he wants both.

### Chapter 0143 — Three Is a Shrine, and There Is No Word for Four
Day 143, the Thursday, the fifty-third day, at a fork nine miles up a road. **The keeping household of a stone is asked, for the first time in a hundred and forty-three days, whether it will hold a fourth page**, and the question is a woman's of, and the person who carries it is a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf, and she says if anybody tries to make it hers she will put the barrow down. The page does not name the village, because a household that knows which village cannot un-know it. **A woman of about sixty-one with a barrow says three pages in a stone is a shrine and one is a wall somebody kept and two is a mistake, and that she has been up that road two hours thinking about it and does not have a word for four, and what she has is a barrow.** She agrees — on the back of the stone, which a girl of seventeen proposes, because the back of a thing nobody in four hundred miles can take down is the only combination anybody in this city has built in a hundred and forty-three days and it took the two of them nine minutes and a barrow. **Three conditions, and the third is the finding of the day: the second page, which is wrong about a woman of forty-four of Corrow, is to be left wrong, because a stone that has had a page taken down is a stone that can be corrected and a stone that can be corrected is a stone that will be corrected by whoever comes next.** And what she says she can offer the woman of forty-four is that she will not take it down, she will not ask her, and she will have to look at it every time she comes up the road for the next forty years, and then refuses to say it twice. The fourth page goes in, the fourteenth shape, and it is the first page anybody in this city has built for the express purpose of not being able to be taken down.

### Chapter 0144 — What Is Revocable
Day 144, the Friday, the fifty-fourth day, four hundred yards of river wall in the wrong weather. **A man of forty-one in a very good coat goes through the eleven lines of the compact, in order, and says which four his office can act on and which six it cannot and which one of the six it wants, and it took about forty minutes and it is the most useful thing anybody from that office has done in a hundred and forty-four days.** Lines two, three, seven and nine: a count, an assent, a household, an ending — four places in a form, all four of them in use for about four hundred years, all four correct, and not one of them a person. Line five is the one he cannot act on, and the reason is that his office revokes things and does not receive things that stop by themselves, and the form for an ending has a compulsory reason in it, and he has been the man who fills in the page about a person for nineteen days. **He did not draft the compact and said why, and asked for it to be entered in advance that he would lie about having never seen it.** And a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf closes the day in nine words that nobody counted: *then the guest who asks to stop is the only person in it.* The Hattie Park counter is written once in a book on a trestle in his own hand, dated, and not read out, and a woman of twenty-four said the word number and not the figure and was right.

### Chapter 0145 — A Delegation Is a Number With Legs
Day 145, the Saturday, the fifty-fifth day, in a room over a market. **A woman of twenty-nine who keeps a public register puts the First Landing on the page as three things kept apart, and the three are: one line in the register of public business of a town four hundred miles inland, in a hand about three centuries old, about a market, with salt in it; the Bright Pilgrims, and why there are three versions of where they came from in about nine rooms and nobody has ever asked; and ground with stone on it that nobody in this city has been to and which is not on the eight, the nine, the ten, or the table in her coat.** A man of thirty-two says the finding: a delegation is a list, and a list of four people going four hundred miles is the same page with a longer column and a worse place to be found in, and what you can send is a number with legs. **A man of about thirty-nine who trades on the board says *I will go* and then, in the same breath, that he will not be named, and asks for the second half to be written down and not the first.** A man of thirty-two says he cannot go and gives the reason: he holds no mark and no workway and no title and no seat and there is nobody who can carry him there and bring him back, and if he were going he would be the only man in four hundred miles who could say what a thing in the ground is and he would be the account of it before anybody else had stood on it, and a delegation with him in it is a description. And the delegation is named as four lines, of which **to open nothing is the clause the woman of twenty-nine put in herself and asked to be kept as hers, because in about four months somebody is going to say a delegation was sent to open something and she did not write that.**

### Chapter 0146 — Nobody Is Asked a Second Time
Day 146, the Sunday, the fifty-sixth day, in a kitchen over a wharf, which is the best room in this city and is not a room. **The best argument in the batch is made and lost.** A man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall proposes that the nine people a man of thirty-two named on the forty-ninth day should be asked properly, in a room, on a day they did not choose, so that the city knows who will be standing there when he refuses the post. A girl of seventeen says no, in about four minutes: asking them would make nine remarks into nine answers, and nine answers is a list, and a list of who will stand in a room is a removal with a column for where the person is. A man of about forty-four then says the other half and it is the cost: **if the number is eight on the day, nobody will ever be able to say who was not in the room, and this city has chosen that on purpose, in a room, with a date on it** — and he adds that he has noticed that not knowing is a preference, and that this is the first time in a hundred and forty-six days he has caught himself having one. **A girl of seventeen says she will stand up, and nobody writes it down, and she says that was why she said it, and then says the sentence of the chapter: she will stand up and say she was not asked, and if that is a description of her then it is one she chose in a kitchen, and she knows exactly what a name at the top of a page is, and she cannot decide which of the two things it is going to be, and she has decided to find out in a room rather than in a book.** The decision not to ask is on a slate with a date and no name and no list, and it is the fourth page anybody in this city has written about itself.

### Chapter 0147 — Nine Kitchens, and Two Days
Days 147 and 148, the fifty-seventh and fifty-eighth days of the month after this one, the hundred and forty-seventh and hundred and forty-eighth days of the flood, a Monday and a Tuesday. **A girl of seventeen goes to nine kitchens over two days and tells nine households four things about a room and not a fifth, and she knows a fifth thing and will not say it, and the reason is that in about four days about four of them would be in a room with a fifth person in it and she has had that once already in a hundred and forty-seven days and did not enjoy it and neither did the girl who had her own page read out badly.** The finding of the two days is a difference of eight days: the ninth household was not told until the Sunday because she could not get there on the Saturday without going past it, and she says out loud that that is not a reason she would put on a page and that she is telling them anyway because about four of them worked out something on the fortieth day of the month after this one about this city not putting its rooms together. A woman of about thirty-four at the fourth kitchen asks whether there is salt in it and a girl of seventeen says she does not know, four times in two days, and that everybody who has asked it has meant something else by it. **On the Tuesday about nine people go up a stair and stand outside a room with a door and nobody goes in, and the chair is still on the landing, and a girl of seventeen decides that she is not going to move it and is not going to be the reason it is moved**, and the fifth asking of the notary is still a slate written on the thirty-first day of the month after this one and not delivered, and it is twenty-six days since and then twenty-seven, and nobody has asked her a sixth time in either of them.

### Chapter 0148 — The Room Is Built
Days 149 and 150, the fifty-ninth and sixtieth days of the month after this one, the hundred and forty-ninth and hundred and fiftieth days of the flood, a Wednesday and a Thursday. **A hearing is a room and the room is built in about eleven hours across two days by about nine people, and it is the first thing anybody in this city has built that is meant to be sat in by somebody who is not from this city, and a building is a shape and the only shape in four hundred miles that is not a page.** The first thing they do is take a table out, and the argument about it took two days, and a man of thirty-two's sentence is the chapter: **a table is a page you can sit at, and a page is a thing a person is described on, and a table is a page a person is described at.** The table goes down the stair and stands in the hall, and a man of about thirty-four says it had been a table for about forty years and a form for about a hundred and forty-nine days and nobody had noticed. A shutter comes off in about four hours of argument with a bar and a boy of nine who wanted to hold the other end of it and did not. **The bench becomes a step**, because a woman of about thirty-four refuses in about four seconds to let a man from that office be given a seat facing the door, and a man of about forty-four who keeps a floor says *then it is not a bench, it is a step.* **And nothing goes on the walls, on the ground that a wall in a room is the cheapest thing in this city and there is already one** — and the date goes on the door instead, because a door is not a page. A keeper of about seventy who has kept a floor for fifty-one years is sent for on the second day and does not come, and nobody sends a second message. The finding is that about two hundred and forty people can hear out of a room, a hall, a landing and a market, **and that this city already knew how to have two hundred and forty people hear a thing in a building and has known it for a hundred and forty-nine days, and it is two rooms and not one.**

### Chapter 0149 — A Person Comes Up the River
Days 151 and 152, the sixty-first and sixty-second days of the month after this one, the hundred and fifty-first and hundred and fifty-second days of the flood, a Friday and a Saturday. **A woman of about thirty-six comes up this river in a passenger place and steps off at a quay in front of about nine people who had gone down to look at the water, and she is the second thing in a hundred and forty years to come up this river and the first one that is a person.** She is a clerk of the fourth desk, eleven years in the post, the ninth person to have had that desk and the fourth to have stayed on it, and she is not the office, and she says that on the quay. **She came because a case with a question in its eighth column has been on her desk for forty-three days and she may neither ask a question about it nor refuse one, and the only thing she is allowed to do is go and look**, and she says that in about four minutes to a room of about nine people, and nobody said anything for about nineteen seconds. A man of forty-one in a very good coat says that the receipt on the wall is his and that if he had known it was on her desk he would have said something, and that this is the first time in a hundred and fifty-one days he has said a thing to a person rather than to a room. **Nobody in this city asks her about the eighth column, and she notices, and she asks for that to be entered about four times because she does not think anybody in the room did.** A man of thirty-two says why in about four seconds: a person named in a question is not told the question exists, and if this city asks, the asking is the account. **And the man in the good coat says he is going to say a thing out loud in a room, in daylight, in the local language, with a date on it, and that it is worth nothing — that his office has no name for a document that is in a room and not in a case, and there is nothing on any form for a thing that was written down in front of nine people and stayed in the room, and he has been trying for nineteen days to find a way to act on one and has not — and the clerk of the fourth desk says no, and he does not argue, and she says she is not being asked, and a man of thirty-two tells her she is about to be so that it is not done to her in the next two days.** She asks for the asking to be in a room and not in a hall, and says she will not be thanked and will not improve on the answer, and that if the answer is no then none of the three of them gets to decide that in advance.

### Chapter 0150 — A Room, in Daylight, with a Date On It
Days 153 and 154, the sixty-third and sixty-fourth days of the month after this one, the hundred and fifty-third and hundred and fifty-fourth days of the flood, a Sunday and a Monday. **THE HEARING, THE VOLUME'S CLIMAX, ITS RESOLUTION AND ITS QUESTION.** The refusal comes first. A man of thirty-two, Stage 2 and Passage holder and in the doing, unbound, barred from every roster at his own request, holding no mark and no workway and no title and no land right and no class privilege and no seat, with no office in Saltwake for what he is, is asked the question in the local language and answers it in the local language in about nine minutes, **and the refusal did not produce the compact, and he says so on the page: the compact was on a slate on the fiftieth day of the month after this one and the post was on a table on the forty-ninth, and if he had said yes the compact would be exactly the eleven lines it is with his voice in it instead of hers.** He reads his own declination out because there is no one else who can, and the declination carries the second eighth clause. The reason is given once and not improved on. He then says what it costs, all of it, in front of about two hundred and forty people: the only thing he is good at as a way of being needed; no office, no standing, no roster, no mark, no seat; the knowing does not come back and the way home goes through a room with no door and neither was coming back whether he said yes or no; and the answer to the eighth column will be read by somebody else or by nobody, and he chose that in a room in daylight in the local language, and nobody is going to thank him for it and he asked for that to be entered before it happened — **and he says out loud that a man with no mark, on no roster, outside every office, is the most findable person in this world, and that this is not a safety.** A man of forty-one in a very good coat then offers the honest version — one reading, one document, one day, no standing, no roster, no seat, no wage — and is refused in about four seconds for the same reason, **because a thing that cannot be argued with because it is a day is a chair with no date on it**, and the man in the good coat says the reason back, *it is a day*, and does not argue. **A woman of twenty-nine who keeps a public register stands up, nobody asked her, there was no line that let anybody choose, and she says the reason out loud: a compact nobody reads is a piece of paper, and she has kept one out of a book for seven years, and a piece of paper is a door** — and she reads the eleven lines in the local language in daylight with the light off the west window and up the wall, and a girl of seventeen reads the whole of it back twice in the hall from a slate and not from her own page. **The arrivals are recorded as named guests, and that means about nine people said their own names out loud, once each, in that room, in daylight, and not one of them was asked and not one of the nine names was written down by anybody anywhere, and the page with a count of nine and nobody at the top of it and the nine names in no page are both true and they disagree.** A notary of fifty is asked, in a room, in daylight, with a date on it, by a person — her name is said out loud for the first time in thirty-eight days, she having last said it herself in a room on the hundred and sixteenth day of this flood, because she asked to be asked by a person — and she answers, and **she says no, not to being asked, and no to the opening: a thing that is opened in a hearing is a thing the hearing is about, and the only instrument this city has with that office is the room, and it is gone in a day.** **And a man of thirty-two then answers the volume's question in a form, and it is unpaid, and it is the whole of what a hundred and fifty-four days have been walking towards: what happened to them is not that somebody took them off a list, it is that a form has one place for a count and one place for an assent and has had that for a hundred and forty years, and a man who was taken goes in the count and does not go in the assent, and there is no column anywhere in nine hundred miles for a man who was taken, and that is not a crime and it is not a conspiracy and it is not a person, it is a shape, and a clerk filling it in on the forty-fourth day of the month after this one was doing the ordinary work of a Tuesday.** He stopped there and it is entered that he had about nine more sentences and nobody asked him for them. The hearing then does what a hearing does, in four lines on a page that is not a roll: **the arrivals are named guests under a compact that may be ended by the asking of a person; the crossing is recognised provisionally, for this district and no other purpose, and ending it is a form; and four people and a question are to go four hundred miles inland to the First Landing, to open nothing, and come back.** The man who reads it says out loud that a provisional recognition is a revocable one and that there is a form for ending it he has not seen. **And three things are left unpaid and one of them is a person: the eight names, which nobody read; the man of thirty-two, who is outside every office; and a woman of forty-eight in a fourth house eleven miles away, who is not asked, and a page in a stone that is wrong and is going to stay wrong on purpose.** The room is over in about four hours and a table goes back into it and nobody agreed to that.

## Volume 04, Batch 0001 — Chapters 0151–0170

**Twenty chapters, days 155 to 174 of the flood, one chapter to one day, the last day a Sunday. The fourth date form, *the Nth day of the Long Month*, is established in Chapter 0152 and the Nth is day 155 + N. These summaries are of the page as it stands after the repair pass of `logs/batch-0001.review.log`.**

### Chapter 0151 — The Nine Miles
Day 155, the Tuesday, the sixty-fifth and last day of the month after this one, nine miles of upper road in six hours of rain. **A man of forty-two comes down off the upper road and puts a printed sheet into a hand before he says a word.** The sheet names the ground in its fourth line, says the paper money is worth nothing here, and says the ground was taken in the seventh week without asking. **A girl of seventeen puts the payer's name at the top of every line and not the name of the person paid, and Jonah Pike says *we did not think of that* and asks for it to be entered that they got it right late.** The money is salt and the salt goes to one buyer nine hundred miles away, and the settlement is an export and not a trading post. **At the ninth hour he answers the ninth sentence — Adrian's, said in a yard on day 59, seventy-eight words, the girl reads it off her own slate — and gives nine names of people in a store who have work and are paid this week, and Adrian does not answer it at all.** The count of silences is sixty-one, one more than on day 154, and a form runs out at midnight.

### Chapter 0152 — The Form Ran Out
Day 156, the Wednesday, the first day of the Long Month, a room over a market. **A form runs out at midnight and there is no month left to write a date in, and a notary of fifty names the month: the Long Month.** She gives nine sentences on what it costs and says she is going to do it anyway and asks for that to be entered before and not after. **A small dry woman of about seventy from the village of nine households is asked in a room and not in a hall and says no, and then says the room is going to do it anyway and she is right to.** The date in the new form is put on a door in chalk and three times somebody tries to take it off and nobody does, and about two hundred and forty people in a market underneath come up a stair to look at it. **A woman of about fifty who counts water on the fingers of her left hand leaves word at a stall: nobody comes to my house, come to the gate.**

### Chapter 0153 — Somebody Is Told
Day 157, the Thursday, the second day of the Long Month, a gate at Redroot, eleven miles from the last room Adrian was in. **The water-count of the Reed Reach says the name *Redroot* out loud, once, at a gate, in daylight, to the safest person in four hundred miles to say it to, and says why before she says it.** She puts the name on a board and not on a page, because a board is a thing and a page is a place a person can be found in, and she says said here once and no more. **She has known since the tenth day of this flood that Redroot is not on the Chapter 0010 list of the hamlets on the shared gates and has never once asked why, and a man of thirty-four who keeps a stall says that is the finding and it is not a finding about the seventeen.** She says the fourth channel is a woman's line and it is in force and the refusal she read out in a converted salt store was not about him.

### Chapter 0154 — The Fourth Channel Gets a Name
Day 158, the Friday, the third day of the Long Month, the junction at Nine Elms, eleven miles out on the Reach. **The fourth channel of the junction is the Nine Elms gate of the Reed Reach, and it gets a name, a keeper and a day on a board in chalk, in daylight, at the thing itself.** The water-count says it is a gate and walks out onto the shut channel to prove it. **A gate is not owned and cannot be bought; what a household has is the days on it, and a board, and a keeping of it, and a name in a speaker's record; and the only way a household loses a gate is by agreeing, and if it gives the days it still has the gate.** The Reach's record of who may use a water gate is a list of persons and every name on it is alive and nobody in this city has ever been angry about it. **The keeper of the Nine Elms gate is not on that list, because nobody ever asked her, and she says why once and it is not flattering to anybody.** About four of the nine people at that junction wanted to go and ask a woman of forty-eight in a fourth house a question and nobody did and nobody went near the house. **The chalk goes in the rain and the keeper's name is a smear with a hook at the end of it and there is no second copy. The channel stays shut.**

### Chapter 0155 — Two Hours in a Queue
Day 159, the Saturday, the fourth day of the Long Month, the upper road and then a market. **The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf stands in the settlement's queue for two hours in the rain, carrying other people's sacks, and takes the wage, and the wage is the first one anybody has been paid out of that store and the first in this run of days that has come out of a store's own stock and not out of a stall.** The queue is good: it moves, there is water in a barrel that is charged to nobody, and the man at the front knows nine people's names without a page. **The thing she cannot get out by arguing is that he said her name when she came in the second time, and he must have asked somebody, and there is a list, and it is a good list, and it is a list.** She says nine words in a market in front of about forty people and does not say whose name was on the cross. **A slate she has carried for nine years and has never written on is in her coat all day and nobody in four hundred miles is going to be told why it is blank, including her.**

### Chapter 0156 — Salt
Day 160, the Sunday, the fifth day of the Long Month, the flats and a causeway and a room over a market. **Four hundred and forty racks, a causeway of four miles that took forty days, one buyer nine hundred miles away: the White Ledger's money is salt and the salt goes out through one gate and any other route is four miles of causeway and forty days.** A man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board walks the whole causeway and then says the answer in a room in nine sentences and does not name the gate and does not name a household and does not describe anybody, and the reason is his own: a description is a removal with a column for where the person is. **A woman of twenty-four finds the engine of the volume out loud: then he cannot own it — a gate is not a thing anybody can own, and a settlement that needs water has to be given it, and being given it is a thing that can be asked for.**

### Chapter 0157 — The Gate Board
Day 161, the Monday, the sixth day of the Long Month, a kitchen at Redroot, eleven miles from the last room Adrian was in. **A bowl of hot water and something in it is put in front of a man of thirty-two and he is not asked a question for about forty minutes and he gets nine words of the thing he came to say out.** **The keeper of the Redroot gate holds the gate board in her left hand and does not put it down while she is arguing.** The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf says afterwards, in a market, that he did not ask, and she wants that said, because he came eleven miles and stood in a kitchen and did not ask her for anything. **He is worse off at the end of it than at the start of it and the reason is that she was kind to him without needing him, and a man with no office has very little practice at being in a room where he is not needed.**

### Chapter 0158 — The Cost, Read Out
Day 162, the Tuesday, the seventh day of the Long Month, the settlement's workroom on the upper road. **A workway is offered in the settlement's rooms with its whole cost read out before the accepting, in daylight, with a date on it: a Warden is a groundkeeper, it reinforces a claimed place, organises defence, resists involuntary displacement, it must defend the place and its residents, and abandoning a claimed ground is a matter of the holder's convenience. There is no other clause and no second page.** Somebody reads the fourth line wrong on purpose to see who would stop him and about four people stop him. **The keeper of the Redroot gate says yes, in nine sentences, and the reason is that a duty to a place is the only wage anybody in this world has ever been able to offer her, and nobody has ever read the cost out to her in nine years, and the ninth sentence asks the person taking the workway to say it again with the board in the room.** A man of about twenty-nine from the workroom says the offer may be a breach of a term and that the only office that can answer is nine hundred miles away. **She puts the gate board down on a table and does not pick it up again, and nobody in the room says what that means.** Adrian hears about it on the Wednesday and says the word *relief* and enters it as a fault.

### Chapter 0159 — A Warden's Duty
Day 163, the Wednesday, the eighth day of the Long Month, a channel at Redroot. **A line goes into the ground and about nine of the settlement's people and about twenty-one of the Reach stand in the same line, and at about the ninth hour a man of about twenty-nine comes down it with a slate and cannot tell which nine of the thirty are his, and does not ask.** The line goes wrong twice; a man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close cuts salt off the bottom of a rack and is not going to be thanked for it. **A person can be a wall, and that is the whole of what he came to say, and there are about nine of them and about twenty-one more and he cannot say which is which and will not.** The settlement's own rule — nobody works a queue and a rota on the same morning — is the reason about nine of the thirty have to be sent away, and the rule was written by the settlement and the settlement is not here.

### Chapter 0160 — Ninety-one Acres of a Hundred and Forty
Day 164, the Thursday, the ninth day of the Long Month, the bottom field and a gateway. **The settlement pays the season of ninety-one acres out of a hundred and forty to a woman of forty-four of Corrow who has been owed a figure and a decision since day 80, which is eighty-four days.** She does not want the money, says so before he has finished the sentence, gives one correct reason, and then takes it while he is still holding the chalk out. A girl of seventeen writes it down anyway and says why in her own words: so that in about four months nobody can say the payment was the only one nobody can find. **Elsa Skell is now owed two correct figures — one from a page on the back of a stone nine miles up a road that a woman of about sixty-one with a barrow will not take down, and one from a stranger with a hand and a date — and there is no instrument in four hundred or nine hundred miles for taking one off the other.** In the same gateway, in daylight, **Adrian says out loud that the quarter runs out on day 189, that the hearing was on day 154, that the interval is thirty-five days, and that he is going to do nothing about it for another twenty-five days on purpose, and the delay is the length of the quarter exactly.** He enters it in his own hand. A refusal is a page. A decision not to do something is also a page, and he has never once put a delay on one.

### Chapter 0161 — The First Day of Doing Nothing
Day 165, the Friday, the tenth day of the Long Month. **Nobody needs a man of thirty-two for anything for a day and he does not know what to do with his hands.** The cost column is unchanged at eleven entries with no figure on the end of any of them and no twelfth; the field is paid; the fourth channel is shut and has been shut fifty-three days; **the number of days he has been owed a season has gone up by one, and nobody anywhere in four hundred miles is waiting for him.** He walks nine miles up a road to a fork where the fourth page of a stone has been face up since day 40, does not turn it over, and **asks a woman of about sixty-one with a barrow the first question he has asked anybody in a hundred and sixty-six days, and it is eleven words long, and she answers it in one word and the word is not a noun.** He is not rested and the day does not call it growth. **The last things on the page are twenty-eight words on a slate lying face down on a shelf and a chair on a landing with no name on it and no date on it and no price under it, and nobody turned the first one over.**

### Chapter 0162 — A Wage
Day 166, the Saturday, the eleventh day of the Long Month, the near gate of the Lock of Salt. **Thirty-nine of forty men turn up and they have not been paid since the first of March, which was the eighty-fifth day of this flood, which is eighty-one days, and not one of the forty is on anything.** The settlement pays one day of a wage to nine of them, out of a store, in the shape a stall-keeper invented: the payer's name on it and the paid man's name off it. **Thirty-eight of the thirty-nine said *thirty-nine* together and the thirty-ninth said it about a quarter of a second after the others and has been coming for eighty-one days and is not timidity and is not checked.** Thirty-eight men walk back up the hill with nothing. The fortieth man has not come for forty days and has not been told, on purpose, and the decision is on a slate with a date on it and no name on it.

### Chapter 0163 — To Open Nothing
Day 167, the Sunday, the twelfth day of the Long Month, a hill and a room over a market. **The delegation of four people and a question still has not left, and a woman of twenty-nine who keeps a public register says her own clause out loud in a room, in daylight, with a date on it, and says what it costs her: *the words to open nothing are mine, I put them in on day 123, nobody has asked me whether I would still say them, and I would like it entered why, in my own words and not improved on.*** The settlement has begun asking, in a store, in front of about nine people, which road goes four hundred miles inland, and that is the first time anybody has asked it out loud and it was asked by strangers. Adrian says nothing that improves on her and the count of the delegation stays a number with legs and not a list of names.

### Chapter 0164 — A Share
Day 168, the Monday, the thirteenth day of the Long Month, a room over a market, the hall and the landing and the market below it full. **A man of forty-two puts a page on the step at the back of the room. The step is not a table, the page is not a form, it has no date on it and no place on it, and it is about eleven lines long and there is nothing in the eleven lines anybody could be obliged to do.** It is a share with no post, no roll, no title, no wage, no conditions and no way to revoke it, and he refuses to read it out because the moment he reads it out it becomes a thing said in a room. **Adrian concedes the ninth sentence out loud, in about four minutes, in front of about two hundred and forty people, and he is right to, and it costs him, and a hundred and nine days is the count.** He is asked *would you like to be able to do something* and has no answer. **He did not refuse the share in four seconds. It took four minutes, and he went looking for the place in it where somebody's reading is the price of somebody else's place, and there is not one, and he is not going to say to about two hundred and forty people that he could not find one.** He asks for four days. **A woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf finds the instrument: a share with no date on it is a share that goes on for as long as nobody objects, and it is not a lease, and there is no form anywhere in nine hundred miles for an arrangement that continues until somebody is annoyed. And nobody is going to be annoyed, because you pay. It is a queue.**

### Chapter 0165 — A Rota and a Queue
