Day 169, the Tuesday, the fourteenth day of the Long Month, Redroot and the flats. **Somebody chalked a rota on a gate board and somebody rubbed it off before the sixth hour, and about nine people turned up at the fifth hour anyway, and the chapter does not say whose rota it was.** The practice has left the settlement: the rota is copied by hand four times, on the seventh, ninth, eleventh and thirteenth days, in two hands plus a third, by about nine people who have never been to the upper road. **The queue at Redroot was built out of a gate, a rope and a bell on a post by a woman who keeps a gate and has never seen the settlement's queue and does not go up it.** The labour goes wrong twice and the second time it goes wrong is a rule, and the rule is the settlement's own and the settlement is not here. **Somebody writes the word *commons* on a board on the flats. It is rubbed off twice and written again by somebody else and the third time it is not rubbed off and nobody can say who wrote any of the three.** The keeper is doing the rota without the workway and without the settlement and would do it if neither existed, and she finds that out on a Tuesday afternoon and does not tell anybody, and the chapter knows it and she does not.

### Chapter 0166 — The Name, Asked For
Day 170, the Wednesday, the fifteenth day of the Long Month, a room with a door at Redroot — the first real room in this stretch of days that is not a room over a market. **The name is refused once, properly, by a person who does not want to be asked, and the refusal is correct: a thing with a name is a thing that can be written to, and a name on a body is a column with a body in it, and about four people in this city have been written to and one of them is on a desk nine hundred miles away with a question in its eighth column and no name in it.** The room does not vote, because a count is a list. **Redroot Commons is named anyway, in about nine words, with a date in chalk on a door: it has no roll and no officer and no seat and no standing, and nobody's workway is a term of it, and no party to it may be asked to give one up, and the difference from the word on the settlement's wall is that that wall is a list and this is a rota.** Bel Dain, the boy of eight's grandmother, holds both halves of the danger: a commons that can be asked is a danger and a commons that cannot be asked is a secret, and this city cannot have either without paying somebody for it. **Adrian is told afterwards, at a gate, by the trader, in about nine seconds, and he is not in the room, and he says one word, and the word is *good*.** The wharf woman, who has never been to Redroot, reads the date off the door from about nine feet away and says it twice, once to herself.

### Chapter 0167 — The Meeting-Ground Floats
Day 171, the Thursday, the sixteenth day of the Long Month, eleven miles up the Reach on a raft with a roof on it. **The settlement asks the Reed Commons for the days on the Redroot gate, and the ask is a quantity of water on given weeks for the rest of the season, and the gate is not being sold and cannot be sold, and the settlement will hold the channel and clear the drain and stand the racks in any weather.** The Reach hears the ask and does not answer it. **The finding is that the Reach's own rule — every household affected by a change must be heard — has a hole in it shaped exactly like the woman of forty-eight in a fourth house at Nine Elms, and the hole is not filled and the house is not approached and nobody stands outside it.** The raft is under way and is not back and will not be back before Saturday night, and the day will be on the slate when it gets here and it is not on it now.

### Chapter 0168 — The Better Record
Day 172, the Friday, the seventeenth day of the Long Month, six hours and a market. **A woman of twenty-four copies nine pages of a store's week by hand in six hours and finds it better than anything on a wall in this city, and gives it away, having been refused once and asked a second time.** The shape that matters is the payer's name and not the paid man's, which she has known since day 135. **She will say where they came from in a yard, in daylight, in front of about forty people, and she will say it in the words of the people who wrote them and not in hers.** A man of about forty-four who keeps a stall, who has been on a gate board since the sixth day of this flood, stands in the gateway with it. A man of forty-two comes down nine miles on foot at the eighth hour of the evening to a yard and finds a girl of seventeen standing in the gateway with a slate. **Nine pages of a better record are in four hands in a city that had one record on a wall, and none of the four hands is on a roll of anything, and about nine people in a yard did not believe him and one of them has a slate and did.**

### Chapter 0169 — The Witness
Day 173, the Saturday, the eighteenth day of the Long Month, a room over a market, eleven hours. **The wall speaks in the morning, says one short thing in the plain language of an offer, and is gone before it has finished arriving, and the refusal costs about four seconds and the cost is named out loud by a person in the room. The day repeats the number before it and the count of silences does not move: seventy-eight yesterday and seventy-eight this morning.** The single System panel of the batch is here, four lines, world-native: **any correct figure may be checked by a person; the person who checks it answers for the check and not for the figure; nothing is owed, nothing is given, it can be done in a room in daylight.** In the afternoon **Jonah Pike gives a man of thirty-two a name, in nine sentences, in daylight, in a room: *the witness*.** Not a title, not an office, not a post, not a form. **A man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall asks the only question in the chapter that matters and it is four words long: *to whom do you answer.*** Adrian does not refuse it. He says *say it again in a month*, and a man of about forty-four who keeps a stall says the man of thirty-two said the same words to this man in a yard about a hundred days ago and then said *I will not*, and Adrian says *I know, I said it to him and I did not mean it, and this time I do not know what I mean, and that is the difference and it is not a better answer.* **By nightfall the name is in four mouths. Somebody in a market has said *the witness* about a man of thirty-two to a person who did not know him, and he is more findable than he was that morning, and being findable is not a penalty for being real. It is the price of it.** A girl of seventeen writes the date and the name on a slate and does not read it back.

### Chapter 0170 — The Date Comes Back
Day 174, the Sunday, the nineteenth day of the Long Month, a gate at Redroot, a flat and a causeway. **A raft comes down eleven miles in the dark with nine words on a slate and a day on it, and the meeting-ground did not set the day and would not set it: the household must be asked, the day is not ours, the day must come back to us.** The day came back as the twenty-fourth of the Long Month, which is day 179 and a Friday, and it is five days away, **and nobody in this chapter knows how it was chosen and about four people have worked out that it must have been chosen by somebody who was not asked either.** A man of about twenty-nine from the settlement's workroom puts the settlement's whole position into about nine sentences and it is the best thing anybody in that store has said: a household is not losing a gate, it is being paid for a duty, and the gate is not being sold and will not be, and nobody in the store is going to write the word *sold* on anything this month. **The keeper of the Redroot gate tells Adrian all of it herself, at a door, in daylight, on the Sunday, in about four minutes, and she does not ask him for anything.** She says four things: the date is right and she chose it; nobody has asked her anything in a hundred and seventy-four days and that is not a complaint; **her household is going to say yes, and she is going to be angry about it for a year, and that is the whole of her and there is not going to be a second day of it**; and he is going to be somewhere on the fifth day from now and she does not want him there and she is telling him in advance so it is not a thing that has to be worked out in a room. **A boy of eight is told on Thursday night and she will do it herself and she asked for that to be entered.** Adrian enters that he is going to do nothing about it for twenty-five days and that the twenty-fourth is the fifteenth of those twenty-five and that he will not break his own entry to be in a room. **The board at the bottom of the channel carries the second and the nineteenth in chalk in two hands, and the channel is still shut, and about nine hundred measures of salt are dry on racks that a settlement pays for, and a causeway of four miles stands finished and empty in the dark, and the days on the Redroot gate belong to nobody yet.**

---

## Volume 04, Batch 0002 — Chapters 0171 to 0190, days 175 to 194

### Chapter 0171 — A Gate With a Household Behind It
Day 175, the Monday, the twentieth day of the Long Month, the flats at Redroot and a market in this city. **About nine people are at the bottom of a channel who were not there on Sunday and a date in chalk on a board has gone eleven miles of the Reach by the second hour of the afternoon, and the finding is nine words long: a date is the only thing anybody in this world has that travels on its own.** A woman of about thirty-eight tells a boy of eight, in about four minutes, that on Friday there will be people in the room, and does not tell him what she is going to say, and nobody improves on him. **Ninety days** is what the thirty-nine men under the near gate have gone unpaid, and a man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close asks a man barred from every roster to go and stand in a place, which is free.

### Chapter 0172 — A Second Workway
Day 176, the Tuesday, the twenty-first day of the Long Month, a kitchen on the flats. **A man of about twenty-nine from the settlement's workroom reads a workway's whole cost out, in full, in daylight, with a date on the door, to a second household, and the finding is that he cannot tell which of five rooms he left a sentence out in, and he says so in that kitchen before anybody asks him.** The woman of the fourth household takes it for a roof, a wage and a Tuesday. **A man of about forty-two says nine sentences in a yard and the ninth of them is that he does not need to lie about a cost, because a man who reads the same true thing out five times can shorten it by accident and nobody checks him.** Nobody threatens anybody. Adrian is eleven miles away, entered that he will do nothing about it for twenty-five days, and cannot put anything in front of a meeting-ground before the twenty-fifth.

### Chapter 0173 — Nine Years, Three People
Day 177, the Wednesday, the twenty-second day of the Long Month, a gate, a queue and a kitchen. **A day that is three-quarters a list of chores nine years old, and a woman of about fifty-nine who keeps the house gives a boy of eight a day off in advance without asking anybody, and the finding is that a household organised around a gate has no hour at which anybody is off and the only person in it who ever has is eight.** The boy makes eleven knots in nine feet of rope in about ten hours and answers four adults identically and nobody improves on him. A woman of about fifty who counts water comes down the flats and says out loud that the Reach's record of who may use a water gate is a list of persons and every name on it is alive and she does not know whether that is a good answer, and writes nothing down.

### Chapter 0174 — Standing the Racks
Day 178, the Thursday, the twenty-third day of the Long Month, the day before. **The weather turns and about four hundred and forty racks have to be covered in about five hours and there is no one from the store on the flat and nobody sent, and the finding is nine words long and belongs to nobody: a promise made in a room is about a room.** The rota goes up on the gate board in a fifth hand. Eleven miles away a man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall tells a man of about thirty-two in a gateway that being right about all of it is going to be the worst thing that has ever happened to him. At about the ninth hour of the evening, in a kitchen, the keeper tells the boy of eight the whole of it herself, and he asks whether the bell will still be there.

### Chapter 0175 — The Days on the Redroot Gate
Day 179, the Friday, the twenty-fourth day of the Long Month, a kitchen at Redroot — THE VOLUME'S MIDPOINT. **A household is asked, in a room, in daylight, with a date in chalk on the door, for the whole of the days on the Redroot gate for the rest of the season, and about nine people are in it and there is no hall and no market under it, and the cost is read out in full first, in the words of the person offering it, and the keeper makes him read out the sentence he had shortened, and the household says yes in one sentence, and there is no vote because a count is a list.** A boy of eight is not in the room. **Adrian is not in the room, did not break his twenty-five-day entry to be in it, and finds out afterwards from a girl of seventeen who stood in a yard and then walked eleven miles, and it is not from a page.** The keeper's own words on the day are that she chose the date, that she is going to be angry about it for a year, and that there is not going to be a reason.

### Chapter 0176 — The Day After
Day 180, the Saturday, the twenty-fifth day of the Long Month, the flats. **About nine people come down eleven miles on foot with bars and none of them was asked and none was sent, and a hundred and forty yards of channel takes nine people a day and a half, and the finding is that nobody came to take anything and there is nothing to be angry about.** The queue is a queue; the boy rang a bell four times; the bed is not a gain and was there in the second month. **A woman of about fifty who counts water comes to the gate and says out loud that the Reach's list is the only list in four hundred miles that has never been wrong about a person and that she does not know whether that is a good answer, and writes nothing down.** A woman of about thirty-four says at about the seventh hour that it is still here.

### Chapter 0177 — Four Legs of Unequal Length
Day 181, the Sunday, the twenty-sixth day of the Long Month, a kitchen at the bottom of a channel. **A recovery with no form in it: a man of thirty-two walks eleven miles with nothing in his hands to be useful at something, says he is sorry, is told no, stands up, and writes nothing down.** The keeper says the whole of a gate in nine words, and it is nine years of hers and he has had it for about a week. **A woman of about fifty-nine who cannot read works out the days since the sixty-fourth day of the month after this one — twenty-seven — and tells him he has not been in a room that wanted him, and says that the not being asked is what she has noticed since about the eighth hour that morning.**

### Chapter 0178 — Nine Hundred Measures
Day 182, the Monday, the twenty-seventh day of the Long Month, the flats and a causeway of four miles. **The first full load of salt goes out of the Reach, nine hundred and nine measures on a sledge, and a man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board walks the causeway nine times over and finds that the only instrument that can stop a road is the ground it is on, and that the ground belongs to a woman of thirty-eight whose days can be given back.** A gate is not owned and a road is not a gate. Nine hundred measures a week go to one buyer nine hundred miles away and that is the whole of the settlement's money. The keeper stands at the gate for the whole day with the board in her left hand and says one sentence to a man of about thirty-four and it is that he said it and not her.

### Chapter 0179 — A Question About a Term
Day 183, the Tuesday, the twenty-eighth day of the Long Month, a market and a gateway with a door that shuts. **The first wage goes out of a store in nine sacks and a man of about thirty-two asks a real, correctly framed question about a term of the trial licence and a clerk of the fourth desk says there is a column for it and that the column goes to a desk and the desk is a room and the room is nine hundred miles away, and that the answer will be correct in four months and worth nothing to a household.** **The fourth channel at the Nine Elms junction has been shut seventy-one days and that junction has not taken water in a hundred and fourteen days.** Adrian puts his two questions next to each other for the first time in twenty-nine days and works out that the reason the first is worth nothing is the second.

### Chapter 0180 — Nine Miles Up the Road
Day 184, the Wednesday, the twenty-ninth day of the Long Month, a workroom with a date in chalk on the door. **A man of about forty-two offers, in nine sentences, to pay four months of the wage of a person with a standing so that the question can be put in the seventh column, and says the reason out loud on purpose, and it is that he would like to stop being the only person in four hundred miles who pays people on a day.** A woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf names the price: about a hundred and forty people who get paid would find out in four months that the wage was on a list, and there is no instrument that takes a person off a list they were not put on. **Adrian refuses it in about nine minutes, which is the third time a refusal has cost anything, and the sentence he uses was said in the second month of this flood and is the oldest one in the room.**

### Chapter 0181 — Disappointed
Day 185, the Thursday, the thirtieth day of the Long Month, a store on the upper road. **A man of about forty-two says out loud in a yard that he is disappointed, and that the sharpest thing he has got is that there is nothing in it he can be argued out of.** **Ninety** is the count of days this city has got nothing out of a wall, and a girl of seventeen says it in a store and says that nobody checks hers. A rota is seven days and nine days of a week is not a week. A woman asks what is the date and a man of forty-two finds out he has been running a settlement on a word for nine weeks.

### Chapter 0182 — What They Will Not Do
Day 186, the Friday, the thirty-first day of the Long Month, a yard with a market under it. **The coalition of the seventeen splits, between people who want a wage and people who want a name, and neither side is wrong, and there is no roll, no count and no vote, and four people say out loud what they will not do.** A woman of about twenty-four who has written down every decision this city has made since the second day of the flood says no to writing any of it down and says why, and it is the first time she has chosen not to write one down because she wanted to. **A man of about thirty-two says nine sentences and does not try to hold it together and says he has not found another instrument and is not going to pretend to two hundred and forty people that he has.**

### Chapter 0183 — What a Warden Is For
Day 187, the Saturday, the thirty-second day of the Long Month, the low end of a causeway. **Water takes the bottom four hundred yards and about nine people from the store and about nine from the flats pull the same rope for two hours without discussing it, and the store pays the nine from the flats, and the finding is that the thing which has not happened in a hundred and eighty-seven days is a wage going the other way.** A woman of about thirty-eight comes out of the gateway with a bar because she took a groundkeeper's workway on the seventh day of the Long Month and the fourth line of it is that she does not get to walk off claimed ground because the weather has turned. Two pairs of boots are a favour and a favour stops.

### Chapter 0184 — The Seventh Day From Now
Day 188, the Sunday, the thirty-third day of the Long Month, a workroom with a date in chalk on the door. **There is one day of the quarter left and it is tomorrow, and two men in a room settle a date and neither of them wins it: the seventh day from now, a Sunday, the fortieth day of the Long Month, on which the store shuts, and both of them arrived at it from opposite ends and a man of about thirty-nine says nobody chose it.** A man of about forty-two refuses to hand over a page with four days on it and puts a date on one instead, because a page with a post in it is a thing with a post in it. A man of about forty-four says nine words: he is going to stand at the back.

### Chapter 0185 — The Thirty-Fourth Day of the Long Month
Day 189, the Monday, the thirty-fourth day of the Long Month, a room over a market. **THE QUARTER RUNS OUT, thirty-five days after the hearing on the sixty-fourth day of the month after this one, and nothing can be sent and nothing could be prepared and there is no road that reaches that office, and about nine people spend the whole day in a room with nothing to do.** **A return was due today and it does not arrive today, and the finding of the day is a man of about thirty-nine's: a thing that was going to arrive on a day and did not is a different kind of information from a thing that arrives.** **Ninety-four** is the count of silences and **a hundred and four** days is what the thirty-nine men have gone unpaid. Adrian's twenty-five days end at the last hour of the evening and about nine people do not count that.

### Chapter 0186 — A Figure With Nothing Under It
Day 190, the Tuesday, the thirty-fifth day of the Long Month, a room over a market. **A cart comes up the Reach road on its way somewhere else with a return in it, and a girl of seventeen reads four lines out, and the return agrees with the twelfth shape on a wall in a converted salt store, and the reading of that number is not Adrian's and never has been.** **A woman of about thirty-four says the whole of it in four sentences: a quarterly return is not a change, and a thing that is not a change is the most dangerous kind of thing there is, and nobody can be stopped by it, and four times a year.** A woman of about seventy of a village of nine households is asked about it in a yard and answers in four words and is not asked again. A man of about thirty-nine says on a road that it came in a cart that was going somewhere else and that is the sharpest thing anybody has said in a hundred and ninety days.

### Chapter 0187 — Ninety-Five
Day 191, the Wednesday, the thirty-sixth day of the Long Month, a room over a market. **The wall speaks and says four short things: the days on a gate can be given back by the household alone; no other party may do it; a person who is findable can be sent into any room; a person who is findable cannot be sent to a gate; nothing is owed, nothing is given, it can be done in a room in daylight.** Adrian refuses it before it has finished going away. **The count is ninety-five and it was ninety-five yesterday and it does not move, and the wall spoke on the eighteenth day of the Long Month and this is the second time it has put two halves of the same fact in one field.** A woman of about thirty-four names the cost and it is the name he did not choose. It gives no date, no room, no person and no instruction.

### Chapter 0188 — Nine Sentences About a Cost
Day 192, the Thursday, the thirty-seventh day of the Long Month, a room over a market with nothing on the table. **About nine people each say what it has cost them, out loud, in their own words, in no order anybody chose, and nobody names a price first and nobody improves on any of it.** The instrument is four sentences long and it was built in about nine minutes. **Tamsin Quill says hers in six sentences and refuses, in a room with about nine people in it, the one thing everybody in the room has been waiting for her to say about the man she has been checking for a hundred and ninety-two days**, and says that the cost of a check is that a person who is needed is a person who can be found and she has never once been asked whether she would rather be a person in a room. A woman of about twenty-four says hers last: she is the only person in this city who can be checked.

### Chapter 0189 — A Question With No Answer In It
Day 193, the Friday, the thirty-eighth day of the Long Month, a workroom nine miles up the upper road. **A man of about thirty-two gives a four-sentence question in his own hand to about nine people four days before a meeting-ground, and the instrument is used for the third time and on a body for the first time, and one person cannot be given it and is not going to be in the room, and he writes her refusal into his own book with the date on it.** A man of about forty-two cannot answer the question and will not refuse it and will be in a yard on the Sunday and not on the raft. A man of about twenty-nine says that a person who is findable is a person who can be sent and a person who can be sent is a person who cannot go. A woman of about twenty-nine says a meeting-ground is a number with legs and a question is better than a delegation. A share with the post taken out is still in a coat and is unresolved.

### Chapter 0190 — At the Back
Day 194, the Saturday, the thirty-ninth day of the Long Month — THE BATCH'S CLIFF, and the first of two chapters on that day. **A question in four sentences is read out in the local language in daylight with a date in chalk on the door in front of about nine people and about two hundred and forty who can hear, and a girl of seventeen reads it back for the people at the back from a slate and not from her own, and a man of about thirty-two stands at the back for four hours and does not answer it, and about two hundred and forty people hear him not answer it.** A woman of about thirty-four says out loud that the question is asking the wrong body, because a gate is not owned and the days are the household's and only a household can give them back. **He knew, since the twenty-sixth day of the Long Month, and wrote it anyway, and gave it in writing four days beforehand, and it is too late to change it, and the finding is that a room asked a question it cannot answer has to find the person who can, and the rule of the Reach is four hundred years old and has no name on it.** A woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf will not put the question on a slate and carries it in her hand up eleven miles in the dark.

## Volume 04, Batch 0003 — Chapters 0191 to 0200, days 194 to 200

### Chapter 0191 — A Yard With Nothing In It
Day 194, the Saturday, the thirty-ninth day of the Long Month, the night before a meeting-ground is poled. **The store on the upper road is shut in the morning and about a hundred and forty people have a day they have not had since the seventh week of this flood, and the shut is in chalk on a door because a door is not a page, and about nine of them are going to walk up the road past this city.** A girl of seventeen says **ninety-eight** and gives the rule. Nine hundred miles away a man of forty-one in a very good coat is told a figure he did not ask for. A woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf walks the question eleven miles in the dark in her hand, would not put it on a slate, and hands it to a water-count on a bank at about the ninth hour of the evening.

### Chapter 0192 — Nine Feet Of Standing Room
Day 195, the Sunday, the fortieth day of the Long Month. **A meeting-ground is poled four hours to the flats at Redroot and the question in four sentences is read out on a flat by a water-count with them on the flat of her left hand, and about nine people are standing there and not one of them was asked to be.** A man of forty-two stands in his own yard and watches about nine people walk up the road past this city and asks none of them where they are going. The raft says it holds no days on any gate, that it is four hundred years old that it will not name a household, and that there is no other date in four hundred miles this month it could have been poled to. Adrian is not there because a person who is findable cannot be sent to a gate.

### Chapter 0193 — It Will Not Name A Household
Day 196, the Monday, the forty-first day of the Long Month. **The rule is said out loud in four sentences and the water-count refuses to name it or say who built it, and then the raft refuses to name the household — not out of preference but because it does not know, and because asking nine people one at a time in front of the other eight would leave a mark on this water in about four months.** A man of about thirty-nine says the raft is right for his reason and not the raft's. Nine people then work out for themselves who is affected, for two hours, with no vote and no count. **The four sentences are legible on a wet hand until about the eleventh hour and then they are not, and about four days later nobody in four hundred miles can check what was asked.** The keeper comes out at the eleventh hour and says she is not going to be asked and is going to say it anyway.

### Chapter 0194 — A Date In Chalk On A Rail
Day 196, the Monday, the forty-first day of the Long Month. **A woman of about thirty-eight gives nine days of a week back, unasked, in daylight, on a flat, having given them in a kitchen with a date in chalk on a door seventeen days before, and she is seventeen days into a year of being angry and nobody is going to argue her out of the rest of it.** Nobody gave her anything and nothing is arriving with her name on it, and a woman of about fifty-nine who keeps the house says she is the one who has to live in it for the rest of the year. The days go back into a rota on a board in a fifth hand, in one copy, not copied. Adrian stood on a bank eleven miles above the cut and did not come down. **And the volume's one sentence is not said again and a gate is not owned stands at zero in all fifty chapters.**

### Chapter 0195 — Four Miles Of Causeway
Day 197, the Tuesday, the forty-second day of the Long Month. **The salt stops and nobody has shut anything: the racks stand in the channel, the channel takes its water from a gate, a rack that has not been worked does not come off, and nine hundred measures stand in a yard at the foot of a four-mile causeway that took forty days to build.** A man of about thirty-four says the whole of it is that the road is not shut and a gate is eleven feet away from it, and about four hundred and forty people dry salt for a buyer nine hundred miles away and there is no route in this world that reaches him. **The loss falls on the settlement and not on the Reach, that sentence is the reason nine people on a flat were able to do it, and a wage that stops is not a favour that stops.** **A hundred and twelve days is what the thirty-nine men under the near gate have gone unpaid, and it is a different debt and it did not change today.**

### Chapter 0196 — One Hundred And Fifty-Eight
Day 198, the Wednesday, the forty-third day of the Long Month. **The trestle count is read out in a room over a yard, in daylight, with a date on the door, by the man who has been keeping it, in his own hand, and it is one hundred and fifty-eight, counted one a day from the fortieth day of this flood and from nothing else.** It was one hundred and fourteen on the sixty-fourth day of the month after this one and eighty-nine on the thirty-ninth, and forty-four days separate the hearing from the room and there are forty-four in the difference. He does not know what a day of it means and has never pretended to. **Adrian was asked whether the day suited him and said no, and said out loud that he will never know who checked a number he was not in the room to hear.** A man of about thirty-nine says a person who is not findable cannot be checked and a person who is findable is a place to be found in and Marrow is neither, and a woman of about twenty-four writes all three down separately.

### Chapter 0197 — Nine And Eight
Day 198, the Wednesday, the forty-third day of the Long Month. **The number goes onto the wall of the room it was read in, in a column with a date at the top, in a hand that is not the hand that keeps the book, and about four people in that room had wanted it not to.** A woman of about thirty-four says a correct page is a page nobody can argue with and a page nobody can argue with is a page nobody can stop. A man of about forty-four recites his cost column once and says the two of those things are the same thing. **And about nine of the seventeen say one sentence each in a yard and about eight say another, so the split is nine and eight, and nobody made a count and nobody voted, and the finding is not the split: eight of them read a number they could not understand and decided the man who can get forty people a week out of a city that cannot get one out is the safer answer to it.**

### Chapter 0198 — A Person May Be Found
Day 199, the Thursday, the forty-fourth day of the Long Month. **A posting goes into a page: the ground stays with the households, no land right transfers, no workway is a term, the labour is shared and posted, a right of refusal that cannot be bought out is on it, and a man of about thirty-nine's own objection goes in with his name and the date on it because he is a household affected by a change.** A man of forty-two takes a share on the same posting as everybody else and says nine sentences, the ninth of which is the word *order*, and Renna Ord is at the back of that room and says nothing and about four people know the word is hers. **And the woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf names the price before she says yes: for nine years nobody has known what she looks like while she is arguing, and from today there is a date and a stall and a name on a page. She asks in advance not to be thanked, and nobody thanks her.**

### Chapter 0199 — Eleven Lines
Day 200, the Friday, the forty-fifth day of the Long Month, and the volume's resolution. **A land-and-labour compact of eleven lines is read out in the local language in daylight in a room over a market with a date in chalk on the door, before about nine people and about two hundred and forty who can hear, which is the sixth time two hundred and forty people have heard a thing said in that room.** A girl of seventeen reads the whole of it back twice from a plain slate and not from her own. **The ninth line is a person and it is *a person may be found*, and it is not the fifth line of the compact of eleven lines, and a woman of about twenty-nine says in five sentences that a document ended by the asking of a person and a document requiring a person to be findable are not the same instrument and neither is a copy of the other.** The name at the front of it is said once in a room and is deliberately not on any page you can read. The keeper is not in the room and nobody went and got her and that is entered as a failure.

### Chapter 0200 — At The Back Of His Own Queue
Day 200, the Friday, the forty-fifth day of the Long Month, and the last chapter of Volume 04. **Out on the flats the four-mile causeway is standing open and empty with no bar on it, the salt has not stopped drying, a boy of eight rang the bell twice, and there is nothing in the day to tell anybody a story about.** In the market under the room, about two hundred and forty people have two sentences between them about a compact nobody can check and a list anybody can be angry at, and neither was said to the other. Nine miles up the road the store is open and the queue worked out of a chest that has two weeks in it, a man of about thirty-four said a hundred and fifteen days and said at the front of his own queue in daylight with a date on the door that he did not shorten it, and **at about the sixth hour of the evening a man of forty-two stands at the back of his own queue with his hands in his pockets and nobody turns him out.**

## Volume 05, Batch 0001 — Chapters 0201 to 0210, days 201 to 210

**Ten chapters, ten days, one chapter to a day, and no double day in the batch. `outline/volume-05.md` does not exist and this batch did not decide what Volume 05 is; the batch's subject is a page and not a volume. The ten chapters have been through one review repair pass — `reviews/volume-05/batch-0001.md`, eleven findings, ten repaired and one checked and declined — and no day, beat, cast member, ending or finding moved; what the repair established on the page is that the recorder is a woman, that the page Adrian Vale's name is on is the compact and is on no wall, that the slate on the chest was written on the Saturday morning of the forty-sixth day of the Long Month, and that the workroom man is about twenty-nine, the woman who keeps a public register is about twenty-nine and the water-counter is about fifty.**

1. **Chapter 0201 — A Price Under Every Line.** Day 201, the forty-sixth day of the Long Month, a Saturday. The posting goes up in chalk on a wall of the store on the upper road at the seventh hour with a date on it and a price under every line, and the prices are in words: a sack, a place at a fire, a day of the queue. A man of about twenty-nine from the settlement's workroom, who wrote nine of the eleven lines in the room over a market on the forty-fourth, says a page with no figure under any price is a list of intentions; the man who wrote it says it is the price and that he does not have a number; a woman of about fifty who counts water on the fingers of her left hand points out that he has written the settlement's own sentence on a wall four days after the thing that paid for it went out of a yard on the Monday night of the forty-first day of the Long Month, and twenty-two days after a man read out a whole workway in a kitchen at Redroot; a man of about thirty-nine says put a number under it, because a cost in words cannot be wrong and a cost in words also cannot be checked. Two numbers go under two lines from a woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road, in that order, unjoined. A woman of about twenty-four picks up the right of refusal on the wall and about four people find it before either number, the man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board asks who administers it, and the man who wrote the page answers in the first person: nobody, and there is no office in four hundred miles that receives a refusal, and it is for the shape and not for the administration.

2. **Chapter 0202 — Four Hundred And Forty.** Day 202, the forty-seventh, a Sunday. About nine people walk the four miles of the causeway with no load, and the first of them has never stood at the top of a road. The woman of about thirty-four from the fourth household says the four hundred and forty again and that about four of her figures are wrong and about four are right. The man of about fifty-two who digs says six days and a rod that goes in further every time and not much. Asked how she gets on the posting, she is asked whose household she is, and the finding is that a shared thing is shared between two bodies and that about four hundred and thirty-one people answer to neither. The seventh line is the only one of the eleven that says anything about a road and nobody in the city knows which road, and the man who built the one road anybody here has built is a party to the compact, and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall enters that he is not going to ask him. The four hundred and forty work eleven miles from a compact and the one person who pays them is nine hundred miles off and cannot be reached.

3. **Chapter 0203 — The Forty-Eighth Day.** Day 203, the forty-eighth, a Monday, and it is the date the keeper of the Redroot gate named. Nobody went. A woman of about fifty-nine who keeps that house and a boy of eight come out onto the step and say the day came, that she is not going and is not going to send the boy, and that she is the one who has to be in that house for the rest of a year; she gives a second reason, that a child sent up the bank with a message would make everything the keeper does for a year a thing a household is watching. The same woman raises the sack: one came on the twenty-fifth and one is due, the days went back on the forty-first, and nobody has worked out whether a thing given in exchange for days given back still comes, and she will not ask because asking is a person saying out loud that she does not trust a man. The keeper works the channel from the ninth hour with the board in her left hand and says nothing about that day, the twenty-fourth, or a year. A man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close says two things have happened on this cut in six days and about nine people can only talk about the one that costs nothing this week. A man of about thirty-two is on the bank a second Monday running and says he was asked twice in five days whether a day suited him and said no twice.

4. **Chapter 0204 — Its Place On It And Not Above It.** Day 204, the forty-ninth, a Tuesday, the queue's day. The store's queue is the ninth and last line of the posting, in the hand of the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, and he put it there on purpose. The wage comes out of the chest at the sixth hour and the chest has a week left in it, and a woman of about thirty-four in that store, on no list in it, has a slate on the chest with the fifty-sixth day of the Long Month on it because that is the last Tuesday a wage can come out of it. He says seven sentences, of which the fourth is that a queue is a line that nobody decided and the fifth that a posting is a line that somebody decided. A woman of about twenty-four writes the order of that queue down at the sixth hour and again at the seventh and the two lists are not the same list, and she puts both in her bag. A man of forty-two says the wage went out at six and it does not matter what line it is on and asks for it to be said in a yard. A hundred and nineteen days is what the thirty-nine men under the near gate have gone unpaid, and they are not in the yard and are not on the page.

5. **Chapter 0205 — One Line A Week.** Day 205, the fiftieth, a Wednesday. The man who keeps the number comes into the room over a yard and writes one line, because he writes one a week, and the number is one hundred and sixty-five; the wall in that room has one hundred and fifty-eight on it with the forty-third day of that month at the top of the column, and there are seven days between the figures and seven in the difference, and the wall is correct for the forty-third and is not wrong. He is asked what a day of it is and says *I do not know*, as he has said in about four rooms. A man of about fifty-two who digs says it would take a hundred and sixty-five days and a person on the ground on each at the same hour, and that there is nobody anybody could be argued out of a thing about because it is a figure with no shape, and that he can date a bank and cannot date a number. A girl of seventeen keeps the other count and will not say it and gives a new reason, that both counts go up one a day and that about four people have added them together and about nine have taken one from the other and not one of those figures is in either book. He turns past the page in that book that is not a figure while a woman of about twenty-four watches and does not ask.

6. **Chapter 0206 — A Refusal With No Reason.** Day 206, the fifty-first, a Thursday. A man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board says no to a line on the posting in a yard, in daylight, in front of about nine people, and does not say a reason and does not say which line. A woman of about twenty-four picks a piece of chalk off the ledge under the wall, holds it, and puts it back down, because a mark is a record and a record is administration. Nobody asked him why, and it is entered in three places that nobody wanted to. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says a refusal that cannot be administered cannot be recorded and cannot be counted, and that a right of refusal which cannot be bought out cannot be afforded either, because a posted day pays a sack and a place at a fire and a day of the queue, and about nine people in this city can afford to refuse and about four hundred and thirty-one cannot. The man of about thirty-nine says he found that on the day he used the right. Then about four people work out that the page does not say whose labour it is.

7. **Chapter 0207 — Sent For.** Day 207, the fifty-second, a Friday. About four people stand in front of the man of about thirty-four's stall with a slip of the posting with a date cut into the corner of it, and he walks four streets to a room over a market with a date in chalk on the door, the seventh time about two hundred and forty people have heard a thing said in that room. Asked four times in nine minutes whether the price under his own line is right, he says he does not know, and then says that he wrote the nine sentences on the Thursday, the compact was read on the Friday, he put the posting on a wall on the Saturday, and he has not had a number since. A man of about forty-four who keeps a stall says a cost with no figure on the end of it is a cost and a price with a figure is a promise, and that the fourth line is administered in four words by a man with a bar of chalk, and refuses to take the page down. A woman of about twenty-four puts her hand flat on the first line of a copy of the compact, does not read it, reads the second name out loud and stops, and says a page with a name on it is a room and not a metaphor. A girl of seventeen says she is the only one of the nine in that room who is not on the front of that page, that it is because she was careful, and that it cost her nothing.

8. **Chapter 0208 — In Front Of The Stall.** Day 208, the fifty-third, a Saturday. A man of about forty-four who keeps a stall stands in front of the stall by the wharf with the front of the compact on the back of a slate, copied on a market table on the afternoon of the forty-fifth because he thought a page ought to be in two places. The woman of about thirty-four who keeps that stall comes out and says the price she named on the forty-fourth was being findable while she is arguing, and that what she has is being findable on a Saturday morning with nothing to argue about. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says none of the last eight days came from the wharf and every bit of it came out of that market table. A woman of about twenty-four says the rule of the Reach has a hole in it shaped exactly like a household, that she has not gone near it and nobody in that yard is going to, and asks for it to be written down that a hole and a wharf were in the same yard and that nobody joined them up. At the second hour a second man comes and stands there with nothing in his hands, having heard the name from about four people, and she says that is the half with no page and no office nine hundred miles away that can stop it.

9. **Chapter 0209 — The Name He Did Not Choose.** Day 209, the fifty-fourth, a Sunday. A woman of about twenty-four says a name in a yard nine miles up the road at the seventh hour and Adrian Vale answers to it in about a second, and she says nobody sent her and that she is the person who has kept the record since the second day of this flood and has never once been on a page herself. They walk nine miles to a room over a market, the eighth time about two hundred and forty people have heard a thing said in that room, and he is asked to say his name, and says it once, in the local language, in daylight, with a date on the door. He then says that a name on a page is the small half and that a word in a room cannot be taken out of it, and that what he has just done is the more expensive instrument, and that about nine people can now fetch him and he answered quickly. The woman of about twenty-four does not write it down. Asked whether the ninth line works, he says he cannot tell them, in four sentences, and says his own sentence has cost him one number, one room, one refusal and every mile of road he has walked since the thirty-sixth day of the Long Month. Nobody offered him anything, he asked for nothing, and a man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall asks for that to go in the record and not be said out loud again.

10. **Chapter 0210 — The Morning Before The Last Tuesday.** Day 210, the fifty-fifth, a Monday, and the queue does not work. About a hundred and forty people are in the yard because tomorrow is the last Tuesday a wage can come out of the chest, and a woman of about thirty-four in that store has said the date out loud four times. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall stands in front of the ninth line for about four minutes, does not take it off the wall, and then says what a posting did not do: it did not make a wage, put a name on a wall, fetch a man to a room, stop a man saying no, put a man in front of a stall, or pay anybody. A hundred and twenty-five days is what the thirty-nine men under the near gate have gone unpaid and they are not on the page and nobody is going to tell them, and he is asked whether he will go and says no and gives the same reason as the other two. The man of about fifty-two who digs says fourteen days and that he has stopped measuring. The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register says the delegation has not left and that *to open nothing* is her sentence, and that she started on Saturday to write down who the four of them are, got two names in, stopped and tore the page out. A man of forty-two stands at the back of his own yard for the third time in three days and says he is going to stop saying out loud that he is not going anywhere, because a thing you keep saying out loud stops being a thing anybody can be angry at.

## Volume 05, Batch 0002 — Chapters 0211 to 0220, days 211 to 220

**Ten chapters, ten days, one chapter to a day, and no double day in the batch. `outline/volume-05.md` does not exist and this batch did not decide what Volume 05 is; the batch's subject is a *day* — the day the last batch was counting down to came and went, and these are the ten days after it — and the record is `state/batch-summaries/volume-05-batch-0002.md` and the rules are `bible/power-system.md` §28. `outline/series.md` carries a one-paragraph line for Volume 05 and this batch saw it and did not act on it and recorded that it did not (§12.5).**

1. **Chapter 0211 — The Last Tuesday.** Day 211, the fifty-sixth day of the Long Month, a Tuesday, and the last Tuesday a wage can come out of the settlement's chest. About a hundred and forty people are in the yard and about nine of them walked nine miles on foot to be in it, and the ninth and last line of the posting is chalk on that wall about nine feet above the back of the last person standing under it, in a different hand from the other eight lines. **A woman of about thirty-four in that store, who is on no list in it, says the date out loud in the yard for the fifth time, and establishes a man of about twenty-seven who washes the store's board, writes the day's figures on it, and has been at the front of that queue since the seventh week of this flood, on no roster of anything and paid nothing for it.** Adrian Vale comes up the road on foot, reads the figures off the board, says *nine*, does not stand in the queue, and goes down the nine miles; about four people in that yard have said since that a man walked nine miles on a day the queue was working and did not stand in it. A man of forty-two goes to the back of the queue, asks the woman to say the date once more, and is thanked by nobody. **He then says a hundred and twenty-six days in a yard, once, in daylight, in front of about nine people — that is what the thirty-nine men under the near gate of the Lock of Salt have gone unpaid, the first of March was the sixtieth day of the new month, they are on no line of the posting, and nobody in that yard is going to tell them, because there is no telling of them that is not an argument and he has lost that argument about four times.** Asked whether that is the wage, a man of about twenty-nine from the settlement's workroom refuses to answer in a yard, and says his book goes nowhere and that a wage is a thing somebody promised and a thing somebody promised has a day on it. **A woman of about twenty-four writes two things down separately and does not join them up.** And a man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says a chest of salt is not a wage and will not pretend it is one, and that about four hundred and forty people on eleven miles of flats put nine measures a day into that same water and are not on a line of that page.

2. **Chapter 0212 — The Third Line In That Book.** Day 212, the fifty-seventh, a Wednesday. The man who keeps the number comes into the room over a yard and washes his hands in the trough by the door before he goes near the trestle, and there are about nine people in that room and a date in chalk on that door. **He writes one hundred and seventy-two, the third line in that book with seven days in it, and the wall in that room has one hundred and fifty-eight for the forty-third day of that month at the top of the column and one hundred and sixty-five for the fiftieth under it, and there is no column anywhere for a week.** The chapter's finding is about a day of the week and not about the number: **the line was due, it was due on a Wednesday, it is a Wednesday, and the two lines before it were both on Wednesdays, and he has not chosen that and has never once known which day of the week his own writing lands on, and the handle was on the figure for as long as he has had the figure and nobody, including him, has ever gone and looked at it.** A man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close asks what it cost and gets *I do not know*, and is told that three lines is everything the man is going to say about that book this morning. **A woman of about fifty-six who keeps a shop four streets down from a market asks *how much is it* and says she has nine prices on slates behind her counter and has never had one of them wrong and has never once been able to check one, and the man answers half of it: a price is a thing a person can be angry about and his number is not a price, and that is why he washes his hands.** A girl of seventeen stands at the rail of that yard for about nine minutes and is not asked anything. A man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall comes to the door, says *you have a Wednesday*, and does not go in. **The line in that book that is not a number is on the page in the narrator for the third time that page has been seen in that room and not asked about, the man did not mention it, nobody asked him, and about four people in that room were waiting for him to say one more thing about that book.**

3. **Chapter 0213 — Two Of The Three.** Day 213, the fifty-eighth, a Thursday. A sack comes down onto a beam and two pans in the yard of the store on the upper road, and the beam was made by a man of about fifty-two who digs out of a length of iron and two lengths of wood and he carried it up nine miles on his shoulder that morning. **He moves the pan four times and says a sack and a ninth of a sack, and says the ninth and not the number so that whoever writes it down has to put a thing in their own hand on it, and he does not write it himself.** A woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road gives the figure back and says the reason he is telling them the ninth is that he would rather have a page with a mistake in it than a whole of good sentences, and that a man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board said that in this yard on the forty-sixth day of the Long Month and did not do it, and she is the one saying it out loud today. **Then he says the thing the sack was in the yard for: a sack is a thing he can put on a beam, a place at a fire is a thing a person can be found at, and a day of the queue is a thing a person can be found in, and about nine people in this city have been carrying a sack past a fire without noticing that a person standing at a fire is a person somebody could go and get, and nobody has ever put that on a page about anything.** Nobody in that yard has a piece of chalk. **The man of about thirty-four who wrote nine of the eleven lines of that page comes in at the eleventh hour and reads the ninth off a slate and does not touch it**, and a woman of about thirty-four from the fourth household tells him that a figure under one line of three says the other two are worth nothing, and asks him to say out loud that he knows it and that he is not going to put it up; about nine minutes later he says the same thing to nobody at all, and that he is not entitled to say it about a fire and a queue. A woman of about twenty-four writes the ninth on a slate of her own and does not leave a gap, because a gap would be a shape and the shape would be a price. The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by a wharf comes up the road and declines to say the thing four people are waiting for her to say. **The beam's iron came off a gate and the wood off a rack and he cannot say whose either, and he will not answer that it is his.**

4. **Chapter 0214 — A Different Hole.** Day 214, the fifty-ninth, a Friday. A rack comes up out of a channel at the top of a cut on the Reach on four people's backs because there is no water in it to float anything, and the channel has been dry eighteen days. **The man of about fifty-two who digs does not put his rod in the hole he has been putting it in and puts it in one nine feet along, and gives the reason out loud at the top of that cut in front of about nine people: yesterday's sack went onto a beam nine miles up the road and a ninth came off it, and he has been standing in a channel for eighteen days waiting to find out whether the salt in that sack came out of this water, and he cannot find out from a rod.** And: **a hole that four people measure is not a hole any more, it is a column about a hole**, and he is going to measure a hole nobody has measured and is not going to tell anybody the answer. One of the four who carried the rack up says what a rack is: four lengths of wood, a rope and four people, about nine of them can be worked and every one of those nine is on somebody's rota, and the settlement's nine are the only nine people in four hundred miles who have been asked whether they want a day. **A woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road says about four hundred and forty out loud, once, in a cut, and attaches a decision to it: on the last Tuesday she is going to ask a settlement nine miles up that road to put a line on a page for about four hundred and thirty-one people, in a room in daylight with a date on the door, because a yard is a thing four people remember and a room is a thing a person can be held to.** A woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder says *you will be refused* and she says yes and that being refused with a date on it is worth that to her and is worth exactly nothing to the four hundred and thirty-one. **Looking down the four miles of causeway she comes nine sentences about the seventh line of the compact and does not finish the ninth, and the ninth was the only one that was not true, and about four people in that cut have said since that she did not say it on purpose and not by accident.** A woman of about fifty who counts water looks into the first hole and not into the second one and says she wants somebody else to have to hold it.

5. **Chapter 0215 — What The Ninth Line Is Worth.** Day 215, the sixtieth, a Saturday, and there is no queue. A man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall puts a slate face down on the boards of his own stall and then turns it over about four minutes later and writes nothing on it, and gives two different reasons for the two things, and lets about nine people work out what he has said about himself: he has written on that slate about four times since the thirty-seventh day of the Long Month and every time somebody told him not to, and a habit and a principle look exactly the same from about four feet away. **Then he says what the ninth line is worth, in four sentences with no figure in any of them: it is worth exactly what the queue was worth before he wrote it down, which was a sack and a place at a fire and a Tuesday, and the page has not changed what it is worth by one grain of salt, and what it has changed is that the ninth line is now the only reason anybody in four hundred miles has for thinking the queue can be taken off the page, and he would take it off the line and is not going to.** A man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board asks *does it work*, which is a different question, and is not answered, and asks *whose is it*, which is a better one, and is not answered either. A man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close works out a cheap test — count how many people come to a stall on a Tuesday to ask whether the queue is still on the page — and says he is not going to write the number down, and that about four people are going to do it anyway and tell him. A woman of about fifty-six who keeps a shop says that every one of the four people who asked her whether the queue was still on the page asked because they were afraid and not one because they wanted to know whether it worked. **A woman of about twenty-four writes one sentence down, then stops, then says there is no finding and that a sentence which says a man said a thing is the beginning of a page about a man.**

6. **Chapter 0216 — A Sack, And Nobody Asks.** Day 216, the sixty-first, a Sunday, and there is nobody building anything on a Sunday. **A man of about forty who carries sacks comes up a bank eleven miles from this city with a sack of salt on a strap, puts it down in a yard at the bottom of the Redroot cut, and does not go up to the door, and it is the fourth he has carried, and he has counted it because that is his trade and not because it is a question, and he asks for that to be written in those words and not in better ones.** A man of about fifty-two who digs comes down the bank for a rod he never had and says why he is there. A woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register is on the step for the third time since the forty-first day of the Long Month with a second slate, and says she has said the one sentence she has to say in a room on the forty-fourth and in a yard on the forty-ninth and on the fifty-fifth and is not going to say it a fourth time, **and that she has been in that yard three times and has not said it once.** The boy of eight unties a rope and ties it again and nobody in that yard says one word about it, and he and the woman of about fifty-nine who keeps that house are the only two people there still doing the thing they did on the eighth day of the Long Month. A man of about forty-four who keeps a stall comes up the bank with a basket he did not need to bring, works out what he would write at the end of a line for a sack, finds there is no figure at the end of that sentence, and does not open a twelfth entry in his column of costs. **The woman of about fifty-nine who keeps that house says the one that was due is the one that was due, that she is not going to say it is the fourth because she does not know that it is the fourth, that nobody in that house has ever counted the sacks and that that is on purpose, and that a count of sacks is a count of what the days bought.** Nobody in that yard asked her the other thing. **The keeper of the Redroot gate, thirty-seven days into a year of being angry, is at the edge of the cut beside that yard at the fourth hour of the afternoon, does not come into it, and says one sentence about the sack and it is where to put it.** The woman of about twenty-nine takes the page she tore out of her own book out of her pocket and reads it and puts it back, and it has the date on the back of it and two names on the front of it and no third, and nobody in that yard asked her who the four are.

7. **Chapter 0217 — A Figure With No Owner.** Day 217, the sixty-second, a Monday, and there is no queue. **A man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board counts on the fingers of his right hand in a market and says seventy-two days out loud, and it is the first figure anybody has put on the delegation of four people and a question, counted from the fifty-fifth day of the month after this one where they were named in a room in Chapter 0145, and he attaches a decision to it: he is not going to say it again and not going to add to it and not going to correct it, and he has just put a column on four people in front of about nine people and heard himself do it.** A woman of about twenty-four writes the figure down and rubs it out, and gives the reason, which is that she does not own it, and that a man of about twenty-nine from the settlement's workroom has a book that goes nowhere and said in the same market that a figure with no owner is a figure anybody can add to. A man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says the reason he did not say the number in a room is not a good one, and that in a yard a number is a thing four people remember and in a market a number is a thing nine people repeat and in a room a number is a thing a person can be held to. **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register is not in that market and is not in that city that day, and nobody in that market asked anybody about her, and the man of about thirty-four says he will not carry it to a fourth place and calls that being useful.** A girl of seventeen stands about nine feet from the man who said the number and says nothing. He buys a quarter of a sack of salt at the fourth hour and pays without hesitating, and about four people have said since that it probably was the only number in the building.

8. **Chapter 0218 — The Man Who Washes The Board.** Day 218, the sixty-third, a Tuesday, **and the third Tuesday the queue has run on counting from the forty-ninth day of the Long Month — which `chapter-0204.md` states is the one it formed on, so the fifty-sixth was the second and this is the third — and the first one it has run on since there was salt in that chest instead of a wage. (This count was wrong in the first version of this summary and in the chapter, both said *second*, and both were corrected in the review repair pass; the chapter is the fact and it now names its own count and its own first day.)** The board is in a trough at the sixth hour with the day before on it, and the man who washes it says the board carries the day before's figures and is written at the seventh hour when he knows what the day was, and that about four people in that yard have been waiting for him to write the difference between two Tuesdays and that he is not going to, because the difference between two Tuesdays is not a thing anybody is owed. **The two Tuesdays in that sentence are the fifty-sixth day of the Long Month and this one, and the Monday between them paid about nine and not about a hundred and forty; the first draft said *yesterday* of the Monday and was wrong.** **Then he says, for the first time out loud, what he has been doing at the front of that queue since the seventh week of this flood: he washes the board because the people who cannot read are the ones a figure on a wall is for, nobody has asked him, nobody has paid him, and he has set himself the question and answered it in a yard on a Tuesday.** A woman of about thirty-four in that store says the date out loud for the second time and says she is not going to say it again for a while. **A man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall stands under the ninth line and finds that it is the only line of the posting that anybody in four hundred miles can stand in front of and say yes or no to, and the only line that was not about anybody's labour, and that a page with one handle on it is not a better page than a page with none.** A woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road says she will not ask in a yard and that the day she chose came and went and she did not do it. **A man of forty-two says one thing he will do instead of the thing he wants said, and that he worked it out on the fifty-fifth day of the Long Month and said nothing about it for eight days.** The man who washes the board writes the day's figures at the seventh hour, washes the board again, and hangs it back up in the same place.

9. **Chapter 0219 — Face Down.** Day 219, the sixty-fourth, a Wednesday. **A woman of about thirty-four in a store on the upper road turns a slate face down on a chest in the back of that store at the eighth hour, and the date on it is the fifty-sixth day of the Long Month and it is eight days old, and it was the only page in this city with a date on it that anybody could read, and about nine people had come nine miles in the last four days to read it.** She does not ask the four people in the back of that store to leave, and when she is asked to put it back up she says no, and gives the reason in her own words: a slate with a day in the past on it is a thing about the past, and there is a sack in that chest instead of a wage, and there is no next date, and she is not going to write one. **A man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says that a date is not a clock, that a clock is a thing that runs on and a date is a thing that happened, that there has not been a clock in this city since the thirty-fourth day of the Long Month, and that about four people in that yard have been calling a slate a clock for seven days because they wanted one — and then admits that he was one of the four, and that he did not read it at all between the forty-sixth and the forty-ninth and has read it about four times a day since the forty-ninth.** **Adrian Vale comes down that road on foot, walks into that yard, and stands about nine feet from a slate lying face down on a chest, and nobody fetches him: about four people in that yard had been waiting since the fifty-fourth day of the Long Month for somebody to say a name and about nine of them heard nothing, and he did not say it himself.** He came because he wanted to see the other half of it, and the other half of it is a woman turning a slate over. A woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by a wharf was not in that yard and nobody sent for her. **A woman of about twenty-four asks whether to write it down and is told no, and writes it down anyway, and neither of them says the word refusal, and the reason the woman of about thirty-four gives for saying no is that she had been on nothing for two hundred days before the forty-fifth day of the Long Month and asked what the date was in four words and was told nothing, and has never asked anybody to write that down, and has not thought about it once until that morning.** A man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board is in that yard at the seventh hour and says nothing at all.

10. **Chapter 0220 — A Page With A Mistake In It.** Day 220, the sixty-fifth, a Thursday, and there is no queue. **A third man stands in front of the stall by a wharf with nothing in his hands and says the sentence wrong in one word, and the word is wall, and there is no wall in this city with anybody's name on it, and he has walked four streets and has never seen a name on a wall he could go and look at.** The woman of about thirty-four who keeps that stall comes out from behind it and says two things, and the second is that somebody will. **Then she corrects it, in a yard, in daylight, at about nine feet from four people, in four sentences, without saying her name, and about four people in that yard were angry and about nine were not, and nobody thanked her and she had asked in advance not to be thanked.** A man of about forty-four who keeps a stall says a third copy would be the second rumour and that he made that distinction up on a Thursday morning. A woman of about twenty-four writes the wrong sentence down and the correction underneath it and does not join them up. **A man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board comes down four streets and says the other half of his own sentence — a page can be corrected by another page and a wall cannot be corrected by anything at all, and a word cannot be corrected by anything at all — and then says he is not going to do anything about it, that he cannot un-say a thing in nine mouths, and that he came four streets to say nothing.** A man of about fifty-two who digs comes down eleven miles to say that he has got nothing, and the yard finds that the only thing anybody said to him all day that was not about a page was a man with a rod saying he has none.

## Volume 05, Batch 0003 — Chapters 0221 to 0230, days 221 to 230

**AND THESE SUMMARIES HAVE BEEN THROUGH THE REVIEW REPAIR PASS OF `reviews/volume-05/batch-0003.md`, and the disposition is `state/batch-summaries/volume-05-batch-0003.md` §17. THIRTEEN PAGE FAULTS WERE REPAIRED ACROSS THE TEN FILES AND NOT ONE SUMMARY BELOW HAD TO BE REWRITTEN, because a summary states what a chapter is for and every repair was inside a chapter: three anchors that pointed at days which had not happened, a central object that had three ages in one chapter, an arithmetic slip in a count of sacks, a four-week interval that stopped on the wrong day against three other places in its own chapter, an impossible chain of Mondays, a duplicated clause, a character who addressed the reader of the book, an interval measured off the wrong day, two empty-section and unmarked-flashback marks, a clause that undid itself, a sentence count that disagreed with itself, and an opening paragraph that denied its own scene. **NOT ONE FIGURE, DATE, ANCHOR, DAY, BEAT, CAST MEMBER, ENDING OR THREAD STATE MOVED, AND NOT ONE OF THESE SUMMARIES IS WRONG ABOUT WHAT ITS CHAPTER IS FOR.** The word count moved from 23,590 to 23,647 and the minimum, median and maximum all moved with it, and those figures are at §17.7 of the batch record and not here.**

**Ten chapters, ten days, one chapter to a day, and no double day. The batch's subject is a *substance* and not a volume: Batch 0001 was a page and Batch 0002 was a day, and this one is what the salt in the settlement's chest does in the fortnight after the last Tuesday a wage came out of it. `outline/volume-05.md` does not exist and this batch did not decide what Volume 05 is. The rules are `bible/power-system.md` §29. The batch ran past day 220 and entered every event in the fourth form and established no fifth.**

1. **Chapter 0221 — It Has Gone Hard.** Day 221, the sixty-sixth, a **Friday**. A bar goes into a quarter of a sack in the yard of the store on the upper road at about the eighth hour and stops, and the man putting it in said he paid a figure for it and is not going to say what it is worth in a yard. The woman of about fifty-six who keeps a shop four streets down from a market has walked nine miles with her own wage under her arm and has put her thumb in the seam of it four times in ten days and has not opened it. **The woman of about thirty-four in that store will not open the chest on a Friday and gives a reason and nobody asks her.** A man of about twenty-seven washes the board and says he is not going to write anything about the sack because that board is about a day and there is no day in a sack. About four people count the knots in the chest and get the same answer four times: four sacks with a knot that is not the knot on the others.

2. **Chapter 0222 — A Lump And Not A Ninth.** Day 222, the sixty-seventh, a **Saturday**. A beam and two pans go down onto a block of stone in the same yard, and the far pan has a crack in it that has been there since the fifty-ninth day of that month, and the man who made it puts his thumb in it and tells about nine people what is wrong with it before he uses it. **A quarter of a sack goes onto the near pan and the beam tells him what it is and cannot tell him what a part of it is.** He has known it since the sixty-sixth day of that month. A man of about forty-four who keeps a stall asks whose wood the pans are and gets a fact about wood and not a fact about a person. **Adrian Vale carries the lump about nine feet in both arms and his hands go white at the prints and says he is the only man in that yard who can be handed a thing and cannot be put on anything.**

3. **Chapter 0223 — Somebody Kept It.** Day 223, the sixty-eighth, a **Sunday**, and there is no queue. A man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close puts a wage he has had for twelve days on the boards of a stall by a wharf and has not opened it, and says that a sack you have opened is a sack you are using and a sack you have not opened is still the wage and he does not know which he is doing. **The woman of about thirty-four who keeps that stall does not touch it.** The woman of about fifty-six says her own nine measures went hard on the thirty-ninth day of that month and she has not opened them either. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says the only thing anybody in that market has done this week he could have put a figure on is a man carrying a wage and not opening it, and he is not going to. **A woman of about thirty-four in that house says no in one word to his asking whether a sack can be left on her boards overnight and then gives four sentences.** And the man of about thirty-one asks how many sacks came out of that chest and nobody in four hundred miles knows.

4. **Chapter 0224 — The Number He Did Not Say.** Day 224, the sixty-ninth, a **Monday**, and the twenty-eighth day the channel has been dry. A rod comes up out of a hole at the bottom of a cut on the Reach dry. **A woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road says the seventy-fifth day of the Long Month out loud at the top of that cut, because the man of about fifty-two who digs said *in about four weeks* in a market on the forty-seventh day of that month and said that four weeks is a direction and not a figure and that about nine people in this city have written the number down instead.** He puts the rod down flat on the cracked bottom instead of in a hole. A man who has carried racks up and down that cut says a rack goes in the water and a rack that is not in the water is a frame and a frame goes in mud and there has been no mud for twenty-eight days, and refuses to say the rest of it in a cut. **A woman of about fifty who counts water does not take a line off a board that says there is no water in that channel because a rod came up dry. A woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road says out loud that she is not going to choose another day in a cut or in a yard and has not chosen one since the fifty-sixth day of that month.**

5. **Chapter 0225 — A Line And No Wage.** Day 225, the seventieth, a **Tuesday**.
