The queue forms for the **fourth** time counting from the forty-ninth day of the Long Month and the **second** time with salt in that chest instead of a wage. **The man of about twenty-seven washes the board and writes nothing and leaves the figures of the day before standing, which was a Monday, and says a blank board is a board that says nothing happened on a day and about nine people did not happen on that Monday.** The woman of about thirty-four in that store does not open the chest and says that a chest opened in a yard is a wage and a chest not opened is not, and that she is not a person who gets to say which. **The man of about thirty-four who wrote the page will not say whether the ninth line works on a morning when there is nothing at the front of it, and has not answered it in a market either.** A man of forty-two comes nine miles and stands at the back of his own queue and does not go to the door. Adrian Vale walks nine miles, is wrong about a queue in front of about nine people and says so, and is not fetched. **A man of about twenty-nine from the settlement's workroom puts the slate he has carried since the forty-sixth day of that month face up on the boards of that yard and says out loud that he is not going to say Adrian Vale's name, with the day he wrote it at the bottom of it and not the day it stops, and picks it up about a minute later.** Nobody fetched anybody.

6. **Chapter 0226 — A Man Carrying Salt.** Day 226, the seventy-first, a **Wednesday**. **Adrian Vale moves eleven sacks along a wall in the back room of that store and does not lift the twelfth, and says that he came up nine miles and has not helped, and that there is no place in this city where a man who is on nothing is told how far down to stop.** The two of them are the only people in that building doing a thing that is on nothing. A man of about twenty-nine stands in the doorway and does not come in. **Adrian Vale takes a piece of paper out of the inside of that coat and puts it back in about the time it takes to turn a thing over, and says it is fifty-eight days and that he does not know whether moving eleven sacks or carrying a piece of of paper is the thing he is better at.** The woman of about thirty-four in that store says she has been putting them in a heap alone for fifteen days and that the kind ones would cost her more than the sacks did. **She says out loud that she put the four re-tied sacks at the bottom of that chest on the fifty-sixth day of that month and has not told anybody, and that the only thing that happened is that she could see all four at once without a slate.**

7. **Chapter 0227 — Four Lengths Of Wood.** Day 227, the seventy-second, a **Thursday**, and the thirty-first day the cut is dry. **A man who has carried racks up and down that cut since the second month of this flood brings a length of wood up a bank and says, in a yard, that it came off a rack, and will not say which rack and will not say the beam.** The man of about fifty-two puts his forefinger in the crack in the far pan and wraps his hand in sackcloth. **A man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board comes up that bank with a bar and a bag and looks at the wood and does not ask for it and does not take it.** The woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder lifts one end of the wood four inches off the boards, puts it down, and does not say why she could not lift the rest of it. The man of about thirty-two says he has never asked anybody for a rack and is not going to start at the top of a cut on a Thursday morning.

8. **Chapter 0228 — Nobody Takes The Wood.** Day 228, the seventy-third, a **Friday**. **The far pan comes apart in the yard with a quarter of a sack sitting in the near one, and the lump goes across the boards and stops against a trestle.** The man of about fifty-two tells the story in the order it happened, that he was asked and then he said it. **The man of about thirty-nine says he has wanted a length of wood since the sixty-sixth day of that month and did not ask for it at the top of that cut on the seventy-second and is not going to ask now, and puts his hand on the pan that is still sound.** The woman of about thirty-four in the store says there is no other length of wood a man can get without taking it off a rack and says that she is not going to be the one who says that out loud a second time in a yard. A man of about thirty-two says he thought about taking one and did not and admits it in a yard. **The man of about fifty-two says he is not going to make another pan, because a beam with one pan on it can only do one of the two things it was made to do, and leaves the beam in that yard.**

9. **Chapter 0229 — The Sack That Did Not Come.** Day 229, the seventy-fourth, a **Saturday**. **Nobody comes up the bank at the bottom of the Redroot cut with a sack on a strap, and about four people in that yard have worked out by the fourth hour of the afternoon that he is not coming and about nine of them had worked it out by the second, and he said on the sixty-first day of that month that he would not be there for the next one.** The woman of about fifty-nine who keeps that house says she said first that she was not going to be the one who asks and is not going to take it back. A boy of eight unties a rope off a wall and ties it again. A woman of about thirty-four in that house says she will not scrub out the wet stripe on the boards. **The woman of about fifty-nine says that if a day when a sack does not come up a bank is written down then the day before it is a day when four men carried four sacks up a bank in the ordinary course, and that in about nine years somebody will read a book about how often sacks come up a bank.** A woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road walks eleven miles to the bottom of that bank and asks for nothing. **The keeper of the Redroot gate is at the top of that cut from first light with the board in her left hand, fifty days into a year of being angry, and says nothing to anybody.**

10. **Chapter 0230 — What Is Left In It.** Day 230, the seventy-fifth, a **Sunday**, and the twenty-eighth day since he said four weeks and the thirty-fourth day since the water stopped. **The man of about fifty-two goes down to the bottom of that cut, says nothing about what he expected to find because he said on the sixty-ninth day of that month that he would not, and says at the bottom of the dry channel that he has never once said what the four weeks was a direction about and that he has let about four people join the water and the four weeks in their own heads for twenty-eight days because it was easier.** A man who has carried racks brings one down and will not put it in, because there is no page in four hundred miles that says which of two is the real one. **Adrian Vale goes down on his own and says that what he saw was a channel and not a sentence and that a date nobody chose did not do what a date on a door does.** The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall comes up the bank and does not answer whether the ninth line works. A man of about thirty-two stands on the bank for the fourth day in a row and says he is not going to be a different one on a Sunday.

## Volume 05, Batch 0004 — Chapters 0231 to 0240, days 231 to 240

**Ten chapters, ten days, one chapter to a day, and no double day. The batch's subject is a wall and a floor and not a volume: Batch 0001 was a page, Batch 0002 was a day, Batch 0003 was a substance, and this one is what a wind and a drying do to the things this city has been keeping still. `outline/volume-05.md` does not exist and this batch did not decide what Volume 05 is. The rules are `bible/power-system.md` §30. The batch ran past day 220 for the second time and entered every event in the fourth form and established no fifth.**

1. **Chapter 0231 — The Board Came Off The Wall.** Day 231, the seventy-sixth, a **Monday**. A board comes off the wall of the store on the upper road in the wind and goes about nine feet and lands face up on the top of that yard, and the man of about twenty-seven who washes it was not holding it. **The last figures written on that board are the figures of the sixty-ninth day of the Long Month and they are seven days old, and he puts it back up and does not write on it, and says the difference between the seventieth and this morning is that one is a man deciding and one is a board coming off a wall.** A man of about forty-two stands under the ninth line with nothing in a Monday yard. A man of about thirty-nine with a bag against his chest for fourteen days comes to the gate. **The top line of the posting has gone pale in about four places and the ninth line is under the date and not beside it.** A woman of about fifty-six who keeps a shop has moved her nine measures off a shelf against an outside wall and will not say where they are. And the man who washes the board says that the board is the day and the posting is the page, and that he only found that out when the wind took the day off the wall.

2. **Chapter 0232 — The Fifth Tuesday.** Day 232, the seventy-seventh, a **Tuesday**. **The queue forms for the fifth time counting from the forty-ninth day of the Long Month and the third time with salt in that chest instead of a wage.** The board is washed and not written on and its figures are eight days old. A man of about twenty-nine puts a slate face up one time on the seventieth and will not put it up a second time, because a thing anybody can look at twice is a thing about the person who looked. A woman of about fifty-six has walked nine miles two mornings running and will not stand in a doorway. A man of about forty-two counts five Tuesdays and two hundred and thirty-one days and will not say what he has worked out about a board. **Adrian Vale gets the trough out from under the wall and empties it in the yard about nine times before the queue is a queue, and is on no rota, and is not fetched.** A man of about thirty-four who wrote the ninth line looks at the bare place where a date was and does not look at his own line.

3. **Chapter 0233 — The Hole That Filled In.** Day 233, the seventy-eighth, a **Wednesday**. **A rod in a hole nine feet from the one everybody measures has come up about four inches and the hole has come up to meet it, and there is a thumb's depth of the flats in the bottom of it.** A woman of about fifty who counts water will not take a figure off it, because a figure is a thing she takes and there is no figure in a heap of dust. A woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder does not clear it out with her hand and gives the reason. The man of about fifty-two who digs comes up the bank, says the hole finished itself, and says he is not going to put a rod in a third one. **A man who has carried racks refuses to ask her to get a hand under a rack because he has said no about wood three times.** A woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road says she comes up on days that are not Tuesdays because a Tuesday is a day about four hundred and forty people are somewhere else on, and would like that written down and not written down, in that order.

4. **Chapter 0234 — Deeper At One End.** Day 234, the seventy-ninth, a **Thursday**. **The floor of the back room is swept for the first time since the fifty-sixth day of the Long Month, by a woman who is on no list in that building and whom nobody asked, and the sweep brings up a groove the whole length of the wall the eleven sacks are along, four inches deep at the end they go down at, with a white crust of salt in the bottom of it.** The man of about twenty-seven says a groove is not a column and a crust is not a figure and will not say his two words in that room. **Adrian Vale does not lift the sack standing in the middle of the floor, and says that a groove is not a hole and a sack in the middle of a floor is not a sack on a rota.** Nobody can say which sack the crust came out of. The man of about twenty-seven sees the slate lying face down on the chest through the doorway and will not go in and turn it over. A man of about forty-four comes up nine miles with a basket he did not need and does not open a twelfth entry in his column.

5. **Chapter 0235 — The Corner Of It.** Day 235, the eightieth, a **Friday**. **The wet stripe on the boards at the bottom of the Redroot cut is not there and nobody scrubbed it out, and about four inches of one end of it are still darker than the boards either side.** A woman of about fifty-nine who keeps that house says she has gone from a stripe to four inches and is not going to say what the four inches are about. A woman of about thirty-four in that house says she is not going to scrub out a corner to make a floor a floor and is not going to leave four inches on a floor either. A woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder has come down the bank after standing at the top of it for an hour. A boy of eight unties a rope off a wall and ties it again. **A woman of about thirty-nine asks a woman of about thirty-four whether it is going to go, which is the first question anybody has asked in that yard in six days, and it is about a corner and not about a sack.**

6. **Chapter 0236 — A Hand Opened Once.** Day 236, the eighty-first, a **Saturday**, in a market four streets from a wharf. A man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close puts a wage he has had for twenty-five days on the boards of a stall and does not open it, and the woman of about thirty-four who keeps that stall does not touch it and leaves a gap about the width of a person. **At the sixth hour his hand opens, on its own, for about as long as it takes to take a board off a stack, and shuts again and does not come open a second time that day, and nobody in that market says a word about it.** A girl of seventeen at the rail reads her slate and stops in the middle of a line, and says a girl of seventeen is not a person anybody comes to with a question, and that she has worked out this cannot go on and is not going to be the one who writes down the day it stops. A woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register comes along the boards with a page in her pocket and will not date it, because a woman asked what a date was in four words on the thirtieth day of the Long Month and was told nothing. A woman of about fifty-six who keeps a shop four streets down from that market has never said one word to him about a wage.

7. **Chapter 0237 — Face Down Against Her Leg.** Day 237, the eighty-second, a **Sunday**, at the top of the Redroot cut, and the cut has been dry for forty-one days. Adrian Vale walks eleven miles with nothing in his hands and sits about nine feet from the keeper of the gate for four hours and says nothing to her. **At the ninth hour the keeper takes her left hand off her board, turns it face down against her leg, and puts her hand back on the outside of it, and does not stop working the channel, and says no word to anybody about it.** Her board is on no list, it carries two dates in two hands and a set of knife marks, and the twenty-fourth is not on it. **She says out loud that there is not going to be a second page going face down from her, and a year is a year and it has not run out, and she says the last three sentences of that to the boards of the bank and not to anybody.** A woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household sees it and says she is not going to say a word about it. A man of about fifty-two who digs says the second hole is not a hole and will not put a rod in a third one. Adrian says he has not asked her and is not going to.

8. **Chapter 0238 — Down The Bank.** Day 238, the eighty-third, a **Monday**, and the seventh Monday in a row a man of about thirty-two has stood on that bank; the six before it were the forty-first, the forty-eighth, the fifty-fifth, the sixty-second, the sixty-ninth and the seventy-sixth days of the Long Month, and the nearest of the six was seven days back. **He goes down the bank, which he has not done since before the fifty-ninth day of that month, because he has said that a cut with nothing in it is a thing a man can stand in.** At the bottom there are three things and he touches none of them. A woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder is at the top and he does not come up to her. A man who has carried racks puts a hand on a rack and takes it off again and does not give the reason he has been holding since the seventy-second day of that month. The man of about fifty-two who digs comes down at the eleventh hour to see what a hole that has filled in looks like to a man who has not been in it.

9. **Chapter 0239 — The Sixth Tuesday.** Day 239, the eighty-fourth, a **Tuesday**. **The queue forms for the sixth time counting from the forty-ninth day of the Long Month and the fourth time with salt in that chest.** The board is washed and not written on and its figures are fifteen days old. **A woman of about twenty-four puts a slate down on the boards of that yard and asks Shall I write this down and nobody answers her, which is the first time in this city that anybody has not.** **The man of about thirty-four who wrote the page comes up nine miles with a piece of chalk in his hand, does not put the date back on the top of that page, and puts the chalk in his pocket, and says what is left of that page is nine lines in two hands with a date gone off the top of it.** Nobody asks him whether the ninth line works. A woman of about fifty-six stands at the gate for a third morning and does not come in. A man of about forty-two goes up that road for the sixth Tuesday and will not say what he has worked out about a board.

10. **Chapter 0240 — Put Down.** Day 240, the eighty-fifth, a **Wednesday**. **A bag of salt comes off the front of the chest of the man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board and goes down onto the boards of that yard, and he has carried it since the sixty-second day of the Long Month, and he does not open it, and he says he put it down and has not decided he is carrying it.** The mark the bag left on the front of his coat is still on the coat. The man of about twenty-seven says he is not going to be the man who puts a bag on a page. **The woman of about thirty-four in that store does not pick it up and says she has not got anybody in that yard who can say whether a wage is a thing a person is given or a thing a person has, and about four hundred and forty people on eleven miles of flats have neither.** Adrian Vale empties a trough nine times, is not fetched, and says the share with the post taken out has been in the inside of that coat since the thirteenth day of the Long Month and that he has taken it out once and put it back in about the time it takes to turn a thing over. **A beam with one pan on it has stood in the middle of that yard with a sack in the pan for twelve days and the man who made it has not been in that yard for twelve days and everything in it has happened without him in it.**

### Chapters 0241–0250 — Volume 05, Batch 0005

**Days 241 to 250 of the flood, the eighty-sixth to the ninety-fifth day of the Long Month. Ten chapters, ten days, one chapter to a day, no double day. The day map was opened from day 1 being a Tuesday and from the Long Month day being day 155 + N, and it was validated against Batch 0004's published table before it was used. The batch record is `state/batch-summaries/volume-05-batch-0005.md`.**

**Chapter 0241 — Nine Feet Round It.** Day 241, a Thursday, the eighty-sixth day of the Long Month. The yard of the store on the upper road, the sixth to the eleventh hour. A sack of salt that a man put down on the eighty-fifth day is still on the boards, and the woman of about thirty-four in that store goes round it four times between the back of the building and the front door inside a quarter of an hour, and the wind has got under a corner of the sacking and lifted it about the width of two fingers. **The man of about twenty-seven who washes that store's board decides he will not throw a bucket of water up the wall above it, and by the eighth hour there is a line in the dirt about the width of a boot going round the sack that nobody made.** Adrian Vale comes in at the eighth hour with the trough full, has to set it down in the open and go round, and does the whole of it without looking down at his own feet. A man of about twenty-nine from the settlement's workroom crosses the yard with a slate under his arm and the wrong side up and stops at nothing. The board's figures are seventeen days old. **Change: a track in the dirt, and nine feet of boards inside it that nobody walks on.**

**Chapter 0242 — Over The Edge Of It.** Day 242, a Friday, the eighty-seventh. The bottom of the Redroot cut, eleven miles. A coil of rope on the top length of a rack goes over the edge at the seventh hour and unrolls down the bank in the time it takes to walk out of a yard, and the woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder comes down after it. The man who carries racks comes down four steps and stops, which is as far as he has gone since the seventy-second day of that month. She rolls the coil on its side and pushes it four inches at a time until it lies along the channel the way rope lies when nobody has opened it, and refuses to carry it up and says why: a rope on a top length is the only thing anybody can use to get a rack into a cut, and if she carries it up she is carrying a tool to a bank. **Adrian Vale comes down the bank at the eighth hour and the wall says one line to him, which is `A thing that has gone over an edge of its own accord has not been given up by anybody`, and it tells him nothing he can act on, and he puts his hand in his pocket and leaves it there.** She says out loud what a rack is, in four statements to nobody in particular. **Change: a bare top length on a rack, and nine feet of rope in the dust at the bottom.**

**Chapter 0243 — What The Gap Filled With.** Day 243, a Saturday, the eighty-eighth. A stall by a wharf, fourth street from a room over a market, the sixth and seventh hour of the evening. A gap about the width of a person that the woman of about thirty-four who keeps that stall has left beside a wage since the eighty-first day of the Long Month has a finger of the flats in the bottom of it, and the girl of seventeen who sweeps those boards will not move the bag to get at it, because a broom going over a wage is a thing a person watches. A man with a basket puts his foot straight down into the gap and catches himself, and the girl does not take his arm. **The woman of about thirty-four puts her hand flat on the top of the bag and takes it off, for the fourth time in four days, and says out loud that the gap is the only ground in that row a broom does not reach and that the wind does not know what a wage is.** A woman of about fifty-six from a shop four streets down stops, looks at the gap, looks at the bag, and says one thing nobody repeats. **Change: grit in a gap, and a strip of front boards that will not be swept.**

**Chapter 0244 — The Only Line.** Day 244, a Sunday, the eighty-ninth. The wall of the store on the upper road, the eighth to the eleventh hour. The posting has one line left that anybody can read from the ground and it is the ninth, in a different hand from the eight above it, which are there and cannot be read; it survived because it was written last with a harder piece of chalk by a hand that leaned on it. The man of about thirty-four who wrote it comes up with a piece of chalk still in his coat from the eighty-fourth and does not use it. **A woman of about thirty-four in that store asks him whether it works and he refuses for the fourth time and gives a new reason: a line is not tested by the man who wrote it, it is tested by the people it was written for, and they have to be able to read it themselves and say so in a yard, and if he says it works then the only person in four hundred miles who knows is him.** Adrian Vale is at the gate with his hands at his sides and nothing to carry, and nobody says a name. The man of about twenty-seven wrings the cloth out once and stops and leaves the wall above the ninth line unwashed. In the back room the eleven sacks along the wall have still never been counted and the woman says she is not going to weigh one. **Change: eight lines gone off a page, and a decision about a wall.**

**Chapter 0245 — Back On The Rack.** Day 245, a Monday, the ninetieth. The bank of the Redroot cut, the seventh to the eleventh hour. A man of about thirty-two comes down the bank for the eighth Monday in a row he has stood on it, goes to the bottom, lifts the coil in one piece at four points, carries it up and puts it back on the top length of a rack that is not his, tied wrong. The rod he put in a crack on the sixty-ninth day of that month is still flat in the crack and he goes past it twice. The woman of about thirty-nine is at the top of the bank on both trips and asks nothing. **The owner comes up at the tenth hour, sees a coil that is not the coil he left, and does not touch it and does not say a word, and the man who tied it says out loud that he does not know how he tied it.** At the tenth hour the man of about thirty-two says to nobody that he is finished and not stopped and is not coming back. **Change: nine feet of rope back on a rack, and a man who says he is finished.**

**Chapter 0246 — Where The Line Went.** Day 246, a Tuesday, the ninety-first. The yard of the store on the upper road, the seventh to the eleventh hour. The seventh day that queue has run on, with nothing to be paid out of the chest. The sack is still where it was put on the eighty-fifth and there is a mark in the boards under it that was not there on the eighty-eighth. The man of about thirty-nine comes up and looks at it and does not pick it up. The man of about twenty-seven washes the board and leaves the figures of the sixty-ninth standing and argues, wrongly and at length, that a date on a board is a day and a figure on a board is what a day came out as. **The queue bends round the north side of the sack and does not stop, and by the eighth hour there are two lines in the dirt, and the woman of about thirty-four in that store is asked whether the queue should go round it and says the queue is not hers.** A man of about twenty-nine from the workroom stands four feet behind Adrian Vale with a slate under his arm and does not say his name; Adrian notices and says nothing about it, because a man who says a word about a man not fetching him has asked to be fetched. The man of about thirty-four who wrote the ninth line comes up and stands at the back and refuses for the fifth time. The man of about forty-two is at the back of a line that is not the one he built. The girl of seventeen crosses the yard and says out loud that a girl nobody can ask is a girl nobody can be angry at, and that she refused a share on the sixty-eighth. **Change: two lines in the dirt where there was one.**

**Chapter 0247 — A Broom.** Day 247, a Wednesday, the ninety-second. The top of the Redroot cut, the ninth to the eleventh hour. A woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road comes up the bank with a broom under her arm, puts it down on the boards about nine feet from the keeper of the Redroot gate and does not say what it is for. The keeper does not stop working the channel, looks at it once, and does not look at it again. **The two of them say out loud, separately and without pressing each other, that a person who sweeps a channel for a keeper has taken a thing over, that the gap in that channel is filling with the wrong thing, that about four hundred and forty people work the flats and not one of them is in this cut, and that the woman has had a number in her mouth for forty-five days and is keeping it off a page.** The keeper works the invert with a stick from the first hour to the eleventh on a day when there is nothing in the channel to work, and keeps a count of it on a board that is on no list. **Change: a broom on some boards that nobody picks up.**

**Chapter 0248 — Dust In A Pan.** Day 248, a Thursday, the ninety-third. The yard of the store on the upper road, the ninth to the eleventh hour. The man of about fifty-two who digs comes up nine miles after twenty days away and finds a finger of the flats on the top of what is in the one pan on his beam, and puts his own coat over it at two corners, and does not turn the pan out. **He gives the reason out loud: a pan in the open that has been standing in the wind since the seventy-fourth has the day on the top of it, and weighing what is under the day is measuring a yard instead of a sack, and he would rather this yard had nothing anybody could check than one thing.** He has said out loud that he is not going to carry the beam home. The woman of about thirty-four in that store comes out, does not come onto the boards, and says the coat is not hers to object to. He says he has not weighed the beam since the fifty-eighth and has not turned the pan out since the sixty-seventh, and that he has used it three times and cannot divide what is in it now. **Change: a coat over a pan, and a figure that is not taken.**

**Chapter 0249 — What He Did Not Ask.** Day 249, a Friday, the ninety-fourth. The top of the Redroot cut, the ninth to the twelfth hour. There are two brooms on the boards, four inches apart, and the second one was not there at the seventh hour. Adrian Vale comes up eleven miles with nothing in his hands, stands for two hours, goes up and down the bank four times and puts his boots in the same four places, and **does not ask the keeper of the Redroot gate a single question, and tells her at the ninth hour in daylight that he came to ask one and is not going to.** At the tenth hour the wall says one line to him, which is `You wrote a rule for yourself about asking. A rule you wrote is still a rule when the person in front of you would answer`, and it does not tell him what the question was. At the eleventh hour the keeper says there is a second one and says nobody brought it, and that a second thing in a place is not an answer, and neither of them will say who put it there. He goes down the bank at the twelfth hour, sits on the boards, and does not go into the cut. **Change: a second broom nobody will account for, and a question not asked.**

**Chapter 0250 — An Island Of Boards.** Day 250, a Saturday, the ninety-fifth. The yard of the store on the upper road, the seventh to the eleventh hour. The track that has been going round the sack since the eighty-sixth and the line the queue bent on the ninety-first come together at the south end of it, and the sack is standing on nine feet of ground that nobody has walked on. The man of about thirty-four who wrote the ninth line stands at the back of a queue that is not his; the man of about twenty-seven washes the board, leaves figures twenty-six days old standing on it, and says out loud that he would rather be looking at the board than at a wall with one line on it, because a board with old figures on it is still telling the truth about the day they are for. The woman of about thirty-four in that store stands at the back of the yard with a pail in each hand and does not go onto the nine feet, and says out loud that she has been asked three times in the ten days that ground has been standing there why she will not step onto it and has answered none of the three, and that she is waiting for the man who put the sack down to say one word about it. **About four people come up that road at the tenth hour and stop at the gate to look at nine feet of ground with a sack in the middle of it, and the man of about twenty-seven says the one thing he is going to say about that sack to the boards and not to her, and it is not a word for a sack.** A man's coat is still on the pan and the wind has got under its hem and lifted it and let it down and the coat has not gone off the pan. **Change: an island. Nobody in that yard counted what was under the sack.** *(An earlier draft of this summary carried a man of about thirty-one going through the Redroot gate with his hand shut and the store woman reporting three questions about that hand in fourteen days. **That was a cut-and-bank paragraph inside a store-yard chapter and that hand belongs to a stall by a wharf and to the woman who keeps that stall**; the review repair pass took it out of the chapter and out of this summary, and **no figure for that hand is on a page in Chapters 0241 to 0250.** See `reviews/volume-05/batch-0005.md` finding 1.)*

---

**Chapter 0251 — A Case Comes Up The River.** Day 251, a Sunday, the ninety-sixth day of the Long Month. The salt wharf from the sixth hour, then a room over a market, to the seventh hour of the evening. A case the size of a bread chest comes off a barge going to Silt Row and goes up nine miles on a handcart and is shut the whole way. The man of about forty who carries sacks has never carried anything that was not a sack and is paid by the load and not by the errand, and his goal for the day is to get it up the road and leave it and not be the man who opened it. **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register copies onto a second slate what was on the outside of the docket, in the order she saw it, and reads it back once and then reads it back a second time in exactly the same words, and about four people in that room noticed the second reading and none of them said a word.** A man of forty-one in a very good coat reads the four lines out in the local language, in plain, and nobody in the room can check a word of them; the board above the store's door is named in its own figures' day, the sixty-ninth day of that month, and has not been written on since the day after them. Adrian Vale is fetched at about the seventh hour by a woman of about twenty-four who was not sent by anybody, and he answers to a name he did not choose in about a second and enters that he did. **Change: a case on a table with a docket tied twice to the handle, and four places on it — a date, a gate, a holder, the days — and about nine people in that city do not know whether *holder* is a word for a thing or a word for a person.**

**Chapter 0252 — Read In A Room.** Day 252, a Monday, the ninety-seventh day of the Long Month. The room over a market, in daylight, with a date in chalk on the door, the ninth hour to the seventh, about nine people inside and about two hundred and forty who could hear out of the hall and the landing. Tamsin Quill is asked to read the four lines and does not do it for about four seconds, and then reads it out exactly, in the local language, without a note and without stopping, and does not put any of it into anything else. **She then names the four places once, slowly — a date, a gate, a holder, the days — and refuses to say them a third time, because a thing said three times in a room is a thing being agreed to, and nobody has agreed to anything.** **She then takes the docket's four lines one at a time: the crossing and the ground adjoining it, which nobody in the room can point at; the days upon every gate between this city and the gates of the west road to be entered in a schedule, which is the line the other three are for and the only one anybody in the room can put a hand on, and which she read in the local language and did not translate; and a company to keep the road, with a word in front of *company* that is not defined on any of the sheets.** Nobody in the room can say who sent it; it is signed by a clerk whose name is in a hand nobody in the room has; the office is nine hundred miles away and the next quarter is not a date this city holds. **In a room with a door of its own at the other end of the same hall, the register-keeper enters the four places on a second slate in the order they were on the page, and the two slates meet in the hallway at about the fourth hour of the afternoon because the man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board carried them, one in each hand, in front of two hundred and forty people.** The columns agree. Then Adrian writes nine words on a slate and does not read them out, and the slate goes to the other end of the hall and is read there by the person who is the account of the document. **Change: the four columns exist, and a page with a person's name on it is a description, and a description is a removal with a column for where the person is, and nobody in the room improved on it, and the finding of the day is that two slates had to be carried between two rooms to be compared and nobody in either room thought that was remarkable.**

**Chapter 0253 — The Queue Runs And The Ninth Line.** Day 253, a Tuesday, the ninety-eighth day of the Long Month. The yard, the door and the wall of the store on the upper road, the seventh hour to the sixth, in weather, about a hundred and twenty people through one door. Jonah Pike, forty-two, on a page for the first time since Chapter 0097, hands the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall a printed sheet with a wage on it before he says a word, which is his tell and which the chapter does not explain. **The queue bends round the trough and round nine feet of ground with a sack standing in it, and neither bend was announced.** The man of about twenty-seven washes the board and leaves figures twenty-nine days old standing on it and has stopped washing the wall, and the wind has been at that wall for twenty-four days, and he says nothing whatever about the ninth line nine inches above the board he has washed every morning for fifty-two days. **Jonah says three things about the docket and is right about two: it is correct, and it does not name the store, and a document that does not name a thing is not a thing that takes it. The third is that the Crown does not do a thing like that for nothing, and he cannot finish it, and he says out loud in that yard that he is not going to guess in a yard.** The trestle count is two hundred and thirteen on the two hundred and fifty-third day of this flood, in narration, dated, with its anchor named in the same sentence. A woman of about thirty-four in that store, on no list in it, tells him in four sentences that a copy of a page is a page and a thing that is not on a page is not a document. **Change: he decides to take a copy of the docket up the road to nine people he can name without a page, and then he puts it down on the boards of the stall and leaves it there.**

**Chapter 0254 — Nobody In The Room Can Say Who Sent It.** Day 254, a Wednesday, the ninety-ninth day of the Long Month. A room with a door, a date in chalk on it, about the ninth hour, about nine people. Hettis Roke, forty-four, of the Hearthguard of the outer ward, nineteen years in, has been given the question in writing four days beforehand, and nobody asked him whether the day suited him, and he turns a waxed slate over and turns it back and says he wants it entered that he cannot say yes and cannot say no and has got nothing to point at. **The man of forty-one in a very good coat says Regent Solenne Dary out loud in the local language, in daylight, with a date on the door, and gives her nine sentences in his own mouth: a quarterly return in her hand shows a district with a population on it and no rota on it and no share on it and no name on any roll she can find, and a road kept by a body that answers to nothing and cannot be counted is not a road, it is a rumour, and in a bad season a rumour kills people before it has been found out to have been one.** He does not give the figure and says why. What she did not say is that the days on a gate are the Crown's to schedule, because nobody asked her. **Roke answers with five figures and no feeling at all — eight, every year, nineteen years, four times a year, nobody — and the register-keeper writes all five on a second slate and does not join them up, and a girl of seventeen copies them onto a third slate in her own hand because she was asked to.** **Change: a Regent's name is on a page in a room, and the first sentence of this volume's argument with the Crown is a gatekeeper's, and it is about a roll of the dead of a gate that went to an office four times a year for nineteen years, and a count of eight written in a clerk's book on a quay every quarter, and nobody came.**

**Chapter 0255 — The Hundredth Day.** Day 255, a Thursday, the hundredth day of the Long Month, and the last day the fourth form can carry. A shop four streets down from a market and its back room, the seventh hour to the sixth, in wind. A woman of about fifty-six has nine measures of salt under her counter that have gone hard since the thirty-ninth day of that month and has not opened one and cannot break one. **A measure that has gone hard is a block, and a block does not have a part of it that a shopkeeper can call a part, and she moves all nine up onto the counter in two goes and down into the back room in one, with her arms and not on a board, and breaks none and opens none and does not put a thumb into one to see whether the middle has gone soft.** The man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board stands in the back door and watches and does not offer to carry any of the nine and gives a reason about a Tuesday queue, and nobody argues with him and nobody thanks him. At about the fourth hour of the afternoon she puts a chalk next to a page in her day-book and writes nothing, because the hundredth day of the Long Month is the hundredth day of it and tomorrow is a hundred and one and there is no hundred and first day of anything in this world. **She carries the day-book to a market and says the three words *there is no form*, and a woman of about twenty-four says a figure out loud — ninety-four gates between this city and the gates of the west road — which nobody in that market can check, and about four people write it down.** **Change: the hundredth day of a month ended without a date on the head of a page, and a number nobody in four hundred miles can check is on four people's hands, and a notary of fifty was told about it before the sixth hour and said she would do it in a room and not in a market.**

**Chapter 0256 — The Hundred and First.** Day 256, a Friday, the first day of the Short Month, and the hundred and first day of the Long Month by the form that ended the day before. A room with a door, daylight, a date in it, the ninth hour to the sixth, about nine people and about two hundred and forty in the hall and the landing. A woman of about twenty-four puts a date at the head of a page and stops. **Sera Vail, a notary of fifty, cannot certify a day, and before she says anything she says what naming one will cost in nine sentences of her own: a name invented by a notary is a name an office can use, and in about four months a clerk nine hundred miles away is going to write it at the top of a page, and she has been the only person in this city keeping a count of her own counting since the fortieth day of this flood and she is tired of it.** A woman of about seventy from a village of nine households that has refused four times refuses first and gives a reason that is hers, and the refusal is entered in her words and does not stop the naming. **The name is the Short Month. It is sixty days long. The hundred and first day of the Long Month and the first day of the Short Month are this morning, and both are true, and a page that says one of them is wrong.** About four minutes later the wall says one thing and it says it once, plain, in its own block: *A name given to a thing so that the thing can go in a column is not the name of the thing.* **She refuses it in about four seconds and says what refusing it has cost, which is that she has just invented a name in a room and she is going to keep the name, and the count of refusals said out loud in this city moves by one, and the two counts in two books that have never been made smaller are not made smaller by it.** A girl of seventeen reads the date, the name and the reason back twice from a plain slate that is not her own, and then writes a date at the head of a page in the new form for the first time. **Change: a form exists that did not exist yesterday, and on the same table the docket from nine hundred miles away lies with a date a month away from the one the girl wrote, and both are correct, and only one of them was chosen by a person.**

**Chapter 0257 — A Safe Route.** Day 257, a Saturday, the second day of the Short Month. The room over a market, about the second hour of the afternoon to the ninth, about nine people. The man of forty-one in a very good coat puts a second sheet on the table, and there is a word on it that is attached to nothing, and about four people in the room can read the line about keeping the road and the rest cannot, and the man who cannot read it has to decide whether it is safe. **He reads four lines out in the order they are written and asks the room to say the word *safe* out loud and attach it to something, and nobody does, and the word is not defined on any of the five sheets.** There are two ways to keep a road open while a schedule is made. **The safe one is eleven weeks of the days of three villages on the gates of the road itself, and the mechanism is the one already on a page and is not reinvented: a rack stands in a channel, a channel takes its water from a gate, a rack that has not been worked does not come off.** He says the sentence that is his own and says that the reading of the sheet is the Crown's words and not his, and that this is the Crown's reading and the Crown has not asked either. A woman of about twenty-four writes the two words *safe* and *villages* on two lines of a wall in a room over a yard and does not join them up. **Eleven weeks is his figure and it is not converted into days and the chapter says so in one sentence and gives no reason.** The man of about thirty-nine goes to the door in the middle of it and looks down the road and comes back in and sits down. **The name of the person who will have to do the asking is Alys Kern, keeper of a gate on the road west, and the stretch of that road with houses on it is called the Hearth Marches, and she has not been asked anything and nobody has been sent.** Change: a safe route is on a page and nobody has been asked and a person has a name.

**Chapter 0258 — Nobody Can Enter The Holding Company.** Day 258, a Sunday, the third day of the Short Month. The room over a market and the step at the back of it, the tenth hour of the morning to the eighth hour of the evening, about nine people. The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register has a posting in front of her with nine lines on it and a compact behind it with eleven, and she is asked which of the two a company goes on, and she says the cost before she says anything else because she has done that three times. **She reads the eight above the ninth in order off a slate the man of about thirty-nine copied off the wall nine miles up the road that morning, and stops, and says what the ninth is in her own words and does not read it, because a line written last on purpose by a man of about thirty-four is not a line a woman stands up in a room and reads out; the wall itself is never in a room. She gives the count of the compact and its order: the ground, the workway, the labour and its posting, the refusal, the fourth line that cannot be administered, the salt, the road, and the store on its queue; then a person may be found; then a share; then a date and two commons, which is the only line of the eleven that says who made it. The posting carries nine of those eleven and the two it does not carry are the ninth and the tenth, and its order on the wall is not the compact's order: the first seven in the compact's order, the date and the two commons as the eighth, and the store and its queue written last at the bottom as the ninth. She says both numbers out loud and then refuses to say which wall or which page either belongs to without the page in front of her. Not one line of either is a company.** Adrian objects on the page, and he is right, and the register-keeper reads his written sentence out because she is the account of the document, and about four people in that room did not want it said. The alternative is stated by a person in a mouth: **a clerk nine hundred miles away will write the word, and the word that gets written first is the word that gets used.** Jonah Pike counts the willing people on his fingers out loud, one through nine, in a yard, and the count is nine, and the register-keeper objects that a count of who said what is a roll, and he says she is right, and the count stands and the objection stands. **The company is entered on the second slate by the name of the place it keeps, and the place is Hallowgate, and a gate is a place and not an office and not a district, and there is a keeper at that gate and the keeper has not been asked anything.** The people from the other side are on that roll one line each, by the name each said out loud in a room in daylight with a date on it, with no class on it, as named guests under the compact read out in a hall nine miles back down that road in the second month of this flood, whose fifth line is that a guest may ask to stop being a guest — and the chapter says aloud that it means that one and not the other. **A guest is the only instrument in four hundred miles that a person can end by asking and a company of guests is not, and that is the finding.** A wage paid out of a store by people who owe it is not a favour, a wage paid out of a pocket is a favour, a favour is a thing that stops, and about nine people will be paid out of a pocket this week. Change: a company is entered and cannot be posted, and the posting is not amended and the ninth line does not move.

**Chapter 0259 — Nobody Will Carry It West.** Day 259, a Monday, the fourth day of the Short Month. A room over a market, daylight, a date on the door, about the fourth hour of the afternoon, about nine people, and the question was given in writing four days beforehand to everybody in the room. **Hettis Roke says no before the sentence is finished, and his reason is nine words and is the whole of it: nobody here carries a Crown case but the Hearthguard.** He will not put the coat on a bench and take the case up the road as a man who is not a Hearthguard, because a case carried that way is a case carried by nobody and the Hearthguard's share of a thing is the only part of it anybody can refuse. The room waits for the rest of it and there is no rest of it. **A woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road is in the room all day and says nothing, and at about the fifth hour of the afternoon she writes something on a slate without speaking, and it is a written asking, dated four days ahead, that the case is to be read to the three villages by somebody who is not carrying it, and nobody in four hundred miles has ever asked for that. She is a different woman from the woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by a wharf, who is in the next chapter and writes a question: the four women of about thirty-four in this manuscript are four women, and this is the standing map.** The register-keeper enters the asking on her second slate in a second hand and reads it back once and does not improve on it. Adrian reads the entry twice and says nothing and nobody asks him. The man of forty-one in a very good coat says he could read it to them himself and that a man in a good coat is not a person three villages will take an instruction from, and that he has not tried and is not going to try inside a hundred days. **Change: a refusal that costs a season's worth of being told nothing, a written asking that goes into a book and out of a room and is the only thing in this batch that leaves a room, and a case that does not go up the road.**

**Chapter 0260 — The Question Goes Into A Kitchen.** Day 260, a Tuesday by the founding rule and stated on no page, the fifth day of the Short Month. **A kitchen up the road past this city and past the last of the houses out here, and no chapter of Volume 06 puts a number on that walk: the only distances in this world are nine, eleven and four miles and the Marches are at none of them.** In daylight, the tenth hour of the morning to the sixth. Adrian Vale walks the whole way with nothing in his hands, sits down at a table and asks nothing. **A keeper of a gate packs the side of a channel at the end of that kitchen with a bar and a bucket of rubble from first light and does not stop for him, and she says no word all day, and she shares no verb, no tool and no phrase with the keeper of the Redroot gate, which the volume has ruled out of every chapter and which a string search could not have found.** A woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by a wharf is at the table; she walked in before him and the chapter does not explain how; she has a piece of paper in the front of her coat with nothing on the top of it. **He gives the four conditions of the instrument in his own hand — the question in the person's own words, in writing, to everybody who will be in the room, four days before — and states that the instrument has been used twice in this city, once on a Hearthguard of forty-four on a day he did not choose, which produced a no and an entry, and once at a meeting-ground where the first thing it did was stop the man who wrote it, and that the man who wrote it was him.** He did not ask the question the wall put to him on the ninety-fourth day of the Long Month and the chapter does not say he was right. **She writes the question on the piece of paper in her own hand and does not sign it, and the reason is hers and it is nine years old: she has had a name at the top of a page once in nine years and did not choose the page, and she has a slate face down on a chest that she has not turned up.** **Change: he is not going out on that road again until the question is in a hand that is not his, and the day it goes into a room is a day he does not choose and he is not in that room, and he walks the whole way back with nothing in his hands.**

---

## Volume 06, Batch 0002 — Chapters 0261 to 0270, days 261 to 270

**Appended by the review repair pass. The batch record is `state/batch-summaries/volume-06-batch-0002.md`, §14 is the pass, and the figures below are the ones on the page after it.**

**Chapter 0261 — Which Gate Is Which.** Day 261, a Wednesday, the sixth day of the Short Month. A room over a market, from the third hour of the morning to the sixth, about nine people. A docket from nine hundred miles away wants the days upon every gate between this city and the gates of the west road in a schedule, and the room cannot start the first line because it does not know where the first gate is. **A smith of about sixty comes in uninvited and says he hung a hinge on a gate out past the last named house twice, in the second week of the dry spell and the fourth, was paid both times, was asked nothing either time, and sends his apprentice instead of answering.** A boy of about thirteen goes out on the smith's errand with a hinge, a length of chain and a bar of tallow and a knotted string on the cart rail, and comes back with **six answers, two of them about a turn, not one of them a name, and four knots of which he is sure of two and only knows he has passed two.** A man of about fifty-five at the first gate has a bar in his hands and a chain he put on because a cart came through in the night in the first month, has never been asked anything, and says *do not come back with it in a book*. A woman at the last gate with a key on a string asks him whether the chain is for the gate or the cart, and he tells her, and she says it is long, and he says it is, and he turns round. **Change: the second of the four places on the docket is unstartable, and the only list this city has of that road is four knots in a string in a pocket, and the woman of about twenty-four will not name two gates she could name because a room that knew which gates were which and did not send the list is a worse sentence than a room that does not know.**

**Chapter 0262 — Four Hands And Nobody Holding It.** Day 262, a Thursday, the seventh day of the Short Month. The room over a market, seven hours. The case is shut on the table and nobody touches it. A man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall says out loud that he has copied the four lines for about four people and will not give the number or the names, and puts a piece of chalk on the table and leaves it there. **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register announces the cost of refusing, then announces she is going to put a number on her second slate, and then does not, and the reason she gives is that a list of who was not asked is a list with a name in it, which is Hettis Roke's sentence from the fourth day of the Short Month, and the room took three days to come round to it.** She then writes one line anyway: the day, the question, and that the room did not answer it — **no name, no figure — and says in a mouth what she bought with it, which is that a question with a day on it and no name on it can only be answered by somebody who was in the room.** The man of about thirty-four says nothing at all for five hours. **Change: a correct page is now in several hands, the number of hands is in a mouth and on no page, and the register-keeper has made herself findable on purpose for the first time in nine days.**

**Chapter 0263 — The Man Who Opens It.** Day 263, a Friday, the eighth day of the Short Month. The first gate on the road out past the last named house, the fourth hour of the morning to the seventh of the evening. A man of about fifty-five unhooks a chain over a pulley with a glove off in the cold, lowers an oak arm shod with iron onto two stones, and tells a reader that a gate on this road is open for about a minute and a half a day and that he has never charged one of about nine people a week. **A loaded cart loses a wheel to a frozen rut; the man of about forty who carries sacks is under it before the load stops leaning, the gatekeeper gets both arms round the axle end, and the pin that comes out is bent about a finger's width and he will not hit it harder.** The gatekeeper says he keeps it because he opens it, that nobody has ever handed him a better reason, that there is a page in a room over a market with a place in it for a name and that he is the name, that he has not been asked and will not volunteer, and **then asks for one thing: do not come back with it in a book.** Half a mile on there is a gate nobody keeps and a woman hanging washing, and the reason the first gate has a chain is that anything came through in the night once. **Change: the third place on the docket has a candidate, and this city has decided not to put him in it, and about four people here have worked out that a refusal to name him leaves a page nine hundred miles away free to send somebody who will.**

**Chapter 0264 — Everybody Here Already Knows.** Day 264, a Saturday, the ninth day of the Short Month. A market town out past the last named house, fourth hour of the afternoon to the ninth of the evening, the market on. A man of forty-one in a very good coat comes down on a salt cart he did not pay for, with four of the town's eleven walking him as far as the first gate and no further. **A company of eleven keeps the market and there is no other word for it; a bar goes round; whose week it is is not written down and, the man of about thirty-six says, does not need to be, because every one of about nine houses knows without asking, and he can walk from the drain to the north gate in about four minutes and the bar is on the near side or the far side of the road and that is the whole of how anybody here knows anything.** He tells the stranger the cost of writing it down before he is asked: a bar on one side of a road is a duty, a duty written down is a rota, a rota is a page with a name on it, and about nine people in that town have never once been on a page. Two villages past it, the second one nine hundred paces on, nine houses, no company, and a channel dry for four weeks that takes its water from a gate in the road whose post has been out since the second week. **He gives a complete answer to what it costs — a post, about nine days of somebody's hands, a bed dug by hand, the fourth household's rack for that week, four days of a whole household's water carried by hand, and nothing at all that anybody in four hundred miles can put in a column — and says a person who cannot be given a cost cannot be made to answer for it. Change: the whole of the Crown's difficulty out there is a difficulty of handwriting, and the man in the good coat opens no sheet and thanks nobody.**

**Chapter 0265 — Nine Days Of Hands.** Day 265, a Sunday, the tenth day of the Short Month. A channel bed at the second village, the sixth hour of the morning to the fifth of the afternoon, about twenty pairs of hands. A footboard over about four feet of clay that has not been turned in a hundred days, a barrow, a spade, and a rack at the low end that has not been worked and does not come off. **The woman of about thirty at the stall walks the nine hundred paces down the road with a pot on her head and gets nine houses sending down a pail line at about the eighth hour, nobody ordered it, and says out loud that she made the line herself and will not be thanked for it.** The man of about forty-three of the fourth household goes into the hole with the footboard and is in it an hour and four minutes, which is the length of four men agreeing about a barrow handle, and comes out with his left arm not doing what it is told. The woman of about forty-six of that household is in the hole with him for the last twenty minutes with both hands on his collar and asks nobody for anything. They get him up the lane on a door off its own hinges, **and about nine hundred buckets of the household's water and nine days of hands do not come out of the hole.** He says it out loud so nobody else has to, and she says what she has and not what she wants, and she refuses to say what the household's days are and gives a reason that is hers and nine years old. **Change: the loss is said aloud by a house for the first time, and a pail line that nobody ordered is the last thing anybody will be able to do on that channel before the frost.**

**Chapter 0266 — What A Household Has.** Day 266, a Monday, the eleventh day of the Short Month. A kitchen at the low end of the channel with no paper in it, the fifth hour of the morning to the seventh of the evening. A man of forty-three cannot lift with the left, feeds the fire with his right, and counts in the hole that it is nine days, which is the figure the man from the town gave on Saturday about a post and one that the two of them arrived at separately and have never discussed. A boy of about eleven stands in the doorway for four hours with a yoke. **The woman of about forty-six says a household's days are not a schedule — a schedule is a set of days with a thing held against them and every day in it is the same as every other day in it — and that theirs are a set of things that happen in a set order, and the order moves with the weather and with how many hands there are and with whose child is awake, and nobody could stand at the end of a week and put a number against Monday and have it be right on Monday.** She will not give the days. A woman from the top of the lane gave nine buckets on Sunday and would not say so twice, which is the model. **The woman of about thirty at the stall comes down the lane at the tenth hour to say what happened on Sunday, and takes back what she said about it at the fourth hour of Sunday — she said it to a stranger and it was not true — and says the line ran itself, was not organised, was not written down and was not hers, and asks for it to be entered that nine houses moved about nine hundred buckets by hand and not one person in the village was told to.** She leaves a full yoke standing against that house's wall. **Change: the volume's own sentence is said in a woman's own mouth in a kitchen with no paper in it, it is not Chapter 0154's sentence and is not that sentence said differently, and the household is the finding of day 294.**

**Chapter 0267 — Eight Names And One Person.** Day 267, a Tuesday, the twelfth day of the Short Month. A room over a market in the market town, a date in chalk on the door, the ninth hour of the morning to the sixth of the evening, about nine people, four of them from this city and five not. The woman of about twenty-four reads the count and the fifth line of the compact and refuses the other ten, and the fifth line is that a guest may ask to stop being a guest. **A man of about thirty-six of that town says the name of the gate out loud, in daylight, with a date on the door, and the gate's name is the only word above the nine names.** Nine people say their names and the woman of about thirty writes them one to a line, and one is spelled two ways and the spelling is a thing a clerk does. A woman of about thirty at the stall says there is no word for the eight and there is not going to be one made in the room. **A wage is an amount, a day to pay on and a name to pay, and the page has only the name, and about four offices that do not exist stand between the page and the person, and the people the schedule was written for are not frightened of it because a thing a person has never had does not frighten anybody when it is taken away.** At the fourth hour a man of about twenty-six stands up, gives his name, and says he is not going to be a guest any more, in nine words, and no reason is asked for and it is not asked twice. **Change: the roll goes up on a wall with nine names and no class on any line and no word above them but a gate, and it cannot be counted and it cannot be posted a wage, and the person who asked to stop has no line.**

**Chapter 0268 — The Rain Got At It.** Day 268, a Wednesday, the thirteenth day of the Short Month. A market town, the second hour of the afternoon to the eighth of the evening, four kitchens and a lane. A carter of about thirty-five stops to water two mules, the rain gets under a tarpaulin, and **the fifth of five sheets — the one with four places ruled on it and nothing written in them — is in a woman's of about twenty-four's hands and is read out in four kitchens between the second hour and the sixth, and nobody in four hundred miles sent it.** A woman of about thirty-three at a bread board with flour to the wrist and four children asks what the third place is out loud before anybody offers it, and is told it is a holder, and is told that the thing it is asking for is out here, a bar that goes round, and that writing it down makes a name on a page. **The woman of about twenty-four reads only the four places, refuses to keep the sheet, and says a page that is held still is a page somebody is keeping and she is not keeping it.** A man of about twenty-six who asked on Tuesday to stop being a guest hears about it on Wednesday and says a guest is the only thing in four hundred miles a person can end by asking, and a page that came into the village off a cart that nobody sent could not be ended by anybody and did not have to be. **Change: the asking a Hearthguard put in writing on the fourth day of the Short Month — that the case be read to the three villages by somebody who was not carrying it — has been answered by accident, and what has been answered is that the asking was for somebody to be answerable, and four people in four kitchens have each been answerable for a quarter of an hour and none of them knows the other three exist.**

**Chapter 0269 — The First Way, On The Ground.** Day 269, a Thursday, the fourteenth day of the Short Month. Three villages out past the last named house, the fourth hour of the morning to the ninth of the evening. A man of forty-one in a very good coat walks the road with about eleven people and **asks the first gate for eleven weeks of the days of three villages, in the Crown's words and not his own, and says the word *safe* once and refuses to say it again.** A man of about forty-two with two mules and nine sacks is stopped by the road without being asked and says he has nine sacks to move and about four hours of light and that not one of his difficulties is the road, and that he has not said no and has said nothing at all, and would like both of those put down if anybody is putting anything down. **At the second village the word has arrived before the road, on a cart of kindling, said at a door by a woman of about thirty-three who does not know where she had it, and by the time the man in the good coat gets there the word *safe* and the word *villages* have been put next to each other in about nine mouths, and in a room over a yard four hundred miles back down the road those two words are on a wall on two lines and have not been joined and are not going to be joined by anybody in this city.** The gate there is a post, a chain and no bar, and the reason there is no bar is that the bar has been lying in the ninth house since the post came out at the bottom. **Change: the first way is on the ground at two gates and nobody out there was asked anything, and a question goes to everybody four days ahead and a road is not a room and a road cannot supply four days.**

**Chapter 0270 — The Third Thing.** Day 270, a Friday, the fifteenth day of the Short Month. A channel at the second village, the seventh hour of the morning to the sixth of the evening, about twenty pairs of hands. **The bar comes off the floor of the ninth house and is laid across the chain and not hung on the post, because the post is out at the bottom and there has been nothing to hang it on since the second week, and the water comes down a foot of the channel by the second hour of the afternoon and the man of about thirty-six says he put it there yesterday because a man in a good coat stood nine paces off it and asked him to, and he has never in nine years done a thing to that gate because a stranger asked him to do it, and he did it, and he has not slept about it.** The rack at the low end does not come off. **The woman of about forty-six works out what the road has actually been kept by for about a hundred and forty years: a bar bent about a finger's width, a post out at the bottom standing in the water, and a person who is not the last one to have held it, and the man who bent it has not had the turn for about nine years, nobody has ever asked him, he is not on a page and there is no page — and not one of the three is on any of five sheets and not one of them is anybody's fault.** Tamsin Quill arrives with a book of Crown Law in a bag at the bottom of it and does not open it. **There is a second way of keeping a road on the fifth sheet and it is a reading entered in a register under the name of a person who can be shown to have made it, and somebody has to be the name, and she is the only person in that village who can read a clerk's hand and is not a household, and Adrian Vale asked her about it and did not say what it would cost her, and she says a thing you know and do not say is a thing you have chosen, and that he did not do it on purpose, and that that is worse.** **Change: the Crown has now written down two ways of keeping that road and neither of them is the third thing, and the fracture is on a page and is unpaid, and Chapter 0272 pays it and this chapter did not.**

## Volume 06, Batch 0003 — Chapters 0271 to 0280, days 271 to 280

**Appended by the review repair pass, which found that this file and `state/character-state.md` carried no entry for the ten chapters of this batch. The batch record is `state/batch-summaries/volume-06-batch-0003.md`, §14 is the pass, and the figures below are the ones on the page after it. Chapters 0271 and 0278 are the only two of the ten that the repair edited.**

**Chapter 0271 — The Seventh Of Nine.** Day 271, a Saturday, the sixteenth day of the Short Month. A kitchen at the low end of a channel nine hundred paces past a market town, with no paper in it, from the seventh hour of the morning to about the second hour of the afternoon. The man of about forty-three of the fourth household gets up off the bench and reaches for the pot with his left hand and the pot goes over about nine inches, and the arm that went into a hole in clay at the fourth hour of the afternoon of the tenth day of that month, a Sunday, is back and is not the arm it was. **He says it does not do what it did: he can put a thing on a shelf and he cannot lift about four buckets, and he takes the sieve out of his wife's hands for a minute and a half and will not be thanked and will not be given the pot.** The woman of about forty-six gives him one Saturday and refuses the shape of it — on Monday they were two people and a sieve, on Saturday they are two people and a sieve and a woman with a butt and a boy, **and the same Saturday is not the same Saturday twice and will not be the same Saturday next week, and the whole of the Crown's page wants it to be the same Saturday twice.** A boy of about eleven fills his own pails at the end of the lane because nobody in the fourth house is going to bring them in, and nobody said the hand was back. **Change: a hand comes back and the household's week is not a schedule, and a man who says he is not going back to the channel says he is not refusing anything.**

**Chapter 0272 — There Is A Fifth.** Day 272, a Sunday, the seventeenth day of the Short Month. A room over a market in a market town, a date in chalk on the door, about four hours, a woman of about twenty-four and a man and a woman on either side of one table. **Tamsin Quill names the price of the second way out loud: a name on a register is a name in a book, a book is kept, a name in a book can be looked up, there is no office between that table and the place the days are supposed to go, and so the person who enters it becomes the name the road is held in, which is the third of the four places on the Crown's page.** Adrian Vale withdraws the asking at the sixth hour before he sets out, refuses to apologise, refuses forgiveness, and says he will not be forgiven and is not asking. **She then says there is a fifth condition, that there always was, that the four written down are about the question and not one of them is about the man asking it, and that she is not going to hand it to him.** He walks back up the road at about the sixth hour with the sheet in his coat and the bag not on his arm, and neither of them says anything about Friday. **Change: the consent fracture is paid by a withdrawal and is not mended, nobody said it was a misunderstanding, and a thing that has been paid is not the same as a thing that has been mended.**

**Chapter 0273 — A Heading Off A Sheet.** Day 273, a Monday, the eighteenth day of the Short Month. A room over a market in this city with a date in chalk on the door, from about the tenth hour of the morning. A man of forty-one in a very good coat has walked the whole road with a sheet folded inside his coat and has not opened it on a gatepost. **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register writes a word of two syllables with a hard break in the middle of it on her second slate, reads it back once, and puts nothing under it and her name beside it, and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall sits in that room with a piece of chalk for six hours and does not use it and is not asked to.** The first way is being kept in three villages and no page in four hundred miles can require anybody to keep it. **Change: a heading is on a Crown page, and a heading is the strongest thing on a page because a clerk files under a heading, so the four places under it stop being a request made by somebody and start being a form somebody expects back.**

**Chapter 0274 — Nobody To Hand It To.** Day 274, a Tuesday, the nineteenth day of the Short Month. A market town nine hundred paces past the last named house, from about the seventh hour of the morning, and a room over that market with about nine people in it and a date on the door four days off. The instrument goes in the writer's own words, in writing, to everybody who will be in the room, and **it went out on the Saturday four days before the Wednesday to four places, and three of the four are a body, a household and a stall, and a company that keeps a place has no words of its own, and the man who wrote it found that out on the day before the room and had already sent it.** The only recipient who can answer is a person. **Change: a body is not a person, the rule that has gone from household to household for about four hundred years and has no name has a hole in it, and the hole is exactly the shape of a body.**

**Chapter 0275 — Four Sentences.** Day 275, a Wednesday, the twentieth day of the Short Month, in daylight, with a date in chalk on the door, about nine people. **The volume's midpoint. Hettis Roke, forty-four, of the Hearthguard of the outer ward, nineteen years in, refuses the first way on the Hearthguard's behalf in four sentences — the instrument has been used twice in this city and on two different days and a question gone to everybody in writing four days early cannot be changed afterwards; the first time this city used it, it stopped the man who wrote it, in front of two hundred and forty people, and the man who wrote it is standing in that room; the second time it worked and what it worked at was a Hearthguard of forty-four and it produced a no out of a man who had not been asked anything — and then one more sentence for the route itself: a rack that has not been worked does not come off, and a rack stands in a channel, and a channel takes its water from a gate, and eleven weeks of a channel without a household's water is eleven weeks of a salt season that is not recovered in this flood or the next one.** The room measures the sentences and the woman of about twenty-four says *that was three sentences*. **Nobody in that room told Adrian what he had built and he worked it out alone in a yard at about the fourth hour of the afternoon, and the chapter says he worked it out and does not say he was right.** **Change: three villages lose eleven weeks of drying, eleven weeks is a figure in words and not a number of days, nobody has said how many people are in them, and nobody in that room is wrong.**

**Chapter 0276 — Less Of It.** Day 276, a Thursday, the twenty-first day of the Short Month. A market out past the last named house, and a store at the north end of it that a man of about forty-nine has kept for eleven years and that pays about nine people out of a pocket out of a wooden bowl. **He counts the bowl at about the second hour of the afternoon: it is four parts in five of the last paying day, it is the same nine, and he says the cost out loud in a market because about four of the nine who are getting a fifth less are standing in that market.** A woman of about thirty at the stall sews through it and a woman of about thirty-three at a bread board works a wage out of her own four loaves. Nobody shut the road and neither of the two men who refused it is the reason. **Change: a wage is a real thing that is four parts in five of what it was, and about four people in that market worked out in a morning that it was not the road and about nine of them worked it out wrong.**

**Chapter 0277 — Nobody Turned Up.** Day 277, a Friday, the twenty-second day of the Short Month.
